## I / I

===Original===
A swarthy turbaned face shone at Miriam from a tapestry screen standing between her and the ferns rising from a basket framework in the bow of the window. Consulting it at intervals as the afternoon wore on, she found that it made very light of the quiet propositions that were being elaborated within hearing of her inattentive ears. Looking beyond it she could catch glimpses between the crowded fernery, when a tram was not jingling by, of a close-set palisade just across the roadway and beyond the palisade of a green level ending at a row of Spanish poplars. The trams seemed very near and noisy. When they passed by the window, the speakers had to raise their voices. Otherwise the little drawing-room was very quiet, with a strange old-fashioned quietness. It was full of old things, like the Gobelin screen, and old thoughts like the thoughts of the ladies who were sitting and talking there. She and her mother had seemed quite modern, fussy, worldly people when they had first come into the room. From the moment the three ladies had come in and begun talking to her mother, the things in the room, and the view of the distant row of poplars had grown more and more peaceful, and now at the end of an hour she felt that she, and to some extent Mrs. Henderson too, belonged to the old-world room with its quiet green outlook shut in by the poplars. Only the trams were disturbing. They came busily by, with their strange jingle-jingle, plock-plock, and made her inattentive. Why were there so many people coming by in trams? Where were they going? Why were all the trams painted that hard, dingy blue?

The sisters talked quietly, outlining their needs in smooth gentle voices, in small broken phrases, frequently interrupting and correcting each other. Miriam heard dreamily that they wanted help with the lower school, the children from six to eight years of age, in the mornings and afternoons, and in the evenings a general superintendence of the four boarders. They kept on saying that the work was very easy and simple; there were no naughty girls—hardly a single naughty girl—in the school; there should be no difficult superintendence, no exercise of authority would be required.

By the time they had reached the statement of these modifications Miriam felt that she knew them quite well. The shortest, who did most of the talking and who had twinkling eyes and crooked pince-nez and soft reddish cheeks and a little red-tipped nose, and whose small coil of sheeny grey hair was pinned askew on the top of her head—stray loops standing out at curious angles—was Miss Jenny, the middle one. The very tall one sitting opposite her, with a delicate wrinkled creamy face and coal-black eyes and a peak of ringletted smooth coal-black hair, was the eldest, Miss Deborah. The other sister, much younger, with neat smooth green-grey hair and a long sad greyish face and faded eyes, was Miss Haddie. They were all three dressed in thin fine black material and had tiny hands and little softly moving feet. What did they think of the trams?

===Chinese===
一幅缠头巾的黝黑面孔，从一座挂毯屏风上朝米丽亚姆（Miriam）莹莹发亮——屏风立在她与窗弓里那丛蕨草之间，蕨草从一只篮架中蓬蓬而起。下午一点点过去，她不时抬眼请教那张脸，发觉它把那些正被从容拟定的提议看得极轻极轻——那些提议就在她心不在焉的耳朵听得见的地方，被细细斟酌着。朝屏风那边望去，趁没有电车叮叮当当驶过的当儿，她能从茂密的蕨叶间瞥见马路对面一道密密的栅栏，栅栏外是一平绿色的场地，尽头立着一排西班牙白杨。电车显得又近又吵。电车驶过窗前时，说话的人便得提高嗓门。除此之外，这间小客厅非常安静，一种古怪的旧派安静。它满是一样样的旧物，比如这座戈布兰挂毯屏风（Gobelin），也满是旧的思想，像坐在那里说话的几位小姐的思想一样旧。她和母亲初进这间屋子时，显得多么摩登、多么忙乱俗气。可从三姐妹进屋同她母亲攀谈的那一刻起，屋里的种种物件，还有远处那排白杨的景致，就变得越来越安宁；此刻，一个钟头过去，她觉得——连亨德森太太（Mrs. Henderson）也在某种程度上——自己已经属于这间旧世界的屋子，属于那被白杨围拢起来的一窗安静的绿。只有电车扰人。它们一趟趟忙碌地驶过，叮叮当当、橐橐（jingle-jingle, plock-plock）地响着怪腔怪调，搅得她心不在焉。怎么会有这么多人坐着电车来来去去？他们都要去哪儿？为什么所有的电车都漆成那种死死板板的、暗蒙蒙的蓝色？

三姐妹轻声细语地谈着，用平婉温软的嗓音、细碎断续的片语，把她们的需求一点点描画出来，不时互相打断、互相补正。米丽亚姆恍恍惚惚地听明白，她们要人帮忙带低年级——六岁到八岁的孩子——上午和下午都要；晚上则总照管着那四名寄宿生。她们反复说这活计轻松简单；学校里没有淘气的姑娘——几乎一个淘气的也没有——；不会有什么难管的照管，用不着行使什么权威。

等她们把这层层修订说完，米丽亚姆觉得自己已经同她们相熟得很了。话最多的是三姐妹里最矮的那个，眼睛忽闪忽闪，夹鼻眼镜（pince-nez）戴得歪歪的，脸颊软软的泛红，鼻尖一点红，一小盘亮灰色的头发别在头顶上，别得偏歪——几绺发圈支棱出古怪的角度——这是居中的珍妮小姐（Miss Jenny）。坐在她对面的那位非常高，一张细腻起皱的乳白脸，乌黑的眼睛，一峰卷得溜光的乌黑发尖，是最年长的黛博拉小姐（Miss Deborah）。另一位妹妹年轻得多，灰绿的头发平整光洁，一张长而忧郁的灰脸，一双褪了色的眼睛，是哈迪小姐（Miss Haddie）。三姐妹都穿着细软的黑色料子，都有一双小小的手和轻轻挪动的小小的脚。她们对电车是怎么想的呢？

===Original===
“Do you think you could manage it, chickie?” said Mrs. Henderson suddenly.

“I think I could.”

“No doubt, my dear, oh, no doubt,” said Miss Jenny with a little sound of laughter as she tapped her knee with the pince-nez she had plucked from their rakish perch on the reddened bridge of her nose.

“I don’t think I could teach Scripture.”

An outbreak of incoherent little sounds and statements from all three taught her that Miss Deborah took the Bible classes of the whole school.

“How old is Miriam?”

“Just eighteen. She has put up her hair today.”

“Oh, poor child, she need not have done that.”

“She is a *born* teacher. She used to hold little classes amongst her schoolfellows when she was only eight years old.”

Miriam turned sharply to her mother. She was sitting with her tired look—bright eyes, and moist flushed face. How had she heard about the little classes? Had there been little classes? She could not remember them.

“She speaks French like a Parisienne.”

That was that silly remark made by the woman in the train coming home from Hanover.

“Eh—we thought—it was in Germany she was—”

“Yes, but I learned more French.”

The sisters smiled provisionally.

“She shared a room with the mademoiselle.”

“Oh—er—hee—hay—perhaps she might speak French with the gels.”

“Oh no, I couldn’t *speak*.”

There was a tender little laugh.

“I don’t know French conversation.”

“Well, well.”

The sisters brought the discussion to an end by offering twenty pounds a year in return for Miriam’s services, and naming the date of the beginning of the autumn term.

===Chinese===
“你觉得你应付得来吗，小宝贝（chickie）？”亨德森太太突然说。

“我觉得应付得来。”

“毫无疑问，亲爱的，哦，毫无疑问。”珍妮小姐说着发出一点笑声，一面用夹鼻眼镜轻轻敲着膝盖——那眼镜刚从她红红的鼻梁上那副歪斜的架势上摘下来。

“我想我教不了《圣经》课。”

三人登时迸出一片不成句的小声响和小话语，让她明白过来：全校的《圣经》课都是黛博拉小姐担任的。

“米丽亚姆多大了？”

“刚满十八。她今天盘起头发了。”

“哦，可怜的孩子，她本不必这么早盘的。”

“她是个*天生*的教师。才八岁的时候，她就在同学中间开小课堂了。”

米丽亚姆猛地转向母亲。母亲带着一脸倦容坐在那里——眼睛亮亮的，脸潮红湿润。她怎么知道那些小课堂的？真有过小课堂吗？她记不起来了。

“她法语说得像巴黎女子。”

这是从汉诺威（Hanover）回来的火车上那位太太说过的傻话。

“呃——我们还以为——她是在德国——”

“是的，可我法语学得更多。”

三姐妹暂且地笑了笑。

“她同那位法国小姐（mademoiselle）合住过一个房间。”

“哦——呃——嘿——嗨——说不定她可以同姑娘们（gels，姐妹们把 girls 念作 gels 的旧派读音）讲讲法语。”

“哦不，我可不会*讲*。”

响起一阵温软的小笑。

“我不会法语会话。”

“好啦，好啦。”

三姐妹把商谈引向结束：提出以每年二十镑（twenty pounds a year）作为米丽亚姆任职的酬劳，并定下了秋季学期开学的日子。

---

===Original===
On the way to the front door they all looked into the principal schoolroom. Miriam saw a long wide dining-room table covered with brown American cloth. Shelves neatly crowded with books lined one wall from floor to ceiling. Opposite them at the far end of the room was a heavy grey marble mantelpiece, on which stood a heavy green marble clock frame. At its centre a gold-faced clock ticked softly. Opposite the windows were two shallow alcoves. In one stood a shrouded blackboard on an easel. The other held a piano with a high slender back. The prancing outward sweep of its lid gave Miriam the impression of an afternoon dress.

Miss Deborah drew up one of the Venetian blinds. They all crowded to the window and looked out on a small garden backed by trees and lying in deep shadow. Beyond were more gardens and the brownish backs of small old brick houses. Low walls separated the school garden from the gardens on either side.

“On our right we have a school for the deaf and dumb,” said Miss Perne; “on the other side is a family of Polish Jews.”

===Chinese===
往前门去的路上，大家顺便看了看主教室。米丽亚姆看见一张长而宽的餐桌，铺着棕色美国油布。一面墙从地板到天花板排满书架，密密地挤满了书。对面，房间那头，是一座笨重的灰色大理石壁炉架，架上立着一个笨重的绿色大理石钟座，座心一面金面钟轻轻地滴答。窗子的对面是两个浅浅的凹室：一间立着画架上蒙了罩布的黑板；另一间放着一架背部高挑细瘦的钢琴，琴盖向外腾跃般地掠开，给米丽亚姆一种下午裙的印象。

黛博拉小姐拉起一扇威尼斯式软百叶帘（Venetian blinds）。大家都拥到窗前，望向一座小花园——背后衬着树，沉在深深的荫翳里。更远处是更多的花园，和一座座旧砖小屋褐色的背面。矮墙把学校花园同两侧的花园隔开。

“我们右边是一所聋哑学校，”珀恩小姐（Miss Perne）说；“另一边是一家波兰犹太人。”

===Original===
“Mother, why *did* you pile it on?”

They would soon be down at the corner of Banbury Park where the tram lines ended and the Favorite omnibuses were standing in the muddy road under the shadow of the railway bridge. Through the jingling of the trams, the dop-dop of the hoofs of the tram-horses and the noise of a screaming train thundering over the bridge, Miriam made her voice heard, gazing through the spotted veil at her mother’s quivering features.

“They might have made me do all sorts of things I can’t do.”

Mrs. Henderson’s voice, breathless with walking, made a little sound of protest, a narrowed sound that told Miriam her amusement was half annoyance. The dark, noisy bridge, the clatter and rattle and the mud through which she must plunge to an omnibus exasperated her to the limit of her endurance.

“I’d got the post,” she said angrily; “you could see it was all settled and then you went saying those things.”

Glancing at the thin shrouded features she saw the faint lift of her mother’s eyebrows and the firmly speechless mouth.

“Piccadilly—jump on, chickie.”

“Let’s go outside now it’s fine,” said Miriam crossly.

Reaching the top of the omnibus she hurried to the front seat on the left hand side.

“That’s a very windy spot.”

“No it isn’t, it’s quite hot. The sun’s come out now. It’s rained for weeks. It won’t rain any more. It’ll be hot. You won’t feel the wind. Will you have the corner, mother?”

“No, chick, you sit there.”

Miriam screwed herself into the corner seat, crossing her knees and grazing the tips of her shoes.

“This is the only place on the top of a bus.”

Mrs. Henderson sat down at her side.

“I always make Harriett come up here when we go up to the West End.”

“Of course it’s the only place,” she insisted in response to her mother’s amused laugh. “No one smoking or talking in front; you can see out in front and you can see the shops if there are any, and you’re not falling off all the time. The bus goes on the left side of the road and tilts to the left.”

The seats were filling up and the driver appeared clambering into his place.

“Didn’t you ever think of that? Didn’t you ever think of the bus tilting that way?” persisted Miriam to her mother’s inattentive face. “Fancy never thinking of it. It’s beastly on the other side.”

The omnibus jerked forward.

“You ought to be a man, Mimmy.”

“I liked that little short one,” said Miriam contentedly as they came from under the roar of the bridge. “They were awfully nice, weren’t they? They seemed to have made up their mind to take me before we went.﻿ … So I think they like us. I wonder why they like us. Didn’t you think they liked us? Don’t you think they are awfully nice?”

“I do. They are very charming ladies.”

“Yes, but wasn’t it awfully rum their liking us in that funny way?”

“I’m sure I don’t see why they should not.”

“Oh, mother, you know what I mean. I like them. I’m perfectly sure I shall like them. D’you remember the little one saying all girls ought to marry? Why did she say that?”

“They are dear funny little O.M.’s,” said Mrs. Henderson merrily. She was sitting with her knees crossed, the stuff of her brown canvas dress was dragged across them into an ugly fold by the weight of the velvet panel at the side of the skirt. She looked very small and resourceless. And there were the Pernes with their house and their school. They were old maids. Of course. What then?

“I never dreamed of getting such a big salary.”

“Oh, my chickie, I’m afraid it isn’t much.”

“It is, mother, it’s lovely.”

“Oh—eh—well.”

Miriam turned fiercely to the roadway on her left.

===Chinese===
“妈妈，你*干嘛*要添油加醋？”

她们很快就要走到班伯里公园（Banbury Park）的街角了，电车轨道在那里到了头，“宠儿”线公共马车（Favorite）一辆辆停在铁路桥阴影下泥泞的路上。米丽亚姆透过电车的叮当声、电车马蹄的哒哒声、还有一列火车吼叫着轰隆隆碾过桥去的喧闹，让自己的声音被听见，一面隔着点点面纱望着母亲颤动的眉眼。

“她们差点就要让我干种种我干不了的活儿了。”

亨德森太太的嗓音走得气喘吁吁，发出一点抗议之声，一种收紧了的声音，告诉米丽亚姆她那份好笑里有一半是恼怒。黑沉沉、闹哄哄的桥，哐哐啷啷的响动，还有她必须一路蹚过去奔向马车的泥泞，把她激怒到了忍耐的极限。

“我本来已经把差事拿到手了，”她气冲冲地说；“你明明看得出全都定妥了，你偏还要说那些话。”

瞥一眼薄纱罩着的瘦脸，她看见母亲的眉毛淡淡一挑，嘴唇抿得紧紧的不出一声。

“去皮卡迪利（Piccadilly）——跳上来吧，小宝贝。”

“天放晴了，我们坐外头吧。”米丽亚姆没好气地说。

登上马车顶，她急急奔向左手边最前排的座位。

“那位置风可大呢。”

“不大，热和着呢。太阳这会儿出来了。下了好几个星期的雨。再也不会下了。要热起来了。你不会觉得有风的。妈妈，你要不要坐角落？”

“不，宝贝，你坐那儿。”

米丽亚姆把自己拧进角落座位里，跷起膝盖，鞋尖轻轻蹭着。

“这才是马车顶上唯一的好位置。”

亨德森太太在她身边坐下。

“我们上西区（the West End）去，我总是拉着哈丽特（Harriett）坐到这儿来。”

“当然是唯一的好位置，”她坚持道，回应母亲被逗乐的笑。“前头没人抽烟没人聊天；朝前看得见外面，有铺子还看得见铺子，也不会一路往下出溜。马车靠路的左边走，朝左边倾。”

座位渐渐坐满，车夫出现了，攀上他的位子。

“你从来没想过这个吗？从来没想过马车朝那边倾吗？”米丽亚姆对着母亲心不在焉的脸追问。“想想看，居然从来没想过。另一边可糟透了。”

马车猛地一颠，向前去了。

“你真该当个男孩子，米米（Mimmy）。”

“我喜欢那个矮矮的小个子，”马车驶出桥的轰响时，米丽亚姆心满意足地说。“她们和气极了，是不是？好像我们还没去，她们就打定主意要我了。……所以我想她们喜欢我们。不知为什么她们喜欢我们。你不觉得她们喜欢我们吗？你不觉得她们好极了？”

“我觉得。她们是非常迷人的小姐。”

“是呀，可她们用那种怪法子喜欢我们，不是怪极了吗？”

“我实在看不出为什么不该。”

“哦，妈妈，你懂我的意思。我喜欢她们。我完全肯定我会一直喜欢她们。你还记得那个小个子说所有的姑娘都该结婚吗？她为什么说那个？”

“她们是可爱的老小姐（O.M.’s＝old maids），”亨德森太太乐呵呵地说。她跷着膝盖坐在那里，棕色帆布裙的料子被裙侧丝绒镶片的分量往横里扯出一道难看的褶子。她看上去那么瘦小、那么无依无靠。而珀恩姐妹们呢，有宅子有学校。她们是老小姐。当然啦。那又如何？

“我做梦也没想过能拿这么大的薪水。”

“哦，我的小宝贝，恐怕这不算多。”

“算的，妈妈，可爱极了。”

“哦——呃——嗯。”

米丽亚姆猛地转向她左边的马路。

---

===Original===
She had missed the first swing forward of the vehicle and the first movements of the compact street.

They were going ahead now at a steady even trot. Her face was bathed in the flow of the breeze.

Little rivulets played about her temples, feeling their way through her hair. She drew off her gloves without turning from the flowing roadway. As they went on and on down the long road Miriam forgot her companion in the tranquil sense of being carried securely forward through the air away from people and problems. Ahead of her, at the end of the long drive, lay three sunlit weeks, bright now in the certainty of the shadow that lay beyond them … “the junior school” … “four boarders.”

===Chinese===
她错过了车辆起步的第一下前冲，也错过了这条紧凑街道最初的动静。

此刻马车以平稳匀称的小跑前行。她的脸浸在流动的微风里。

一股股小小的溪流在她的鬓边嬉戏，在头发里摸索着路径。她摘下手套，目光不离流淌而过的马路。马车沿着长街一程一程走下去，米丽亚姆在一种安然被载着穿越空气、远离人群与难题的宁静感里，忘记了身边的同伴。前方，在这条长长车程的尽头，躺着三个星期的阳光，此刻正亮着，因为确知那之后横着的阴影……“低年级”……“四名寄宿生。”

===Original===
They lumbered at last round a corner and out into a wide thoroughfare, drawing up outside a newly-built public house. Above it rose row upon row of upper windows sunk in masses of ornamental terra-cotta-coloured plaster. Branch roads, laid with tram lines led off in every direction. Miriam’s eyes followed a dull blue tram with a grubby white-painted seatless roof jingling busily off up a roadway where short trees stood all the way along in the small dim gardens of little grey houses. On the near corner of the road stood a wide white building, bulging into heavy domes against the sky. Across its side, large gilt letters standing far apart spelled out “Banbury Empire.”

“It must be a theatre,” she told herself in astonishment. “That’s what they call a suburban theatre. People think it is really a theatre.”

The little shock sent her mind feeling out along the road they had just left. She considered its unbroken length, its shops, its treelessness. The wide thoroughfare, up which they now began to rumble, repeated it on a larger scale. The pavements were wide causeways reached from the roadway by stone steps, three deep. The people passing along them were unlike any she knew. There were no ladies, no gentlemen, no girls or young men such as she knew. They were all alike. They were … She could find no word for the strange impression they made. It coloured the whole of the district through which they had come. It was part of the new world to which she was pledged to go on September 18th. It was her world already; and she had no words for it. She would not be able to convey it to others. She felt sure her mother had not noticed it. She must deal with it alone. To try to speak about it, even with Eve, would sap her courage. It was her secret. A strange secret for all her life as Hanover had been. But Hanover was beautiful, with distant country through the *Saal* windows with its colours misty in the sunlight, the beautiful, happy town and the woodland villages so near. This new secret was shabby, ugly and shabby. The half-perceived something persisted unchanged when the causeways and shops disappeared and long rows of houses streamed by, their close ranks broken only by an occasional cross road. They were large, high, flat-fronted houses with flights of grey stone steps leading to their porchless doors. They had tiny railed-in front gardens crowded with shrubs. Here and there long narrow strips of garden pushed a row of houses back from the roadway. In these longer plots stood signboards and showcases. “Photographic Studio,” “Commercial College,” “Eye Treatment,” “Academy of Dancing.” … She read the announcements with growing disquietude.

Rows of shops reappeared and densely crowded pavements, and then more high straight houses.

===Chinese===
马车终于隆隆转过街角，驶入一条宽阔的大道，在一座新建的酒馆外停了停。酒馆上方，一层层高窗从大片装饰性的赤陶色灰泥里凹嵌而出。铺着电车轨道的岔路朝四面八方伸展出去。米丽亚姆的目光追着一辆暗蓝色的电车——顶棚漆成白色、没有座位、邋里邋遢——叮叮当当地忙碌驶上一条马路，路两边灰色小屋前昏暗的小花园里，一路都立着矮树。路的近角上立着一座宽大的白色建筑，鼓出一团团沉重的圆顶，顶着天。建筑侧面，大个大大的鎏金字母彼此隔得老远，拼出“班伯里帝国（Banbury Empire）”。

“这准是家剧院，”她惊愕地对自己说。“这就是他们所说的郊区剧院。人们还当它真是家剧院呢。”

这小小的震动让她的心思顺着方才那条路摸索回去。她掂量着它一望不断的长度、它的铺子、它的没有树。此刻他们开始隆隆驶上的这条宽阔大道，把它放大了重演一遍。人行道是宽宽的高台，从马路上要登三级石阶才上得去。上面来往的人和她认识的一切人都不同。没有淑女，没有绅士，没有她认识的那类姑娘和年轻人。他们全都一个样。他们是……她找不到一个词来形容他们给她的怪异印象。这印象给她们一路穿过的整个地区上了色。它是那个新世界的一部分——她已应许9月18日去投奔的世界。它已经是她的世界了；而她对它无言可说。她没法把它传达给别人。她肯定母亲根本没注意到它。她必须独自应付它。想谈论它，哪怕同伊芙（Eve）谈，都会泄了她的勇气。这是她的秘密。一个将伴随她一生的怪异秘密，像汉诺威一样。可汉诺威是美的——透过大厅（Saal）的窗子望得见远处的乡野，色彩在阳光里朦朦胧胧，那美丽快活的城，近在咫尺的林中村落。这个新秘密却寒碜，又丑又寒碜。那半觉半察的什么，在石台和铺子消失、长排长排的房屋连绵掠过时，依旧不变地持续着——密密的行列只偶尔被一条横路打断。那都是高大、平脸的房子，一道道灰色石阶通向没有门廊的门。房前有小小的围栏花园，挤满了灌木。间或有一条条狭长的花园地把一排房子从马路边推开去。这些较长的园地里立着招牌和陈列橱窗。“照相馆”、“商学院”、“眼科诊疗”、“舞蹈学校”。……她读着这些告示，心里越来越不安。

一排排铺子重新出现，人行道熙熙攘攘，然后又是更多高而笔直的房子。

---

===Original===
She roused herself at last from her puzzled contemplation and turned to glance at her mother. Mrs. Henderson was looking out ahead. The exhausted face was ready, Miriam saw, with its faintly questioning eyebrows and tightly-held lips, for emotional response. She turned away uneasily to the spellbound streets.

“Useless to try to talk about anything.﻿ … Mother would be somehow violent. She would be overpowering. The strange new impressions would be dissolved.”

But she must do something, show some sign of companionship. She began humming softly. The air was so full of clamour that she could not hear her voice. The houses and shops had disappeared. Drab brick walls were passing slowly by on either side. A goods’ yard. She deepened her humming, accentuating her phrases so that the sound might reach her companion through the reverberations of the clangour of shunting trains.

===Chinese===
她终于从困惑的凝想里醒过来，转身瞥了母亲一眼。亨德森太太正朝前方望出去。米丽亚姆看见，那张疲惫的脸已经备好了——眉毛微微探问，嘴唇紧紧抿着——只等感情的回应。她不安地转开去，回到那些着了魔的街上。

“谈什么都白费。……妈妈会不知怎么就激烈起来。她会排山倒海。那些陌生的新印象就会被化掉。”

可她总得做点什么，表示一点相伴的意思。她开始轻轻哼唱。空气里满是喧嚣，她听不见自己的声音。房屋和铺子消失了。土褐色的砖墙在两侧缓缓掠过。一处货场。她加深了哼唱，把乐句的抑扬加重，好让声音穿过调车火车铿锵轰鸣的回响，传到同伴耳边。

---

===Original===
The high brick walls were drawing away. The end of the long roadway was in sight. Its widening mouth offered no sign of escape from the disquieting strangeness. The open stretch of thoroughfare into which they emerged was fed by innumerable lanes of traffic. From the islands dotted over its surface towered huge lamp standards branching out thin arms. As they rattled noisily over the stone setts they jolted across several lines of tramway and wove their way through currents of traffic crossing each other in all directions.

“I wonder where we’re going—I wonder if this is a Piccadilly bus,” Miriam thought of saying. Impossible to shout through the din.

===Chinese===
高高的砖墙渐渐退去。长路的尽头已在望。它张开的大口并没有提供逃离这不安的怪异的出口。他们驶入的那段开阔大道，由数不清的车道车流汇灌而成。点缀在路面上的安全岛上，耸立着巨大的灯柱，伸出细细的臂。马车在石块路面上哐啷哐啷地驶过，颠簸着横穿几条电车轨道，在四面八方交错的车流里穿行。

“不知我们要去哪儿——不知这是不是去皮卡迪利的马车，”米丽亚姆想着要不要说。隔着这片喧声喊话是不可能的。

---

===Original===
The driver gathered up his horses and they clattered deafeningly over the last open stretch and turned into a smooth wide prospect.

“Oh bliss, wood-paving,” murmured Miriam.

A mass of smoke-greyed, sharply steepled stone building appeared on the right. Her eyes rested on its soft shadows.

On the left a tall grey church was coming towards them, spindling up into the sky. It sailed by, showing Miriam a circle of little stone pillars built into its spire. Plumy trees streamed by, standing large and separate on moss-green grass railed from the roadway. Bright white-faced houses with pillared porches shone through from behind them and blazed white above them against the blue sky. Wide side streets opened showing high balconied houses. The side streets were feathered with trees and ended mistily.

Away ahead were edges of clean bright masonry in profile, soft tufted heads of trees, bright green in the clear light. At the end of the vista the air was like pure saffron-tinted mother-of-pearl.

Miriam sat back and drew a deep breath.

===Chinese===
车夫把马收拢起来，马车震耳欲聋地嗒嗒驶过最后一段开阔路，拐进一条平阔的远景。

“哦，至福，木块铺的路，”米丽亚姆低声说。

右边出现一大片被烟熏灰的、尖塔峻峭的石砌建筑。她的目光停驻在它柔和的阴影上。

左边，一座高高的灰色教堂正朝他们迎来，细细地挑上天空。它驶过去了，让米丽亚姆看见塔尖里砌着的一圈小石柱。羽冠般的树木一棵棵连绵掠过，高大而各自独立，立在用栏杆围离马路的苔绿色草地上。柱廊门廊、白脸鲜亮的房子从树后透出光来，又在树梢之上衬着蓝天烧得雪白。宽阔的横街一条条张开，露出高高的带阳台的房子。横街上覆着羽毛般的树影，朦朦胧胧地到了头。

远远前方，是一侧侧洁净明亮的石砌建筑侧影，是一团团柔柔簇拥的树冠，在澄澈的光里鲜绿。景致的尽头，空气像纯纯的染了藏红花色的珍珠母。

米丽亚姆靠回椅背，深深吸了一口气。

---

===Original===
“Well, chickie?”

“What’s the matter?”

“Why, you’ve been very funny!”

“How?”

“You’ve been so dummel.”

“No, I haven’t.”

“Oh—eh.”

“How d’you mean I’ve been funny?”

“Not speaking to poor old mum-jam.”

“Well, you haven’t spoken to me.”

“No.”

“I shan’t take any of my summer things there,” said Miriam.

Mrs. Henderson’s face twitched.

“Shall I?”

“I’m afraid you haven’t very much in the way of thick clothing.”

“I’ve only got my plaid dress for every day and my mixy grey for best and my dark blue summer skirt. My velveteen skirt and my nainsook blouse are too old.”

“You can wear the dark blue muslin blouse with the blue skirt for a long time yet with something warm underneath.”

“My grey’s very grubby.”

“You look very well in it indeed.”

“I don’t mean that. I mean it’s all gone sort of dull and grubby over the surface when you look down it.”

“Oh, that’s your imagination.”

“It isn’t my imagination and I can see how Harriett’s looks.”

“You both look very nice.”

“That’s not the point.”

“Don’t make a mountain out of a molehill, my chick.”

“I’m not making anything. The simple fact is that the grey dresses are piggy.”

Mrs. Henderson flushed deeply, twining and untwining her silk-gloved fingers.

“She thinks that’s ‘gross exaggeration.’ That’s what she wants to say,” pondered Miriam wearily.

They turned into Langham Place.

She glanced to see whether her mother realised where they were.

“Look, we’re in the West End, mother! Oh, I’m not going to think about Banbury Park till it begins!”

===Chinese===
“怎么样，小宝贝？”

“怎么了？”

“嘿，你一路怪得很哪！”

“怎么个怪法？”

“你闷声不响的。”

“我没有。”

“哦——呃。”

“你说我怪，是什么意思？”

“不同可怜的老妈妈（mum-jam）说话呀。”

“哼，你也没同我说话。”

“是没说。”

“我一件夏天的衣裳也不带去，”米丽亚姆说。

亨德森太太的脸抽动了一下。

“要带吗？”

“恐怕你厚实的衣裳也没有几件。”

“我只有平时穿的格呢裙、做客穿的混灰呢（mixy grey），还有深蓝的夏裙。棉绒裙和细棉纱衬衫（nainsook）都太旧了。”

“那件深蓝麦斯林纱衬衫配蓝裙子还能穿好一阵子呢，里面穿暖和点就是。”

“我那条灰的脏得很。”

“你穿它其实好看得很。”

“我不是说那个。我是说顺着衣身望下去，整个表面都发乌发脏了。”

“哦，那是你的想象。”

“才不是想象，哈丽特那条什么样我看得见。”

“你们俩穿得都很好看。”

“问题不在这儿。”

“别小题大做了，我的宝贝。”

“我不是小题大做。简单的事实是，灰裙子脏得猪猡似的。”

亨德森太太涨红了脸，丝手套里的手指绞了又绞。

“她准想着这又是‘言过其实’。她就想说这个，”米丽亚姆倦倦地想着。

马车拐进兰厄姆广场（Langham Place）。

她瞟了一眼，看母亲是否意识到了她们身在何处。

“看，我们到西区了，妈妈！哦，开学之前我才不去想班伯里公园呢！”

---

===Original===
They drew up near the Maison Nouvelle.

“*Stanlake* is,” said a refined emphatic voice from the pavement.

Miriam did not look for the speaker. The quality of the voice brought her a moment’s realisation of the meaning of her afternoon’s adventure. She was going to be shut up away from the grown-up things, the sunlit world, and the people who were enjoying it. She would be shut up and surrounded in Wordsworth House, a proper schooly school, amongst all those strange roadways. It would be cold English pianos and dreadful English children—and trams going up and down that grey road outside.

As they went on down Regent Street she fastened, for refuge from her thoughts, upon a window where softly falling dresses of dull olive stood about against a draped background of pale dead yellow. She held it in her mind as shop after shop streamed by.

“These shops are extremely récherché.”

“It’s old Regent Street, mother,” said Miriam argumentatively. “Glorious old Regent Street. Ruby wine.”

“Ah, Regent Street.”

“We always walk up one side and down the other. Up the dolls’ hospital side and down Liberty’s. Glory, glory, ruby wine.”

“You *are* enthusiastic.”

“But it’s so glorious. Don’t you think so?”

“Sit back a little, chickie. One can’t see the windows. You’re such a solid young woman.”

“You’ll see our A.B.C. soon. You know. The one we go to after the Saturday pops. You’ve been to it. You came to it the day we came to Madame Schumann’s farewell. It’s just round here in Piccadilly. Here it is. Glorious. I must make the others come up once more before I die. I always have a scone. I don’t like the aryated bread. We go along the Burlington Arcade too. I don’t believe you’ve ever been along there. It’s simply perfect. Glove shops and fans and a smell of the most exquisite scent everywhere.”

“Dear me. It must be very captivating.”

“Now we shall pass the parks. Oh, isn’t the sun A1 copper bottom!”

Mrs. Henderson laughed wistfully.

“What delicious shade under those fine old trees. I almost wish I had brought my *en-tout-cas*.”

“Oh no, you don’t really want it. There will be more breeze presently. The bus always begins to go quicker along here. It’s the Green Park, that one. Those are clubs that side, the West End clubs. It’s fascinating all the way along here to Hyde Park Corner. You just see Park Lane going up at the side. Park Lane. It goes wiggling away, straight into heaven. We’ve never been up there. I always read the name at the corner.”

“You ridiculous chick—ah, there is the Royal Academy of Arts.”

“Oh yes, I wonder if there are any Leightons this year.”

“Or Leader. Charles Leader. I think there is nothing more charming than those landscape scenes by Leader.”

“I’ve got three bally weeks. I can see Hyde Park. We’ve got ages yet. It goes on being fascinating right down through Kensington and right on up to the other side of Putney Bridge.”

“Dear me. Isn’t it fascinating after that?”

“Oh, not all that awful walk along the Upper Richmond Road—not until our avenue begins—”

===Chinese===
马车在新屋百货（Maison Nouvelle）附近停了停。

“就得数斯坦莱克（Stanlake）。”人行道上一个文雅而笃定的声音说。

米丽亚姆没有去寻那说话的人。那嗓音的质地让她一时明白了下午这番奇遇的意义。她就要被关起来了，远离一切成年人的事物，远离这阳光普照的世界和世上享乐的人们。她将被关在华兹华斯楼（Wordsworth House）里，被围在一所规规矩矩、学堂味十足的学堂里，围在那一带条条陌生的马路中间。将是冷冰冰的英国钢琴和可怕的英国孩子——还有那条灰色马路外面来来去去的电车。

马车沿摄政街（Regent Street）走下去，她为了躲避自己的念头，把心拴在了一扇橱窗上——几件暗橄榄色的连衣裙柔柔垂立，衬着一幅淡淡的死黄色帘幕背景。铺子一家家流过去，她把这扇窗在心里攥着不放。

“这些铺子精致考究（récherché）极了。”

“这是老摄政街呀，妈妈，”米丽亚姆争辩道。“辉煌的老摄政街。红宝石酒。”

“啊，摄政街。”

“我们总是一边走上去、另一边走下来。从玩偶医院（dolls’ hospital）那侧上去，从利伯提（Liberty’s）那侧下来。荣耀啊，荣耀，红宝石酒。”

“你可*真*是兴致勃勃。”

“可它就这么辉煌。你不觉得吗？”

“往后靠一点儿，小宝贝。人家都看不见橱窗了。你都这么墩墩实实一位大姑娘了。”

“很快你就要看见我们那家A.B.C.（充气面包公司茶室）了。你知道的。周六流行音乐会（Saturday pops）散场后我们去的那家。你去过的。我们来听舒曼夫人（Madame Schumann）告别音乐会那天还去过。就在皮卡迪利这拐角上。到了。多好。我死之前一定还要拉她们再来一趟。我每次都吃一块司康饼。我不喜欢那种充气面包（aryated bread）。我们还沿伯灵顿拱廊（Burlington Arcade）走。我看你根本没去过那儿。那儿简直完美。手套店、扇子店，到处飘着最精致的香气。”

“哎呀。那一定非常迷人。”

“现在我们要经过几座公园了。哦，这太阳岂不是一等一的铜底好货（A1 copper bottom）！”

亨德森太太怅然笑了。

“这些好看的老树下多荫凉呀。我几乎要后悔没带我的晴雨两用伞（en-tout-cas）了。”

“哦不，你其实用不着。一会儿风还要大些。马车一到这一段总是跑得快些。那是绿园（Green Park）。那一边都是俱乐部，西区的俱乐部。从这里一路到海德公园角（Hyde Park Corner）都妙极了。你刚好能看见帕克巷（Park Lane）在旁边升上去。帕克巷。它弯弯扭扭地一路蜿蜒，直通天上。我们从来没上去过。我总是在街角读那名字。”

“你这个滑稽的宝贝——啊，皇家艺术院（Royal Academy of Arts）到了。”

“哦对，不知今年有没有莱顿（Leighton）的画。”

“或者利德（Leader）。查尔斯·利德。我想没有什么比利德的那些风景画更迷人的了。”

“我还有整整三个好得冒泡的星期呢。我看得见海德公园（Hyde Park）。我们时间还多着呢。一路穿过肯辛顿（Kensington）一直到帕特尼桥（Putney Bridge）对岸，都一直这么妙下去。”

“哎呀。过了那儿之后不也妙得很吗？”

“哦，上里士满路（Upper Richmond Road）那段可怕的走路可不算——一直要到我们家的林荫道开始——”

---

===Original===
Miriam fumbled with the fastening of the low wide gate as her mother passed on up the drive. She waited until the footsteps were muffled by the fullness of the may trees linking their middle branches over the bend in the drive. Then she looked steadily down the sunflecked asphalted avenue along which they had just come. The level sunlight streamed along the empty roadway and the shadows of the lime trees lay across the path and up the oak palings. Her eyes travelled up and down the boles of the trees, stopping at each little stunted tuft of greenery. She could no longer hear her mother’s footsteps. There was a scented coolness in the shady watered garden. Leaning gently with her breast against the upper bars of the gate she broke away from the sense of her newly-made engagement.

She scanned the whole length of the shrouded avenue from end to end and at last looked freely up amongst the interwoven lime trees. Long she watched, her eyes roaming from the closely-growing leaves where the green was densest to the edges of the trees where the light shone through. “Gold and green,” she whispered, “green and gold, held up by firm brown stems bathed in gold.”

When she reached the open garden beyond the bend she ran once round the large centre bed where berberus and laurestinus bushes stood in a clump ringed by violas and blue lobelias and heavily scented masses of cherry-ripe. Taking the shallow steps in two silent strides she reached the shelter of the deep porch. The outer door and the door of the vestibule stood open. Gently closing the vestibule she ran across the paved hall and opened the door on the right.

Harriett, in a long fawn canvas dress with a deep silk sash, was standing in the middle of the drawing-room floor with a large pot of scented geraniums in her arms.

===Chinese===
母亲先走上车道时，米丽亚姆在低矮的大门上摸索着门闩。她等到脚步声被山楂树交连在车道拐弯上空的繁枝密叶闷住了，才静静地顺望这条洒满阳光的柏油林荫道——她们方才就是从这上面来的。平铺的阳光沿空荡荡的车路流淌，椴树的影子横过小径、爬上橡木栅栏。她的目光在树干上上下下地巡，在每一小丛矮墩墩的绿上停一停。母亲的脚步声已听不见了。背阴的、洒过水的花园里有一片芳香的清凉。她把胸脯轻轻抵在门的上栏上，从那桩刚刚定下的聘约之感里脱身出来。

她把这条荫翳的林荫道从头到尾扫了一遍，终于自由自在地仰望进交柯连理的椴树之间。她久久望着，目光从绿得最浓处挤挤挨挨的叶丛，漫游到阳光透亮的树缘。“金色和绿色，”她低语，“绿色和金色，由浸在金色里的坚牢棕色树干高高擎着。”

走到拐弯外那片敞亮的花园，她绕着中央的大花坛跑了一圈——坛里一丛小檗（berberus）和月桂荚蒾（laurestinus），围着一圈堇菜和蓝色半边莲，还有一大团一大团香气浓重的“熟樱桃”（cherry-ripe）。她两级一跨、悄然无声地登上浅台阶，到了深门廊的荫庇下。外门和前厅的门都敞着。她轻轻带上前厅的门，跑过铺砖的门厅，推开右手边的门。

哈丽特穿着一条浅黄褐色帆布长裙、系一条深色丝腰带，正站在客厅地板正中，怀里抱一大盆香叶天竺葵。

---

===Original===
“Hullo!” said Miriam.

Putting down her pot Harriett fixed brown eyes upon her and began jumping lightly up and down where she stood. The small tips of her fawn glacé kid shoes shone together between the hem of her dress and the pale green of the carpet.

“What you doing?” said Miriam quietly shutting the door behind her and flushing with pleasure.

Harriett hopped more energetically. The blaze from the western window caught the paste stone in the tortoiseshell comb crowning her little high twist of hair and the prisms of the lustres standing behind her on the white marble mantelpiece.

“What you doing, booby?”

“Old conservatory,” panted Harriett.

Miriam looked vaguely down the length of the long room to where the conservatory doors stood wide open. As she gazed at the wet tiling Harriett ceased hopping and kicked her delicately. “Well, gooby?”

Miriam grinned.

“You’ve got it. I knew you would. The Misses Perne have engaged Miss Miriam Henderson as resident teacher for the junior school.”

“Oh yes, I’ve got it,” smiled Miriam. “But don’t let’s talk about that. It’s just an old school, a house. I don’t know a bit what it’ll be like. I’ve got three bally blooming weeks. Don’t let’s talk about it.”

“Awri.”

“What about Saturday?”

“It’s all right. Ted was at the club.”

“Was he!”

“Yes, old scarlet face, he were.”

“I’m *not*.”

“He came in just before closing time and straight up to me and ast where you were. He looked sick when I told him, and so fagged.”

“It was awfully hot in town,” murmured Miriam tenderly.

She went to the piano and struck a note very softly.

“He played a single with the duffer and lost it.”

“Oh, well, of course, he was so tired.”

“Yes, but it wasn’t that. It was because you weren’t there. He’s simply no good when you’re not there, now. He’s perfectly different.”

===Chinese===
“嘿！”米丽亚姆说。

哈丽特放下花盆，一双棕眼睛定定地盯住她，开始在原地轻轻地蹦。浅黄褐色的光面小山羊皮鞋的小鞋尖，在她裙摆和浅绿地毯之间一齐闪闪发亮。

“你干吗呢？”米丽亚姆说着，随手轻轻带上门，高兴得脸都红了。

哈丽特蹦得更起劲了。西窗的烈焰照上她高高的小发髻上那柄龟甲梳里镶的人造宝石，也照上她身后白色大理石壁炉架上那些玻璃坠饰烛台（lustres）的棱片。

“你干吗呢，傻瓜？”

“弄那个老花房，”哈丽特喘着气说。

米丽亚姆恍恍惚惚地顺着长房间望过去，花房的门大敞着。她盯着湿漉漉的花砖地出神，哈丽特不蹦了，轻轻踢了她一脚。“怎么样，古比（gooby）？”

米丽亚姆咧嘴笑了。

“你猜着了。我就知道你会。珀恩姐妹们聘了米丽亚姆·亨德森小姐做低年级的住校教师。”

“哦对，我猜着了，”米丽亚姆笑了。“可咱们别谈那个了。不过是一所老学校，一座房子。它什么样我一点儿都不知道。我还有整整三个好得开花的好星期。别谈它了。”

“好嘞。”

“礼拜六怎么说？”

“没跑了。特德（Ted）去俱乐部了。”

“是吗！”

“是呀，老红脸，他去了。”

“我才*没有*红。”

“他关门前才进来，径直走到我跟前，问你去哪儿了。我一告诉他，他一脸的败兴，还累得要命。”

“城里热极了，”米丽亚姆怜爱地低声说。

她走到钢琴边，极轻地按了一个音。

“他同那个蹩脚货（the duffer）打了一场单打，还输了。”

“哦，那当然了，他太累了嘛。”

“是呀，可不光是累。是因为你不在。现在你不在，他就整个人都不行了。他完全变了一个人。”

===Original===
Miriam struck her note again.

“Listen, that’s E flat.”

“Go on.”

“That’s a chord in E flat. Isn’t it lovely? It sounds perfectly different in C. Listen. Isn’t it funny?”

“Well, don’t you want to know why it’s all right about Saturday?”

“Yes, screamingly.”

“Well, that’s the perfectly flabbergasting thing. Ted simply came to say they’ve got a man coming to stay with them and can he bring him.”

“My dear! What a heavenly relief. That makes twelve men and fourteen girls. That’ll do.”

“Nan Babington’s hurt her ankle, but she swears she’s coming.” Harriett sniffed and sank down on the white sheepskin, drawing her knees up to her chin.

“You shouldn’t say ‘swears.’”

“Well, you bet. She simply loves our dances.”

“Did she say she did?”

“She sat on the pavilion seat with Bevan Seymour all the afternoon and I was with them when Ted was playing with the duffer. She told Bevan that she didn’t know anywhere else where the kids arranged the dances, and everything was so jolly. It’s *screaming*, my dear, she said.”

“It’s horrid the way she calls him ‘my dear.’ Your ring is simply dazzling like that, Harry. D’you see? It’s the sun.”

“Of course it’ll mean she’ll sit out in a deck chair in the garden with Bevan all the time.”

“How disgusting.”

“It’s her turn for the pavilion tea on Saturday. She’s coming in her white muslin and then coming straight on here with two sticks and wants us to keep her some flowers. Let’s go and have tea. It’ll be nearly dinner time.”

“Has Mary made a cake?”

“I dunno. Tea was to be in the breakfast-room when you came back.”

“Why not in the conservatory?”

“Because, you silly old crow, I’m beranging it for Saturday.”

===Chinese===
米丽亚姆又按了一下那个音。

“听，这是降E。”

“接着说。”

“这是降E的和弦。多好听？换成C调就完全两样了。听。怪不怪？”

“喂，你到底想不想知道礼拜六的事为什么没跑了？”

“想，想得要命。”

“嘿，这才叫吓人一跳呢。特德专门跑来一趟，就为说他们家有位先生要来做客，问他能不能把人带来。”

“哎呀！真是天大的解脱。这就凑成十二位先生、十四位姑娘。够了。”

“楠·巴宾顿（Nan Babington）崴了脚脖子，可她发誓一定要来。”哈丽特吸了吸鼻子，一屁股坐到白羊皮毯上，把膝盖抱到下巴跟前。

“你不该说‘发誓’这种字眼。”

“哼，等着瞧吧。她就是爱死我们的舞会了。”

“她自己这么说过吗？”

“她一下午都同贝文·西摩（Bevan Seymour）坐在亭子座上，特德同蹩脚货打球的时候我就在他们旁边。她告诉贝文，说不知道还有哪家是孩子们自己张罗舞会的，样样都这么快活。‘简直笑死人，我亲爱的，’她说。”

“她管他叫‘我亲爱的’那样子真讨厌。你那戒指这么一晃真耀眼，哈里（Harry）。看见没？是太阳照的。”

“那当然，她就得整个晚上同贝文坐在花园的帆布躺椅里歇着了。”

“多恶心。”

“礼拜六轮到她在亭子里办茶点。她要穿她的白麦斯林纱裙来，然后就拄着两根手杖径直上这儿来，还要我们给她留些花儿。咱们喝茶去吧。快到晚饭时候了。”

“玛丽（Mary）做蛋糕了吗？”

“不知道。说好你回来就把茶摆在早餐室。”

“干吗不摆在花房？”

“因为呀，你这个傻老乌鸦，我正把它归置好预备礼拜六呢。”

===Original===
“Shall we have the piano in there?”

“Well, don’t you think so?”

“Twenty-six of us. Perhaps it’ll be more blissful.”

“If we have the breakfast-room piano in the hall it’ll bung up the hall.”

“Yes, but the Erard bass is so perfect for waltzes.”

“And the be-rilliant Collard treble is so all right in the vatoire.”

“I thought it was Eve and I talked about the Collard treble.”

“Well, I was there.”

“Anyhow we’ll have the grand in the conservatory. Oh, Bacchus! Ta-ra-ra-boom-deay.”

“*Tea*,” said a rounded voice near the keyhole.

“Eve!” shouted Miriam.

The door opened slightly. “I know,” said the voice.

“Come *in*, Eve,” commanded Miriam, trying to swing the door wide.

“I know,” said the voice quivering with the effort of holding the door. “I know all about the new Misses Perne and the new man—Max Sonnenheim—Max.”

“This way out,” called Harriett from the conservatory.

“Eve,” pleaded Miriam, tugging at the door, “let me get at you. Don’t be an idiot.”

A gurgle of amusement made her loosen her hold.

“I’m not trying, you beast. Take your iron wrists away.”

A small white hand waggled fingers through the aperture.

Miriam seized and covered it. “Come in for a minute,” she begged. “I want to see you. What have you got on?”

“*Tea.*”

The hand twisted itself free and Eve fled through the hall.

Miriam flung after her with a yell and caught at her slender body. “I’ve a great mind to drag down your old hair.”

“Tea,” smiled Eve serenely.

“All *right*, I’m coming, damn you, aren’t I?”

“Oh, Mimmy!”

“Well, damn *me*, then. Somebody in the house must swear. I say, Eve?”

“What?”

“Nothing, only I *say*.”

“Um.”

===Chinese===
“咱们把钢琴搬进去好不好？”

“嗯，你不觉得该吗？”

“咱们二十六个人呢。说不定更妙。”

“要是把早餐室的钢琴搁在门厅，门厅就堵死了。”

“是呀，可埃拉德（Erard）的低音跳起华尔兹来再完美不过。”

“还有那亮堂堂（be-rilliant）的科拉德（Collard）高音，搁在花房（vatoire）里正合适。”

“我记得同我谈科拉德高音的是伊芙（Eve）和我。”

“哼，我也在场来着。”

“不管怎么说，大钢琴放花房。哦，酒神巴科斯！塔-拉-拉-嘭-嘚-哎（Ta-ra-ra-boom-de-ay）。”

“喝*茶*了，”钥匙孔边一个圆润的声音说。

“伊芙！”米丽亚姆喊。

门开了一条缝。“我知道，”那声音说。

“进来呀，伊芙，”米丽亚姆命令道，使劲要把门推开。

“我知道，”那声音说，因抵住门而发颤。“珀恩姐妹的事我全知道了，还有那位新来的先生——马克斯·索南海姆（Max Sonnenheim）——马克斯。”

“往这边出去，”哈丽特从花房里喊。

“伊芙，”米丽亚姆拽着门央求道，“让我够着你。别犯傻了。”

一声咯咯的笑让她松了手。

“我才没用劲儿呢，你这个坏东西。把你那铁腕子拿开。”

一只小小的白手从门缝里晃着手指。

米丽亚姆一把抓住它，紧紧握住。“进来待一分钟嘛，”她央求。“我想看看你。你身上穿了什么？”

“喝*茶*。”

那手一拧挣脱了，伊芙穿过门厅逃走。

米丽亚姆叫喊着追过去，一把抓住她纤细的身子。“我真恨不得把你那头发扯下来。”

“喝茶，”伊芙安然地笑着。

“好——啦，我这就来，该死的，还不行吗？”

“哦，米米！”

“那就咒*我*吧。这家里总得有个人骂骂脏话。我说，伊芙？”

“什么？”

“没什么，我就是说说。”

“嗯。”

## II / II

===Original===
Miriam extended herself on the drawing-room sofa which had been drawn up at the end of the room under the open window.

The quintets of candles on the girandoles hanging on either side of the high overmantel gave out an unflickering radiance, and in the centre of the large room the chandelier, pulled low, held out in all directions bulbs of softly tinted light.

In an intensity of rose-shaded brilliance pouring from a tall standard lamp across the sheepskin hearthrug stood a guest with a fiddle under her arm fluttering pages on a music stand. The family sat grouped towards her in a circle.

On her low sofa, outside the more brilliant light, Miriam was a retreating loop in the circle of seated forms, all visible as she lay with her eyes on the ceiling. But no eyes could meet and pilfer her own. The darkness brimmed in from the window on her right. She could touch the rose-leaves on the sill and listen to the dewy stillness of the garden.

===Chinese===
米丽亚姆舒展开身子，躺在客厅的沙发上——沙发已被拖到房间那头敞开的窗下。

高炉壁架两侧悬挂的枝形壁灯架（girandoles）上，一簇簇五支一组的蜡烛放出纹丝不摇的光焰；大房间正中，枝形吊灯（chandelier）拉得低低的，朝四面八方探出一球球柔彩的光。

一盏高立灯泼下浓烈的玫瑰色光华，照过白羊皮炉边地毯——一位客人站在光里，臂下夹着小提琴，翻动着谱架上的乐谱。一家人围着她坐成一圈。

在低低的沙发上，在较亮的光圈之外，米丽亚姆是围坐的一圈人形里一个退出去的环扣；她躺着，眼睛望着天花板，一切都尽收眼底。可没有谁的目光能遇上她的目光、从里面偷走什么。黑暗从她右边的窗里漫进来。她够得着窗台上的玫瑰花瓣，听得见花园里带露的静。

===Original===
“What shall I play?” said the guest.

“What have you there?”

“Gluck﻿ … *Klassische Stücke*﻿ … Cavatina.”

“Ah, Gluck,” said Mr. Henderson, smoothing his long knees with outspread fingers.

“Have you got that Beethoven thing?” asked Sarah.

“Not here, Sally.”

“I saw it—on the piano—with chords,” said Sarah excitedly.

“Chords,” encouraged Miriam.

“Yes, I think so,” muttered Sarah taking up her crochet. “I daresay I’m wrong,” she giggled, throwing out a foot and hastily withdrawing it.

“I can find it, dear,” chanted the guest.

Miriam raised a flourishing hand. The crimsoned oval of Eve’s face appeared inverted above her own. She poked a finger into one of the dark eyes and looking at the screwed-up lid whispered voicelessly, “Make her play the Romance first and *then* the Cavatina without talking in between.﻿ …”

Eve’s large soft mouth pursed a little, and Miriam watched steadily until dimples appeared. “Go on, Eve,” she said, removing her hand.

“Shall I play the Beethoven first?” enquired the guest.

“Mm—and then the Cavatina,” murmured Miriam, as if half asleep, turning wholly towards the garden, as Eve went to collect the piano scores.

===Chinese===
“我弹点什么好呢？”客人说。

“你手里都有什么？”

“格鲁克……《古典小品》（Klassische Stücke）……卡伐蒂娜（Cavatina）。”

“啊，格鲁克。”亨德森先生说，张开手指抚着他长长的膝盖。

“你带那支贝多芬的曲子了吗？”萨拉问。

“没在这儿，萨莉。”

“我看见过——在钢琴上——带和弦的。”萨拉兴奋地说。

“和弦。”米丽亚姆鼓励道。

“对，我想是的。”萨拉咕哝着拿起钩针活。“八成是我记错了。”她咯咯一笑，伸出一只脚，又慌忙缩回去。

“我找得着它，亲爱的。”客人拖长调子说。

米丽亚姆举起一只挥动的手。伊芙的脸那红扑扑的椭圆倒着出现在她脸的上方。她把一根手指戳进其中一只黑眼睛里，看着闭紧的眼皮，无声地用口型说：“让她先弹罗曼斯（Romance），*然后*再弹卡伐蒂娜，中间别讲话。……”

伊芙那大大的软嘴唇微微一抿，米丽亚姆目不转睛地盯着，直到酒窝出来了。“去吧，伊芙。”她说着收回手。

“我先弹贝多芬好吗？”客人问。

“嗯——然后卡伐蒂娜。”米丽亚姆喃喃地说，像半睡着似的，整个人转向花园，伊芙去收琴谱了。

---

===Original===
She seemed to grow larger and stronger and easier as the thoughtful chords came musing out into the night and hovered amongst the dark trees. She found herself drawing easy breaths and relaxing completely against the support of the hard friendly sofa. How quietly everyone was listening.﻿ …

After a while, everything was dissolved, past and future and present and she was nothing but an ear, intent on the meditative harmony which stole out into the garden.

===Chinese===
沉思的和弦一路冥想着流进夜里、盘桓在黑树之间，她仿佛随之变得更大、更强壮、更舒展了。她发觉自己呼吸匀畅，整个人松弛下来，靠在那坚硬而友善的沙发支撑上。大家听得多么安静啊。……

过了一会儿，一切都溶化了，过去、未来和现在，她只剩下一只耳朵，专注地听那潜进花园里去的冥想和声。

---

===Original===
When the last gently strung notes had ceased she turned from her window and found Harriett’s near eye fixed upon her, the eyebrow travelling slowly up the forehead.

“Wow,” mouthed Miriam.

Harriett screwed her mouth to one side and strained her eyebrow higher.

The piano introduction to the Cavatina drowned the comments on the guest’s playing and the family relaxed once more into listening.

“Pink anemones, eh,” suggested Miriam softly.

Harriett drew in her chin and nodded approvingly.

“Pink anemones,” sighed Miriam, and turned to watch Margaret Wedderburn standing in her full-skirted white dress on the hearthrug in a radiance of red and golden light. Her heavily waving fair hair fell back towards its tightly braided basket of plaits from a face as serene as death. From between furry eyelashes her eyes looked steadfastly out, robbed of their everyday sentimental expression.

As she gazed at the broad white forehead, the fine gold down covering the cheeks and upper lip, and traced the outline of the heavy chin and firm large mouth and the steady arm that swept out in rich cello-like notes the devout theme of the lyric, Miriam drifted to an extremity of happiness.

===Chinese===
最后几个轻拨慢捻的音符停止时，她从窗边转过头，发现哈丽特凑得极近的一只眼睛正定定地盯着她，眉毛慢慢朝额头上旅行。

“哇。”米丽亚姆用口型说。

哈丽特把嘴拧到一边，眉毛又使劲挑高些。

卡伐蒂娜的钢琴引子淹没了关于客人琴艺的议论，一家人重新松弛下来，进入聆听。

“粉色银莲花，嗯。”米丽亚姆轻轻地说。

哈丽特收拢下巴，赞许地点点头。

“粉色银莲花。”米丽亚姆叹道，转身去看玛格丽特·韦德伯恩（Margaret Wedderburn）——她穿着宽摆白裙站在炉边地毯上，一片红金色的光华里。她那浓密波浪的淡色长发从一张恬静如死亡的脸后披垂下来，落向紧紧编成的篮式辫。毛茸茸的睫毛间，她的眼睛坚定地望出来，被剥去了平日那份多愁善感的表情。

她凝望着那宽阔白皙的额头，覆在双颊和上唇的细细金色绒毛，描摹着那沉甸甸的下巴和坚定大嘴的轮廓，还有那条沉稳的手臂——它正以大提琴般丰厚的音符扫出那支抒情曲虔诚的主题——米丽亚姆漂到了幸福的极点。

---

===Original===
… Tomorrow the room would be lit and decked and clear. Amongst the crowd of guests, he would come across the room, walking in his way.﻿ … She smiled to herself. He would come “sloping in” in his way, like a shadow, not looking at anyone. His strange friend would be with him. There would be introductions and greetings. Then he would dance with her silently and not looking at her, as if they were strangers, and then be dancing with someone else … with smiling, mocking, tender brown eyes and talking and answering and all the time looking about the room. And then again with her, cool and silent and not looking. And presently she would tell him about going away to Banbury Park.

===Chinese===
……明天，这房间就会点起灯、装点一新、清清爽爽。在一屋子宾客中间，他会以他特有的步子穿过房间走来。……她独自微笑。他会以他的样子“溜着边儿进来”，像一道影子，不看任何人。他那位古怪的朋友会跟他一起来。会有一番介绍和寒暄。然后他会一言不发地同她跳舞，不看她，仿佛他们是陌生人，接着又同别的什么人跳……带着微笑的、嘲弄的、温柔的棕色眼睛，说着话、应答着，眼睛却一直扫视全屋。然后又回到她这里，冷冷的、默默的、不看她。过一会儿她就要告诉他，自己要去班伯里公园了。

---

===Original===
Perhaps he would look wretched and miserable again as he had done when they were alone by the piano the Sunday before she went to Germany.﻿ … “Play ‘Abide with me,’ Miriam; play ‘Abide with me.’﻿”﻿ …

Tomorrow there would be another moment like that. He would say her name suddenly, as he had done last week at the Babingtons’ dance, very low, half-turning towards her. She would be ready this time and say his name and move instead of being turned to stone. Confidently the music assured her of that moment.

===Chinese===
也许他又会显出那副愁苦可怜的样子，像她去德国前的那个星期天、他们单独在钢琴边时那样。……“弹《求主同住》（Abide with Me）吧，米丽亚姆；弹《求主同住》吧。”……

明天会有另一个这样的时刻。他会突然说出她的名字，像上礼拜在巴宾顿家的舞会上那样，声音极低，半侧过身朝向她。这一次她会准备好，说出他的名字，动起来，而不是变成石头。音乐充满信心地向她担保了那个时刻。

---

===Original===
She lay looking quietly into his imagined face till the room had gone. Then the face grew dim and far off and at last receded altogether into darkness. That darkness was dreadful. It was his own life. She would never know it. However well they got to know each other they would always be strangers. Probably he never thought about her when he was alone. Only of Shakespeare and politics. What would he think if he knew she thought of him? But he thought of her when he saw her. That was utterly certain; the one thing certain in the world.﻿ … That day, coming along Putney Hill with mother, tired and dull and trying to keep her temper, passing his house, seeing him standing at his window, alone and pale and serious. The sudden lightening of his face surprised her again, violently, as she recalled it. It had lit up the whole world from end to end. He did not know that he had looked like that. She had turned swiftly from the sudden knowledge coming like a blow on her heart, that one day he would kiss her. Not for years and years. But one day he would bend his head. She wrenched herself from the thought, but it was too late. She thanked heaven she had looked; she wished she had not; the kiss had come; she would forget it; it had not touched her, it was like the breath of the summer. Everything had wavered; her feet had not felt the pavement. She remembered walking on, exulting with hanging head, cringing close to the ivy which hung from the top of the garden wall, sorry and pitiful towards her mother, and everyone who would never stand first with Ted.

===Chinese===
她躺着，静静望进他想象中的脸，直到房间消失。然后那张脸暗下去、远起来，终于整个退进黑暗里。那黑暗是可怕的。那是他自己的生活。她永远不会知道它。无论他们彼此了解得多深，他们将永远是陌生人。他独自一人时多半从来不想她。只想莎士比亚和政治。倘若他知道她想他，他会怎么想？可他见到她时是想她的。这一点千真万确；世上唯一确凿的事。……那一天，同妈妈沿着帕特尼山走来，又累又乏，竭力忍着性子，经过他的家，看见他站在窗前，独自一人，苍白而严肃。回忆起来，他脸上骤然一亮，又一次猛烈地震惊了她。那一亮把整个世界从头到尾都照亮了。他自己不知道自己那一刻是什么样子。她猛地转过脸去，躲开那像一记打击般落在心上的突然的知——总有一天他会吻她。不是许多许多年以后。可总有一天他会低下头来。她从这念头里硬生生挣脱出来，可太迟了。她谢天谢地自己看了那一眼；她但愿没看；那个吻已经来了；她会忘掉它；它没有碰到她，它像夏天的一息。一切都晃动过；她的脚没有踩到石板路。她记得自己一路走了下去，垂着头，扬扬得意，紧紧偎着从花园墙头垂下来的常春藤，心里对妈妈、对所有永远不会排在特德之先的人们，满怀歉意与怜悯。

---

===Original===
… There were girls who let themselves be kissed for fun.﻿ … Playing “Kiss in the Ring,” being kissed by someone they did not mean to always be with, all their life … how sad and dreadful. Why did it not break their hearts?

===Chinese===
……有的姑娘让人家为了取乐吻她们。……玩“环吻游戏”（Kiss in the Ring），被一个并不打算终身相守的人吻着，一辈子……多么悲哀，多么可怕。为什么她们的心竟不碎呢？

---

===Original===
Meg Wedderburn was smiling on her hearthrug, being thanked and praised. Her brown violin hung amongst the folds of her skirt.

“People *do* like us,” mused Miriam, listening to the peculiar sympathy of the family voice.

Meg was there, away from her own home, happy with them, the front door shut, their garden and house all round her and her strange luggage upstairs in one of the spare rooms. Nice Meg.﻿ …

===Chinese===
梅格在炉边地毯上微笑着，受人道谢、受人夸赞。她那褐色的小提琴悬在她裙子的褶影里。

“人们*确实*喜欢我们。”米丽亚姆冥想着，听着全家人声音里那种特有的温情。

梅格在这儿，离开了自己的家，同他们在一起快活，大门关着，他们的花园和房子四面围着她，她那古怪的行李搁在楼上一间空屋里。好梅格。……

---

===Original===
After breakfast the next morning Miriam sat in a low carpet chair at a window in the long bedroom she shared with Harriett. It was a morning of blazing sunlight and bright blue. She had just come up through the cool house from a rose-gathering tour of the garden with Harriett. A little bunch of pink anemones she had picked for herself were set in a tumbler on the wash-hand-stand.

She had left the door open to hear coming faintly up from the faraway drawing-room the tap-tap of hammering that told her Sarah and Eve were stretching the drugget.

On her knee lay her father’s cigarette-making machine and a parcel of papers and tobacco. An empty cigarette tin stood upon the windowsill.

She began packing tobacco into the groove of the machine, distributing and pressing it lightly with the tips of her fingers, watching as she worked the heavy pink cups of the anemones and the shining of their green stalks through the water. They were, she reflected, a little too much out. In the sun they would have come out still more. They would close up at night unless the rooms grew very hot. Slipping the paper evenly into the slot she shut the machine and turned the roller. As the sound of the loosely working cogs came up to her she revolted from her self-imposed task. She was too happy to make cigarettes. It would use up her happiness too stupidly.

She was surprised by a sudden suggestion that she should smoke the single cigarette herself. Why not? Why had she never yet smoked one? She glanced at the slowly swinging door. No one would come. She was alone on the top floor. Everyone was downstairs and busy. The finished cigarette lay on her knee. Taking it between her fingers she pressed a little hanging thread of tobacco into place. The cigarette felt pleasantly plump and firm. It was well made. As she rose to get matches the mowing machine sounded suddenly from the front lawn. She started and looked out of the window, concealing the cigarette in her hand. It was the gardener with bent shoulders pushing with all his might. With some difficulty she unhitched the phosphorescent matchbox from its place under the gas-bracket and got back into her low chair, invisible from the lawn.

The cool air flowed in garden-scented. She held the cigarette between two fingers. The match hissed and flared as she held it carefully below the sill, and the flame flowed towards her while she set the paper alight. Raising the cigarette to her lips she blew gently outwards, down through the tobacco. The flame twisted and went out, leaving the paper charred. She struck another match angrily, urging herself to draw, and drew little panting breaths with the cigarette well in the flame. It smoked. Blowing out the match she looked at the end of the cigarette. It was glowing all over and a delicate little spiral of smoke rose into her face. Quickly she applied her lips again and drew little breaths, opening her mouth wide between each breath and holding the cigarette sideways away from her. The end glowed afresh with each breath. The paper charred evenly away and little flecks of ash fell about her.

===Chinese===
第二天早晨，早饭之后，米丽亚姆坐在一张低低的铺毯面的椅子里，守着她同哈丽特合住的那间长卧室的一扇窗。那是一个烈日湛蓝的早晨。她刚同哈丽特在花园里采了一趟玫瑰，穿过凉凉的屋子走上来。她给自己摘的一小束粉色银莲花，插在脸盆架上的大玻璃杯里。

她让房门敞着，好听见从远处客厅里隐隐传上来的笃笃锤声——那是萨拉和伊芙在绷那张粗呢地毯（drugget）。

她膝上放着爸爸的卷烟机（cigarette-making machine）和一包卷烟纸与烟丝。窗台上放着一只空的香烟铁罐。

她开始把烟丝填进机器的凹槽，用指尖轻轻摊匀、轻按，一边干活一边望着银莲花沉甸甸的粉色杯盏和绿茎在水里发亮。她想，它们开得稍稍过了。在太阳底下还会开得更足。夜里就会闭拢，除非屋里变得很热。她把烟纸匀匀插进缝口，合上机器，转动滚轴。松松垮垮的齿轮声传上来，她对这自己派给自己的活儿反感起来。她太幸福了，幸福得不该拿来卷烟。那会把她的幸福蠢不可及地耗掉。

一个突然冒出的念头让她一怔——这支卷好的烟，何不她自己抽掉？有何不可？她怎么从来没抽过一支呢？她瞥了一眼慢慢晃动的房门。不会有人来的。她一个人在顶楼。大家都在楼下忙着。卷好的烟躺在她膝上。她把它夹在指间，把一小截耷拉出来的烟丝按回去。这支烟摸上去喜人地饱满结实。卷得真好。她起身去拿火柴，割草机忽然从前草坪那边响起来。她一惊，望向窗外，把烟藏进手心。是园丁，弓着肩膀，拼命推着机器。她费了点劲把那只磷面火柴盒从煤气灯架下它别着的地方解下来，回到她的低椅子里——从草坪上看不见的地方。

清凉的空气带着花园的香味流进来。她两指夹着烟。火柴嘶的一声燃起，她小心地把火举在窗台下面，火苗朝她这边淌过来，她点着了烟纸。她把烟举到唇边，轻轻朝外、朝下往烟丝里吹气。火苗扭了扭，灭了，烟纸焦了一圈。她气恼地又划一根火柴，催着自己吸，把烟凑在火苗里，小口小口喘息着吸。烟点着了。她吹灭火柴，看烟头。它整个红透了，一缕细细的烟螺旋着升上她的脸。她赶紧又把嘴唇凑上去，小口小口地吸，每吸一口都把嘴张得大大的，把烟侧着拿开。每吸一口，烟头就重新亮一次。烟纸匀匀地燃尽，一小片一小片的烟灰落在她身上。

---

===Original===
A third of the whole length was consumed. Her nostrils breathed in smoke, and as she tasted the burnt flavour the sweetness of the unpolluted air all around her was a new thing. The acrid tang in her nostrils intoxicated her. She drew more boldly. There was smoke in her mouth. She opened it quickly, sharply exhaling a yellow cloud oddly different from the grey spirals wreathing their way from the end of the cigarette. She went on drawing in mouthful after mouthful of smoke, expelling each quickly with widely-opened lips, turning to look at the well-known room through the yellow haze and again at the sky, which drew nearer as she puffed at it. The sight of the treetops scrolled with her little clouds brought her a sense of power. She had chosen to smoke and she was smoking, and the morning world gleamed back at her.﻿ …

===Chinese===
整支烟烧掉了三分之一。她的鼻孔吸进烟气，尝到焦味的同时，四围未受污染的空气的甜美成了一样新东西。鼻孔里那股辛辣的气味使她陶然。她吸得更大胆了。嘴里有了烟。她很快张开嘴，猛地呼出一团黄云，同烟头袅袅盘旋而上的灰螺旋奇怪地不一样。她一口接一口地吸满嘴的烟，每吸一口就张开嘴很快吐掉，转头隔着黄色的薄雾看看这间熟识的房间，再看看天空——她朝天喷烟，天就朝她凑近。树梢卷着她小小的烟云，这景象给她一种权力的感觉。她自己选择了抽烟，她正在抽，而早晨的世界朝她熠熠回望。……

---

===Original===
The morning gleamed. She would choose her fate. It should be amongst green trees and sunshine. That daunted lump who had accepted the post at Banbury Park had nothing to do with her. Morning gladness flooded her, and her gladness of the thought of the evening to come quickened as it had done last night into certainty.

She burned the last inch of the cigarette in the grate, wrapped with combings from the toilet-tidy in a screw of paper. When all was consumed she carefully replaced the summer bundle of ornamental mohair behind the bars.

Useless to tell anyone. No one would believe she had not felt ill. She found it difficult to understand why anyone should feel sick from smoking. Dizzy perhaps﻿ … a little drunk. Pater’s tobacco was very strong, some people could not smoke it.﻿ … She had smoked a whole cigarette of strong tobacco and liked it. Raising her arms above her head she worked them upwards, stretching every muscle of her body. No, she was anything but ill.

Leaving the window wide she went on to the landing. The smell of tobacco was everywhere. She flung into each room in turn, throwing up windows and leaving doors propped ajar.

Harriett coming up the garden with a basket of cut flowers saw her at the cook’s bedroom window.

“What on earth you doing thayer!” she shrieked putting down her basket.

Hanging from the window Miriam made a trumpet of her hands.

“Something blew in!”

===Chinese===
早晨熠熠发亮。她要选择自己的命运。它应该在绿树和阳光中间。那个吓怕了的、接受了班伯里公园教职的家伙，同她毫无关系。清晨的欢悦漫过她，想到即将来临的夜晚，那份欢悦像昨夜一样加快成确信。

她把最后一截烟连着梳发盒里集的落发，裹进一卷纸里，在炉膛里烧掉。都烧尽了，她把那束夏天遮炉子用的装饰马海毛小心放回炉条后面。

告诉谁都没用。谁也不会相信她没觉得难受。她觉得很难理解，抽烟怎么会让人恶心。晕乎倒是也许……有点醉。爸爸（Pater）的烟丝很凶，有的人抽不得。……她抽了一整支凶烟丝的烟，还挺喜欢。她把双臂举过头顶，向上伸展，把全身每一条肌肉都抻开。不，她哪儿有什么不舒服。

她让窗大敞着，走到楼梯平台上。烟味到处都是。她挨个儿冲进每间屋子，推开窗，让房门半掩着支住。

哈丽特挎着一篮剪下的花从花园上来，看见她在厨娘卧室的窗前。

“你到底在那儿干吗呀！”她尖叫着放下篮子。

米丽亚姆从窗口探出身子，双手拢成喇叭。

“有什么东西被风吹进来了！”

---

===Original===
All preparations for the evening were made and the younger members of the household were having a late tea in the breakfast-room. “We’ve done the alcoves,” said Sarah explosively, “in case it rains.”

Nan Babington sat up in her long chair to bring her face round to the deep bay where Sarah stood.

“My *dear*! Seraphina! And she’s doing the pink bows! *Will* some saint take my cup? Ta.﻿ … My dear, how *perfectly* screaming.”

Miriam raised her head from the petal-scattered table, where she lay prone side by side with Harriett, to watch Nan sitting up in her firm white dress beaming at Sarah through her slanting eye.

“What flowers you going to wear, Nan?”

Nan patted her sleek slightly Japanese-looking hair. “*Ah*﻿ … splashes of scarlet, my dear. Splashes of scarlet. One in my hair and one here.” She patted the broad level of her enviable breast towards the left shoulder.

“Almost *on* the shoulder, you know—arranged flat, *can’t* be squashed and showing as you dance.”

“Geraniums! Oom. You’ve got awfully good taste. What a frightfully good effect. Bright red and bright white. Clean. Go on, Nan.”

“*Killing*,” pursued Nan. “Tom said at breakfast with his mouth absolutely *full* of sweetbread, ‘it’ll rain’﻿—growled, you know, with his mouth *crammed* full. ‘Never mind, Tommy,’ said Ella with the *utmost* promptitude, ‘they’re sure to have the alcoves.’ ‘Oomph,’ growled Tommy, pretending not to care. *Naughty* Tommy, naughty, *naughty* Tommy!”

“Any cake left?” sighed Miriam, sinking back amongst her petals and hoping that Nan’s voice would go on.

“You girls are the most adorable individuals I ever met.﻿ … *Did* anybody see Pearlie going home this afternoon?”

Everyone chuckled and waited.

“My *dears*! My *dears*!! Bevan *dragged* me along to the end of the pavilion to see him enter up the handicaps with his new automatic pen﻿—*awfully* smashing﻿—and I was just hobbling the last few yards past the apple trees when we *saw* Pearlie hand-in-hand with the Botterford boys, prancing along the asphalt court﻿—*prancing*, my dears!”

Miriam and Harriett dragged themselves up to see. Nan bridled and swayed from listener to listener, her wide throat gleaming as she sang out her words.

===Chinese===
晚间的一切准备都停当了，家里的年轻一辈正在早餐室里吃下午较晚的茶。“我们把凹室都弄好了，”萨拉没头没脑地冒出一句，“以防下雨。”

楠·巴宾顿在躺椅里坐起身，把脸转过来朝着萨拉站着的深凹窗间。

“我*亲爱的*！塞拉菲娜！还叫她钉粉蝴蝶结！哪位*圣徒*行行好替我端端杯子？谢谢。……亲爱的，这可*实在*笑死人了。”

米丽亚姆从撒满花瓣的餐桌那边抬起头——她同哈丽特并排趴在桌上——看楠穿着挺括的白裙坐起身，用她那斜眼喜洋洋地朝萨拉放光。

“你打算戴什么花，楠？”

楠拍了拍她那微微日式、光溜溜的头发。“*啊*……大红的一泼泼，我亲爱的。大红的一泼泼。头发里一朵，这儿一朵。”她拍了拍她那令人艳羡的胸口那片宽阔的平坡，靠左肩的地方。

“几乎就*在*肩膀上，你知道——平贴着摆，*压*不坏，跳舞的时候看得见。”

“天竺葵！嗯。你的品味好极了。效果妙透了。鲜红配雪白。干净。接着说，楠。”

“*要命*，”楠接着说。“汤姆（Tom）早饭时嘴里塞得*满满*的甜面包，还说什么‘要下雨’——咕哝着说的，你知道，嘴里*塞*得满满的。‘不要紧，汤米（Tommy），’埃拉（Ella）以*最快*的口快说，‘他们肯定备好了凹室。’‘嗯哼，’汤米咕哝，装作不在乎。*淘气*的汤米，淘气，*淘气*的汤米！”

“还有蛋糕剩吗？”米丽亚姆叹着气问，重新伏回她的花瓣堆里，盼着楠的声音说下去。

“你们这些姑娘是我见过的最可爱的人了。……今天下午有人看见珀莉回家去吗？”

人人都轻笑着等下文。

“我*亲爱的们*！我*亲爱的们*！！贝文*拖*着我到亭子那头，看他用他新买的自动钢笔登记让分赛——*神气*极了——我正一瘸一拐挨过苹果树前最后几码，我们*看见*珀莉同博特福德家的男孩们（the Botterford boys）手拉手，在沥青球场上昂首阔步——*昂首阔步*呢，亲爱的们！”

米丽亚姆和哈丽特撑起身来看。楠昂着头，从这张脸晃到那张脸，宽宽的喉咙发着亮，把她的话唱出来。

===Original===
“Prancing﻿—with straggles of grey hair sticking out and that *tiny* sailor hat cocked *almost* on to her nose. My dear, you sh’d’ve *seen* Bevan! He put up his eyeglass, my dears, for a *fraction* of a second,” Nan’s head went up﻿—“Madame Pompadour,” thought Miriam﻿—and her slanting eyes glanced down her nose, “and dropped it, clickety-click. You sh’d’ve *seen* the expression on his angelic countenance.”

“I say, she *is* an awful little creature, isn’t she?” said Miriam, watching Eve bend a crimson face over the tea-tray on the hearthrug. “She put her boots on the pavilion table this afternoon when all those men were there﻿—about a mile high they are﻿—with tassels. Why *does* she go on like that?”

“Men like that sort of thing,” said Sarah lightly.

“Sally!”

“They do.﻿ … I believe she drinks.”

“Sally! My *dear*!”

“I believe she *does*. She’s always having shandygaff with the men.”

“Oh, well, perhaps she doesn’t,” murmured Eve.

“Chuck me a lump of sugar, Eve.”

Miriam subsided once more amongst the rose petals.

“Bevvy thinks I oughtn’t to dance.”

“Did he say so?”

“Of course, my dear. But old Wyman said I could, every third, except the Lancers.”

“You sh’d’ve seen Bevvy’s face. ‘Brother Tommy doesn’t object,’ I said. ‘He’s going to look after me!’ ‘*Is* he?’ said Bevvy in his *most* superior manner.”

“What a fearful scrunching you’re making,” said Harriett, pinching Miriam’s nose.

“Let’s go and dress,” said Miriam, rolling off the table.

===Chinese===
“昂首阔步——灰白的头发披散出来，那顶*小小的*水手帽翘得*几乎*搭上鼻尖。亲爱的，你真该*看看*贝文！他举起单片眼镜，亲爱的们，举了*一眨巴眼*的工夫，”楠的头扬上去——“蓬帕杜夫人（Madame Pompadour），”米丽亚姆想——斜眼顺着鼻梁往下扫，“又放下来，咔嗒一响。你真该*看看*他那张天使脸孔上的表情。”

“我说，她*真*是个可怕的小东西，是不是？”米丽亚姆说，看着伊芙在炉边地毯上朝茶盘俯下一张通红的脸。“今天下午那些男人都在的时候，她把靴子翘上亭子桌——那靴子有一英里高——还带着穗子。她*干嘛*总那样？”

“男人就喜欢那一套。”萨拉轻轻地说。

“萨莉！”

“就是嘛。……我看她喝酒。”

“萨莉！我*亲爱的*！”

“我看她*就是*喝。她总跟男人们喝香迪格夫（shandygaff）。”

“哦，好吧，也许她不喝。”伊芙喃喃说。

“扔块方糖给我，伊芙。”

米丽亚姆又伏回玫瑰花瓣里。

“贝维（Bevvy）觉得我不该跳舞。”

“他这么说了？”

“当然啦，亲爱的。可老怀曼说我可以，每隔三支跳一支，方阵舞（the Lancers）除外。”

“你真该看看贝维的脸。‘汤米哥哥不反对，’我说。‘他要照看我呢！’‘*是吗*？’贝维用他*最*居高临下的腔调说。”

“你嚼得多吓人啊。”哈丽特说着，捏了捏米丽亚姆的鼻子。

“咱们更衣去吧。”米丽亚姆说着从桌上滚下来。

===Original===
“How many times has she met him?” asked Miriam as they went through the hall.

“I dunno. Not many.”

“I think it’s simply hateful.”

“Mimmy!” It was Nan’s insinuating voice.

“Coming,” called Miriam. “And, you know, Tommy needn’t think he can carry on with *Meg* in an alcove.”

“*What* would she think? Let’s go and tell Meg she must dress.”

“Mimmy!”

Miriam went back and put her head round the breakfast-room door.

“Let me see you when you’re dressed.”

“Why?”

“I want to kiss the back of your neck, my dear; love kissing people’s necks.”

Miriam smiled herself vaguely out of the room, putting away the unpleasant suggestion.

“I wish I’d got a dress like Nan’s,” she said, joining Harriett in the dark lobby.

“I say, somebody’s been using the ‘Financial Times’ to cut up flowers on. It’s all wet.” Harriett lifted the limp newspaper from the marble-topped coil of pipes and shook it.

“Hang it up somewhere.”

“Where? Everything’s cleared up.”

“Stick it out of the lavatory window and pull the window down on it.”

“Awri, you hold the door open.”

Miriam laughed as Harriett fell into the room.

===Chinese===
“她同他见过几回面了？”穿过门厅时米丽亚姆问。

“不知道。没几回。”

“我觉得这实在可恶。”

“米米！”是楠那缠人的声音。

“来了。”米丽亚姆应道。“还有，你可知道，汤米休想他能在凹室里同*梅格*调情。”

“她会*怎么*想？咱们去告诉梅格她得更衣了。”

“米米！”

米丽亚姆折回去，把头探进早餐室的门。

“你更衣完了让我看看。”

“为什么？”

“我想吻你后颈呀，亲爱的；我就爱吻人的后颈。”

米丽亚姆恍惚地笑着走出房间，把这不愉快的暗示撇开。

“我但愿有件楠那样的裙子。”她说着，在黑暗的过道里同哈丽特会合。

“喂，有人拿《金融时报》（Financial Times）垫着切过花。全湿了。”哈丽特把那份软塌塌的报纸从卷着的大理石面暖气管上提起来抖了抖。

“把它挂哪儿吧。”

“挂哪儿？东西全收拾走了。”

“把它从盥洗室窗户里捅出去，把窗压下来夹住。”

“好嘞，你扶着门别关。”

哈丽特一头栽进屋里，米丽亚姆笑了。

===Original===
“Blooming bootjack.”

“Is it all right in there? Are all the pegs clear? Is the washing-basin all right?”

A faint light came in as Harriett pushed up the frosted pane.

“Here’s a pair of boots all over the floor and your old Zulu hat hanging on a peg. The basin’s all right except a perfectly foul smell of nicotine. It’s pater’s old feather.”

“That doesn’t matter. The men won’t mind that. My old hat can stay. There are ten pegs out here and all the slab, and there’s hardly anything on the hall stand. That’s it. Don’t cram the window down so as to cut the paper. That’ll do. Come on.”

“I wish I had a really stunning dress,” remarked Miriam, as they tapped across the wide hall.

“You needn’t.”

The drawing-room door was open. They surveyed the sea of drugget, dark grey in the fading light. “*Pong*-pong-*pong* de doodle, *pong*-pong-*pong* de doodle,” murmured Miriam as they stood swaying on tiptoe in the doorway.

“Let’s have the gas and *two* candlesticks, Harry, on the dressing-table under the gas.”

“All right,” mouthed Harriett in a stage whisper, making for the stairs as the breakfast-room door opened.

It was Eve. “I say, Eve, I’m *scared*,” said Miriam, meeting her.

Eve giggled triumphantly.

“Look here. I shan’t come down at first. I’ll play the first dance. I’ll get them all started with ‘Bittersweet.’﻿”

“Don’t worry, Mim.”

“My *dear*, I simply don’t know how to face the evening.”

“You do,” murmured Eve. “You are proud.”

“What of?”

“You know quite well.”

“What?”

“He’s the nicest boy we know.”

“But he’s not my boy. Of course not. You’re insane. Besides, I don’t know who you’re talking about.”

“Oh, well, we won’t talk. We’ll go and arrange your chignon.”

“I’m going to have simply twists and perhaps a hair ornament.”

===Chinese===
“该死的脱靴器。”

“里面都妥了吗？挂钩都空着吗？洗脸盆没事吧？”

哈丽特推开磨砂玻璃窗，一点微光进来。

“地板上丢着一只靴子，你的旧祖鲁帽（Zulu hat）挂在挂钩上。盆没事，就是一股烟油子臭味，冲极了。是爸爸那根旧羽毛。”

“那不要紧。先生们不会介意的。我的旧帽子就搁那儿吧。外面有十个挂钩，还有整块搁板，门厅衣帽架上几乎没挂什么。就这样。别把窗压得太死，把报纸剪坏了。行了。走吧。”

“我但愿有一件真正惊艳的裙子。”米丽亚姆说，两人嗒嗒地走过宽阔的门厅。

“你用不着。”

客厅门开着。她们检阅那满地粗呢地毯的汪洋，在渐暗的光里呈着深灰。“*嘭*-嘭-*嘭*德嘟豆，*嘭*-嘭-*嘭*德嘟豆。”米丽亚姆低声哼着，两人踮着脚尖在门口轻轻摇晃。

“点煤气灯，再加*两*支烛台，哈里，放在煤气灯下面的梳妆台上。”

“好。”哈丽特用舞台式的耳语说，朝楼梯走去，这时早餐室的门开了。

是伊芙。“喂，伊芙，我*怕*。”米丽亚姆迎上去说。

伊芙得意地咯咯笑。

“听我说。一开始我不下楼。第一支舞我来弹。我用《苦甜》（Bittersweet）把他们全都带起来。”

“别愁，米姆。”

“我*亲爱的*，我简直不知道怎样面对今天晚上。”

“你知道的。”伊芙喃喃说。“你骄傲。”

“骄傲什么？”

“你心里清楚得很。”

“什么？”

“他是我们认识的最好的男孩子。”

“可他不是我的男孩子。当然不是。你疯了。再说，我都不知道你说的是谁。”

“哦，好吧，我们不谈了。我们去盘你的发髻。”

“我就简简单单拧几个转儿，兴许再戴个发饰。”

===Original===
Miriam reached the conservatory from the garden door and set about opening the lid of the grand piano. She could see at the far end of the almost empty drawing-room a little ruddy thickset bearded man with a roll of music under his arm talking to her mother. He was standing very near to her, surrounding her with his eager presence. “Mother’s wonderful,” thought Miriam, with a moment’s adoration for Mrs. Henderson’s softly-smiling girlish tremulousness. Listening to the man’s hilarious expostulating narrative voice she fumbled hastily for her waltz amongst the scattered piles of music on the lid of the piano.

As she struck her opening chords she watched her mother gently quell the narrative and steer the sturdy form towards a group of people hesitating in the doorway. “Have they had coffee?” she wondered anxiously. “Is Mary driving them into the dining room properly?” Before she had reached the end of her second page everyone had disappeared. She paused a moment and looked down the brightly lit empty room﻿—the sight of the cold sheeny drugget filled her with despair. The hilarious voice resounded in the hall. There couldn’t be many there yet. Were they all looking after them properly? For a moment she was tempted to leave her piano and go and make some desperate attempt at geniality. Then the sound of the pervading voice back again in the room and brisk footsteps coming towards the conservatory drove her back to her music. The little man stepped quickly over the low moulding into the conservatory.

===Chinese===
米丽亚姆从花园门走进花房，动手开大钢琴的琴盖。她看得见几乎空着的客厅那头，一个红光满面、矮壮结实、蓄着胡子的小个子男人，臂下夹着一卷乐谱，正同她母亲说话。他站得离她极近，用他热切的存在把她整个围住。“妈妈真了不起。”米丽亚姆想着，一时对亨德森太太那柔柔含笑、少女似的轻颤生出崇拜。她听着那男人兴高采烈、连连分辩的叙事嗓音，在钢琴盖上散乱的几堆乐谱里急急翻找她的华尔兹。

她弹出开头的和弦，眼睛看着母亲轻轻按住那人的叙述，把那结实的身形引向门口一群踌躇不前的人。“他们喝上咖啡了吗？”她焦虑地想。“玛丽把他们好好让进饭厅了吗？”还没弹到第二页末尾，人都不见了。她停了停，顺着灯火通明的空房间望下去——那片冷冷的、亮光光的粗呢地毯看得她满心绝望。那兴高采烈的嗓音在门厅里回荡。来的人想必还不多。大家把他们照应好了吗？有一阵她真想丢下钢琴，去做一次豁出去的亲热。随即，那无所不在的嗓门又回到厅里、轻快的脚步朝花房走来，把她赶回她的音乐里。那小个子男人快步迈过矮线脚，走进花房。

===Original===
“Ah, Mariamne,” he blared gently.

“Oh, Bennett, you angel, how *did* you get here so early?” responded Miriam, playing with zealous emphasis.

“Got old Barrowgate to finish off the outpatients,” he said with a choke of amusement.

“I say, Mirry, don’t you play. Let me take it on. You go and ply the light fantastic.” He laid his hands upon her shoulders and burred the tune she was playing like a muted euphonium over the top of her head. “No. It’s all right. Go and get them dancing. Get over the awfulness﻿—*you* know.”

“Get over the awfulness, eh? Oh, I’ll get over the awfulness.”

“Ssh﻿—are there many there?”

They both looked round into the drawing-room.

Nan Babington was backing slowly up and down the room supported by the outstretched arms of Bevan Seymour, her black head thrown back level with his, the little scarlet knot in her hair hardly registering the smooth movements of her invisible feet.

“They seem to have begun,” shouted Bennett in a whisper as Harriett and her fiancé swung easily circling into the room and were followed by two more couples.

“Go and dance with Meg. She only knows Tommy Babington.”

“Like the lid up?”

===Chinese===
“啊，玛丽亚姆涅（Mariamne）。”他温和地大声嚷道。

“哦，贝内特（Bennett），你这天使，你怎么*这么*早就到了？”米丽亚姆应道，弹得加倍起劲。

“让老巴罗盖特（Barrowgate）替我把门诊看完啦。”他说着，乐得直呛。

“我说，米丽，你别弹了。让我来接。你去翩翩起舞吧。”他两手搭上她的肩，把她正弹的调子像一支加了弱音器的上低音号（euphonium）似的，嗡嗡地吹过她的头顶。“不。没事的。去让他们跳起来。把那难堪劲儿熬过去——*你*懂的。”

“把难堪劲儿熬过去，嗯？哦，我会熬过去的。”

“嘘——那边人多吗？”

两人都回头朝客厅里望。

楠·巴宾顿正由贝文·西摩平伸的双臂架着，在厅里缓缓地进退，她乌黑的头后仰着，同他的头齐平，发间那小小的红结几乎显不出她那看不见的双脚平稳的移动。

“好像已经开始了。”贝内特用喊叫般的耳语说——正赶上哈丽特和她未婚夫轻松旋进厅里，后面又跟进来两对。

“去同梅格跳吧。她只认得汤米·巴宾顿（Tommy Babington）。”

“琴盖要不要掀起来？”

===Original===
Miriam’s rhythmic clangour doubled its resonance in the tiled conservatory as the great lid of the piano went up.

“*Magnifique*, Mirry, *parfaitement magnifique*,” intoned Tommy Babington, appearing in the doorway with Meg on his arm.

“Bonsoir, Tomasso.”

“You are like an expressive metronome.”

“Oh﻿—*nom d’un pipe*.”

“You would make a rhinoceros dance.”

Adjusting his pince-nez he dexterously seized tall Meg and swung her rapidly in amongst the dancers.

“Sarah’ll say he’s had a Turkish bath,” thought Miriam, recalling the unusual clear pallor of his rather overfed face. “Pleated shirt. That’s to impress Meg.”

She felt all at once that the air seemed cold. It was not like a summer night. How badly the ferns were arranged. Nearly all of them together on the staging behind the end of the piano; not enough visible from the drawing-room. Her muscles were somehow stiffening into the wrong mood. Presently she would be playing badly. She watched the forms circling past the gap in the curtains and slowed a little. The room seemed fairly full.

“That’s it﻿—perfect, Mim,” signalled Harriett’s partner, swinging her by. She held to the fresh rhythm and passing into a tender old waltz tune that she knew by heart gave herself to her playing. She need not watch the feet any longer. She could go on forever. She knew she was not playing altogether for the dancers. She was playing to two hearers. But she could not play that tune if they came. They would be late. But they must be here now. Where were they? Were they having coffee? Dancing? She flung a terrified glance at the room and met the cold eye of Bevan Seymour. She would not look again. The right feeling for the dreamy old tune came and went uncontrollably. Why did they not come? Presently she would be cold and sick and done for, for the evening. She played on, harking back to the memory of the kindly challenge in the eyes of her brother-in-law to be, dancing gravely with a grave Harriett﻿—fearing her﻿ … writing in her album:

> “She was his life,
 

 The ocean to the river of his thoughts—
 

 Which terminated all.”

===Chinese===
大琴盖一掀起来，米丽亚姆那节奏铿锵的琴声在铺花砖的花房里共鸣加倍。

“*Magnifique*（妙极了），米丽，*parfaitement magnifique*（妙不可言）。”汤米·巴宾顿抑扬顿挫地念着，挽着梅格出现在门口。

“Bonsoir（晚上好），汤马索（Tomasso）。”

“你像一台富有表情的节拍器。”

“哦——*nom d’un pipe*（我的老天）。”

“你能让犀牛都跳起舞来。”

他正一正夹鼻眼镜，麻利地一把揽过高挑的梅格，飞快地把她旋进舞者的行列。

“萨拉准会说他去洗了土耳其浴。”米丽亚姆想着，回想起他那张略嫌饱食的脸上那不寻常的清白脸色。“打褶衬衫。那是给梅格看的。”

她忽然觉得空气似乎冷了。不像一个夏夜。蕨草摆得多糟啊。几乎全堆在钢琴后面尽头的花架上；从客厅里望过去看不见多少。她的肌肉不知怎么正僵进一种不对的情绪里。过一会儿她就要弹坏了。她望着人影一圈圈掠过帘子的豁口，放慢了一点。厅里人似乎相当满了。

“就是这调子——完美，米姆。”哈丽特的舞伴擦身而过时向她打信号。她守住这新鲜的节奏，转入一支她烂熟于心的温柔的旧华尔兹，把自己整个交给琴。她不必再看那些脚了。她可以一直弹下去。她知道自己并不全是为跳舞的人弹。她是弹给两位听者。可他们要是来了，她就弹不成那支曲子了。他们会迟到。可他们此刻本该到了。他们在哪儿？在喝咖啡？在跳舞？她惊惶地朝厅里一瞥，正碰上贝文·西摩冷冷的眼睛。她不再看了。配那支迷蒙旧曲的正当情绪来了又去，抓不住。他们怎么还不来？再过一会儿她就要又冷又恶心，这一晚就完了。她继续弹，回想着她那位准姐夫眼里友善的挑战——他正神情庄重地同庄重的哈丽特跳着——怕她……在纪念册里写：

> “她是他的生命，
 

 是他思想之河所奔赴的海洋——
 

 万流到此而尽。”

===Original===
… cold, calm little Harriett. Her waltz had swung soft and low and the dancers were hushed. Only Tommy Babington’s voice still threaded the little throng.

Someone held back the near curtain. A voice said quietly, “Here she is.”

===Chinese===
……冷冷的、静静的小哈丽特。她的华尔兹已放得又软又低，舞者们都被压静了。只有汤米·巴宾顿的声音还在那一小群人里穿来穿去。

有人撩开近处的帘子。一个声音静静地说：“她来了。”
