===Original===
Miriam sat on in the schoolroom after supper with the newspaper spread out on the brown American cloth table cover under the gas. She found a long column headed “The Royal Commission on Education.” The Queen, then, was interesting herself in education. But in England the sovereign had no power, was only a figurehead. Perhaps the Queen had been advised to interest herself in education by the Privy Council and the Conservatives, people of leisure and cultivation. A commission was a sort of command﻿—it must be important, something the Privy Council had decided and sent out in the Queen’s name.

She read her column, sitting comfortlessly between the window and the open door. As she read the room grew still. The memory of the talking and clinking supper-table faded, and presently even the ticking of the clock was no longer there. She raised her head at last. No wonder people read newspapers. You could read about what was going on in the country, actually what the Government was doing at that very moment. Of course; men seemed to know such a lot because they read the newspapers and talked about what was in them. But anybody could know as much as the men sitting in the armchairs if they chose; read all about everything, written down for everybody to see. That was the freedom of the Press﻿—Areopagitica, that the history books said so much about and was one of those new important things, more important than facts and dates. Like the Independence of Ireland. Yet very few people really talked like newspapers. Only angry men with loud voices. Here was the free Press that Milton had gone to prison for. Certainly it made a great difference. The room was quite changed. There was hardly any pain in the silent cane-seated chairs. There were really people making the world better. Now. At last. Perhaps it was rather a happy fate to be a teacher in the Banbury Park school and read newspapers. There were plenty of people who could neither read nor write. Someone had a servant like that who did all the marketing and never forgot anything or made any mistake over the change﻿—none the worse for it, pater said, people who wanted book-learning could get it, there must always be hewers of wood and drawers of water, laissez-faire. But Gladstone did not believe that. At this moment Gladstone was saying that because the people of England as a whole were uneducated their “condition of ignorance” affected the whole of the “body politic.” That was Gladstone. He had found that out﻿ … with large moist silky eyes like a dog and pointed collars seeing things as they were and going to change them.﻿ … Miriam stirred uneasily as she felt the beating of her heart.﻿ … If only she were at home how she could rush up and down the house and shout about it and shake Mary by the shoulders. She shrank into herself and sat stiffly up, suddenly discovering she was lounging over the table. As she moved she reflected that probably Gladstone’s being so very dark made him determined that things should not go on as they were. In that case Gladstonians would be dark﻿—perhaps not musical. Someone had said musical people were a queer soft lot. Laissez-faire. Lazy fair. But perhaps it was possible to be fair and musical and to be a Gladstonian too. You can’t have your cake and eat it. No. It was a good thing, one’s best self knew it was a good thing that someone had found out why people were so awful; like a dentist finding out a bad tooth however much it hurt. Only if education was going to be the principal thing and all teachers were to be “qualified” it was no use going on. Miss Jenny had said private schools were doomed.

===Chinese===
晚饭后，米丽亚姆仍在教室里坐着，报纸摊开在煤气灯下棕色美国油布的桌布上。她找到一长栏，标题是“皇家教育委员会”（The Royal Commission on Education）。这么说，女王也关心起教育来了。可在英国，君主没有实权，不过是个名义首领。也许女王是受了枢密院和保守党人的劝告才来关心教育的——都是有闲、有教养的人。委员会是一种命令——想必是重大的事，是枢密院议定、以女王名义颁布的。

她读她那一栏，坐在窗和敞开的门之间，浑身不得劲。她读着，房间静了下来。晚饭桌上又谈又碰杯的记忆淡去了，不多久，连时钟的滴答也不在了。她终于抬起头来。难怪人们读报纸。你可以读到国内正在发生的事，实实在在读到政府此时此刻正在做什么。当然；男人们显得懂得那么多，是因为他们读报、谈论报上的东西。可谁只要愿意，都能懂得同扶手椅里坐着的男人们一样多；一切都有白纸黑字，写给所有人看。这就是出版自由——《论出版自由》（Areopagitica），历史书里大书特书、属于那些崭新的重大事物之一，比史实和日期更重大。像爱尔兰的独立一样。然而真正说话像报纸的人很少。只有些嗓门大的愤怒男人。这就是弥尔顿为之坐牢的自由报刊。这确实大不一样。房间整个变了。静静的藤座椅里几乎不再有痛楚。世上真的有人在把世界变好。现在。终于。在班伯里公园的学校做教师、读读报纸，也许倒是相当幸福的命运。世上多的是不会读写的人。有谁家的女仆就是这样，采买全靠她，从不忘事、找钱从不出错——也并不因此差什么，爸爸说，想要书本学问的人自可以去求，世上总得有人砍柴挑水，自由放任（laissez-faire）。可格莱斯顿不信这个。此时此刻，格莱斯顿正在说：因为英格兰人民整体未受教育，他们的“无知状况”便影响整个“政治机体”。这就是格莱斯顿。他发现了这一点……一双狗一样湿润柔亮的大眼睛，尖尖的衣领，看见事物的本来面目，并且要去改变它们。……米丽亚姆觉出自己心跳，不安地动了动。……她要是在家该多好，可以在屋子里冲上冲下，为这个大喊大叫，抓住玛丽的肩膀摇。……她缩回自己里面，僵硬地坐直，忽然发现自己正瘫伏在桌上。她一动，便想到多半是因为格莱斯顿生得那么黑，才使他决意不许事情照旧下去。那样的话，格莱斯顿派就都黑——也许不爱好音乐。有谁说过爱好音乐的人是一伙古怪的软心肠。自由放任（laissez-faire）。懒散放任（lazy fair）。可也许又公正、又懂音乐、又是格莱斯顿派，也是可能的。鱼与熊掌不可兼得。不。这是件好事，人最好的那个自我知道这是件好事：有人查明了人为什么这么糟糕；就像牙医找出一颗坏牙，不管多疼。只是，倘若教育就要成为头等大事、所有教师都要“持证”，那再走下去就没有用了。珍妮小姐说过，私立学校气数已尽。

---

===Original===
For a long time she sat blankly contemplating the new world that was coming. Everyone would be trained and efficient but herself. She was not strong enough to earn a living and qualify as a teacher at the same time. The day’s work tired her to death. She must hide somewhere.﻿ … She would not be wanted.﻿ … If you were not wanted.﻿ … If you knew you were not wanted﻿—you ought to get out of the way. Chloroform. Someone had drunk a bottle of carbolic acid. The clock struck ten. Gathering up the newspaper she folded it neatly, put it on the hall table and went slowly upstairs, watching the faint reflection of the half-lowered hall gas upon the polished balustrade. The staircase was cold and airy. Cold rooms and landings stretched up away above her into the darkness. She became aware of a curious buoyancy rising within her. It was so strange that she stood still for a moment on the stair. For a second, life seemed to cease in her and the staircase to be swept from under her feet.﻿ … “I’m alive.”﻿ … It was as if something had struck her, struck right through her impalpable body, sweeping it away, leaving her there shouting silently without it. I’m alive.﻿ … I’m alive. Then with a thump her heart went on again and her feet carried her body warm and happy and elastic easily on up the solid stairs. She tried once or twice deliberately to bring back the breathless moment standing still on a stair. Each time something of it returned. “It’s me, *me*; this is *me* being alive,” she murmured with a feeling under her like the sudden drop of a lift. But her thoughts distracted her. They were eagerly talking to her declaring that she had had this feeling before. She opened her bedroom door very quietly. The air of the room told her that Nancie and Beadie were asleep. Going lightly across to the chest of drawers dressing-table by the window as if she were treading on air, she stood holding its edge in the darkness. Two forgotten incidents flowed past her in quick succession; one of waking up on her seventh birthday in the seaside villa alone in a small dark room and suddenly saying to herself that one day her father and mother would die and she would still be there, and after a curious moment when the darkness seemed to move against her, feeling very old and crying bitterly, and another of standing in the bow of the dining-room window at Barnes looking at the raindrops falling from the leaves through the sunshine and saying to Eve, who came into the room as she watched, “D’you know, Eve, I feel as if I’d suddenly wakened up out of a dream.” The bedroom was no longer dark. She could see the outlines of everything in the light coming from the streetlamps through the half-closed Venetian blinds. Beadie sighed and stirred. Miriam began impatiently preparing for bed without lighting the gas. “What’s the use of feeling like that if it doesn’t stay? It doesn’t change anything. Next time I’ll make it stay. It might whisk me right away. There’s something in me that can’t be touched or altered. Me. If it comes again. If it’s stronger every time.﻿ … Perhaps it goes on getting stronger till you die.”

===Chinese===
她久久木然坐着，凝想那个正在到来的新世界。人人都将受训练、都有效率，只除了她。她不够强壮，没法一边谋生一边考教师资格。一天的工作把她累得要死。她得躲到什么地方去。……人家不会要她了。……倘若人家不要你。……倘若你自知人家不要你——就该让开路。氯仿。有谁喝了一瓶石炭酸。钟敲了十点。她收起报纸，叠得整整齐齐，放在门厅桌上，慢慢上楼去，眼睛望着吊低了一半的门厅煤气灯在擦亮的栏杆上淡淡的映光。楼梯又冷又空旷。一间间冷冷的房间和平台在她上方伸展上去，没入黑暗。她觉出一种奇异的轻快从身体里面升起来。那感觉如此古怪，她在楼梯上站住了一会儿。有一秒钟，生命仿佛在她体内停止了，楼梯仿佛从她脚下被抽走。……“我活着。”……仿佛有什么东西击中了她，径直穿过她那无形的身体，把它整个扫走，把她留在那里，没有身体地无声呐喊。我活着。……我活着。随后，咚的一声，她的心脏重新跳动起来，她的双脚载着她温暖、快活、富有弹性的身体，轻轻松松继续登上结实的楼梯。她特意在楼梯上站定，试了一两次想唤回那屏息的瞬间。每一次都回来了一点。“是我，*我*；这是*我*活着。”她喃喃地说，身子底下有一种电梯骤降般的感觉。可她的思绪岔开了她。它们急切地同她说话，宣称这种感觉她从前就有过。她极轻地推开卧室的门。房间里的空气告诉她，南茜和比迪都睡着了。她像踩着空气似的轻轻走向窗边五斗柜的梳妆台，在黑暗中扶着台沿站住。两件忘掉的旧事接连从她身上流过：一件是七岁生日那天，她独自在海滨别墅一间黑暗的小房间里醒来，突然对自己说，总有一天爸爸妈妈会死，而她还会在；在黑暗仿佛朝她涌来的古怪一刻之后，她觉得自己非常老，哭得好伤心；另一件是站在巴恩斯饭厅凸窗的窗弓里，看雨点从叶子上滴落、穿过阳光，对走进屋来的伊芙说：“你知道吗，伊芙，我觉得自己好像突然从一场梦里醒过来了。”卧室不再黑了。借着半掩的威尼斯式软百叶帘透进的街灯光，她看得清每样东西的轮廓。比迪叹了口气，翻了个身。米丽亚姆不耐烦地开始更衣就寝，没有点煤气灯。“这种感觉要是留不住，又有什么用？它什么也改变不了。下回我要让它留下。它也许能把我整个带走。我身上有一样东西，碰不着，也改不了。我。它要是再来。它要是每一次都更强。……也许它就这样一直增强，直到你死。”

## IV / IV

===Original===
Wheezing, cook had spread a plaster of dampened ashy cinders upon the basement schoolroom fire and gone bonily away across the oilcloth in her heelless boots. As the door closed Miriam’s eye went up from her book to the little slope of grass showing above the concrete wall of the area. The grass gleamed along the edge of a bank of mist. In the mist the area railings stood hard and solid against the edge of empty space. Several times she glanced at the rich green, feeling that neither “emerald,” “emerald velvet,” nor “velvety enamel” quite expressed it. She had not noticed that there was a mist shutting in and making brilliant the half-darkness of the room at breakfast-time, only feeling that for some reason it was a good day. “It’s fog﻿—there’s a sort of fog,” she said, glowing. The fog made the room with the strange brilliant brown light on the table, on the horsehair chairs, on the shabby length of brown and yellow oilcloth running out to the bay of the low window, seem to be rushing through space, alone. It was quite safe, going on its journey﻿—towards some great good.

The back door, just across the little basement hall, scrooped inwards across the oilcloth, jingling its little bell, and was banged to. The flounter-*crack* of a rain-cloak smartly shaken out was followed by a gentle scrabbling in a shoebox﻿—the earliest girl, peaceful and calm, a wonderful sort of girl, coming into the empty basement quietly getting off her things, with all the rabble of the school coming along the roads, behind. The jingling door was pushed open again just as her slippered feet ran upstairs. “Khoo﻿—what a filthy day!” said a vibrating hard mature voice. Miriam glanced at her timetable, history﻿—dictation﻿—geography﻿—sums﻿—writing﻿—and shrank to her utmost air of preoccupation lest either of the elder girls should look in.

Sounds increased in the little hall, loud abrupt voices, short rallying laughs, the stubbing and stamping of feet on the oilcloth. At the expected rattling of the handle of her own door she crouched over her book. The door opened and was quietly closed again. A small figure flung itself forward. Miriam was clutched by harsh serge-clad arms. As she moved, startled, firm cracked lips were pressed against her cheekbone. “Good morning, Burra,” she said, turning to put an arm round the child. She caught a glimpse of broad cheeks bulging firmly against a dark bush of short hair. Large fierce bloodshot eyes glared close to her own. “Hoo﻿—angel.” The little gasping body stiffened against her shoulder, pinning down her arm. The crimson face tried to reach her breast. “Have you changed your boots,” said Miriam coldly. “Hoo﻿—hoo.” The short hard fingers hurt her. “Go and get them off at once.” Head down Burra rushed at the door, colliding with the incoming figure of a neat little girl dressed in velvet-trimmed red merino, with a rose and white face and short gentle gold hair. She put a little pile of books on the table and stood still near to Miriam, with her hands behind her. They both looked down the room out of the window, with quiet unsmiling faces. “What have you been doing since Friday, Gertie?” Miriam said presently. “We went for a walk,” said Gertie in a neat liquid little voice, dimpling and faintly raising her eyebrows.

===Chinese===
厨娘呼哧呼哧喘着，在地下室教室的炉火上敷了一层浸湿的灰渣膏，拖着瘦骨嶙峋的脚步，趿着无跟鞋咔哒咔哒蹭过油地毡走了。门一关上，米丽亚姆的眼睛便从书上抬起来，望向地下室采光井水泥墙上方露出的那一小片斜坡草地。草地沿着雾堤的边缘闪闪发亮。雾中，采光井的栏杆硬邦邦、实沉沉地立在空虚的边缘上。她好几次瞥那浓郁的绿，觉得不管是“翡翠”、“翡翠天鹅绒”还是“天鹅绒般的珐琅”，都说不尽它。她原本没留意早餐时有一场雾四合拢来，把房间的半明半暗衬得亮晃晃的，只觉得不知怎的，今天是个好日子。“是雾——起了一层雾。”她喜滋滋地说。雾让这房间——桌上、马鬃椅上、一直铺到低窗凹间的那条破旧的棕黄油地毡上，那奇异的明亮的褐光——像是在独自穿越空间飞驰。它安安稳稳，行在旅途上——朝着某种大的美好。

后门就在地下室小门厅对面，吱嘎一声朝里蹭过油地毡，门上小铃叮当作响，随即砰地合上。雨篷斗篷利落一抖、啪地一声脆响之后，接着是鞋盒里窸窸窣窣的轻响——最早到的姑娘，安静又平和，一个顶好的姑娘，走进空荡荡的地下室，悄没声儿地脱下出门的衣裳，而全校那群乌合之众还远远落在后面的路上。她趿着拖鞋的脚步刚跑上楼，那扇叮当响的门又被推开了。“嚯——这鬼天儿！”一个发硬的、老成的、震得人耳朵嗡嗡响的嗓音说。米丽亚姆瞥了一眼课表，历史——听写——地理——算术——写字——随即缩成一副全神贯注到极点的模样，唯恐那两个大姑娘里哪一个探头进来。

小门厅里声响多起来，大声的、突兀的话音，短促的哄笑，油地毡上磕磕绊绊、跺跺踏踏的脚步。等到她自己门上把手那预期中的一阵哗啦响，她便伏到书上去。门开了，又轻轻合上。一个小小的身影直扑过来。米丽亚姆被两条粗哔叽衣袖的胳膊紧紧搂住。她吃惊地一动，一双干裂的嘴唇紧紧压上她的颧骨。“早上好，布拉。”她说着转过身去，用一条胳膊搂住孩子。她瞥见宽阔的两颊紧绷绷地鼓着，抵着一丛乌黑的短发。一双凶巴巴的充血大眼睛凑得极近地瞪着她的眼睛。“嚯——天使。”那小小的、喘成一团的身体抵着她的肩膀僵住，把她的胳膊压得死死的。通红的脸拼命要够到她的胸口。“你换靴子了没有？”米丽亚姆冷冷地说。“嚯——嚯。”那短短的硬手指掐得她生疼。“马上去脱掉。”布拉埋着头朝门口冲去，一头撞上进来的一个小巧齐整的姑娘身上——她穿一条丝绒镶边的红色细呢裙，玫瑰粉白的脸，短短的柔金色头发。她把一小摞书放在桌上，双手背在身后，静静地站到米丽亚姆近旁。两人都朝着房间那头的窗外望去，脸上安安静静，不带笑意。“从上礼拜五到现在你都做什么了，格蒂？”米丽亚姆过了一会儿说。“我们去散步了。”格蒂用一种利落清脆的小嗓音说，脸上漾起酒窝，淡淡地扬了扬眉毛。

===Original===
The eight little girls who made up the upper class of the junior school stood in a close row as near as possible to Miriam’s chair at the head of the table. They were silent and fresh and eagerly crowded, waiting for her to begin. She kept them silent for a few moments for the pleasure of having them there with her. She knew that Miss Perne, sitting in the window space with the youngest class drawn up in a half-circle for their Scripture lesson, was an approving presence, keeping her own little class at a level of quiet question and answer that made a background rather than a disturbance for the adventure of the elder girls. “Not too close together,” said Miriam at last, gathering herself with a deep breath; “throw back your shoulders and stand straight. Don’t lump down on your heels. Let your weight come on the ball of your feet. Are you all all right? Don’t poke your heads forward.” As the girls eagerly manoeuvred themselves, wilfully carrying out her instructions even to turning their heads to face the opposite wall, she caught most of the eyes in turn smiling their eager affectionate conspiracy, and restraining her desire to get up then and there and clasp the little figures one by one, began the lesson. Four of the girls, two square-built Quakeresses with straight brown frocks, deep slow voices and dreamy eyes, a white-faced, tawny-haired, thin child with an eager stammer, and a brilliant little Jewess knew the “principal facts and dates” of the reign of Edward I by rote backwards and forwards in response to any form of question. Burra hung her head and knew nothing. Beadie Featherwell, dreadfully tall, a head taller, with her twelve years, than the tallest child in the lower school, knew no more than Burra and stood staring at the wall and biting her lips. A stout child with open mouth and snoring breath answered with perfect exactitude from the book, but her answers bore no relationship to the questions, and Gertie could only pipe replies if the questions were so put as to contain part of the answer. The white-faced girl was beginning to gnaw her fingers by the time the questioning was at an end.

===Chinese===
初小高年级的八个小姑娘紧挨着站成一排，尽量贴近米丽亚姆桌首的椅子。她们不出声，鲜灵灵地挤作一团，等她开口。她让她们静了好一会儿，只为享受她们同她在一起的那份快乐。她知道珀恩小姐正坐在窗间，让最小的班级半围成圈上《圣经》课，她是一道嘉许的目光，把自己那班小小的问问答答维持在安安静静的水平上，对大姑娘们的历险来说，是背景，不是搅扰。“别挤得太紧。”米丽亚姆终于开口，深深吸一口气定住神；“肩膀向后展，站直。别塌在脚后跟上。让重量落在脚掌上。都站好了吗？头别往前伸。”姑娘们急切地调整着姿势，一个劲儿照她的指示办，连把头转过去面对对面那条墙这一条都照做，她挨个儿接住大多数眼睛里笑盈盈的、热切而亲热的密谋，按捺住当场起身把那些小小的身影一个一个搂进怀里的冲动，开始上课。姑娘里有四个——两个体格方正的贵格会姑娘，穿直筒筒的棕色裙，嗓音低沉缓慢，眼神朦朦胧胧；一个白脸黄褐头发、说话急急巴巴的瘦孩子；还有一个灵秀照人的犹太小姑娘——把爱德华一世一朝的“主要史实和日期”背得滚瓜烂熟，正着倒着都能答，随便怎么问都行。布拉垂着头，什么都不知道。比迪·费瑟韦尔个子高得吓人，以她十二岁的年纪，比低年级最高的孩子还高出一头，懂得却不比布拉多，站在那里瞪着墙，咬着嘴唇。一个胖乎乎、张着嘴、呼吸像打鼾的孩子照着课本答得分毫不差，可她的答案同问题毫无干系；格蒂呢，只有把问题问得自带一半答案，才尖着嗓子答得上来。问到快完的时候，那个白脸的姑娘已经啃起自己的手指头来。

===Original===
“Well now, what is the difficulty,” said Miriam, “of getting hold of the events of this queer little reign?” Everybody laughed and was silent again at once because Miriam’s voice went on, trying to interest both herself and the successful girls in inventing ways of remembering all the things that had to be “hooked on to the word Edward.” In less than ten minutes even the stout snoring girl could repeat the reign successfully, and for the remainder of their time they talked aimlessly.

The children standing at ease, saying whatever occurred to them, even the snoring girl secured from ridicule by Miriam’s consideration of whatever was offered. Their adventure took them away from their subject into what Miriam knew “clever” people would call “side issues.” “Nothing is a side issue,” she told herself passionately with her eyes on the green glare beyond the window. The breaking up of Miss Perne’s class left the whole of the lower school on her hands for the rest of the morning.

===Chinese===
“那么，难处到底在哪儿呢，”米丽亚姆说，“要把这段古怪小朝代的史事抓到手，难在哪儿？”大家都笑了，随即又立刻安静下来，因为米丽亚姆的声音还在往下讲，努力让自己也让那些答得上来的姑娘提起兴致，一起发明种种法子，把所有必须“挂在爱德华这个词上”的东西记住。不到十分钟，连那个打鼾的胖姑娘也能把这一朝顺顺当当背下来了，剩下的时间，她们就漫无边际地闲聊。

孩子们随意站着，想到什么说什么，连打鼾的姑娘也靠着米丽亚姆对每一句贡献的郑重看待，得了个免遭哄笑的保障。她们的历险带着她们离开了正题，闯进米丽亚姆知道“聪明人”会称之为“枝节”的地方去。“没有什么枝节。”她目光灼灼地望着窗外刺眼的绿，热烈地对自己说。珀恩小姐那边一下课，上午剩下的时光，整个低年级就都归她照管了。

---

===Original===
By half-past twelve she was sitting alone and exhausted with aching throat at her place at the head of the table.

“Khoo, *isn’t* it a filthy day!” Polly Allen, a short heavy girl with a sallow pitted face, thin ill-nourished hair and kind swiftly moving grey eyes, marched in out of the dark hall with flapping bootlaces. In the bay she sat down and began to lace up her boots. The laces flicked carelessly upon the linoleum as she threaded, profaning the little sanctuary of the window space. “Oh me bones, me poor old bones,” she muttered. “Eunice!” her hard mature voice vibrated through the room. “Eunice Dupont!”

“What’s the jolly row?” said a slow voice at the door. “Wot’s the bally shindy, beloved?”

“Like a really beautiful Cheshire cat,” Miriam repeated to herself, propped studiously on her elbows shrinking, and hoping that if she did not look round, Eunice’s carved brown curls, her gleaming slithering opaque oval eyes and her short upper lip, the strange evil carriage of her head, the wicked lines of her figure, would be withdrawn. “Cheshire, Cheshire,” she scolded inwardly, feeling the pain in her throat increase.

“Nothing. Wait for me. That’s all. Oh, my lungs, bones *and* et ceteras. It’s old age, I suppose, Uncle William.”

“Well, hurry your old age up, that’s all. I’m ready.”

“Well, don’t go away, you funny cuckoo, you can wait, can’t you?”

A party of girls straggled in one by one and drifted towards Polly in the window space.

===Chinese===
十二点半，她已经独自坐在桌首自己的位子上，嗓子发疼，筋疲力尽。

“嚯，这真是个*鬼*天儿！”波莉·艾伦——一个矮壮的姑娘，蜡黄的麻脸，稀稀拉拉营养不良的头发，一双和善的灰眼睛转得飞快——拖着甩来甩去的靴带，从昏暗的门厅里大步走了进来。她在窗凹间坐下，开始系靴子。她穿鞋带时长长的带子漫不经心地甩在亚麻油地毡上，亵渎了窗间这一小方圣所。“哎哟我的骨头哟，我可怜的老骨头哟。”她咕哝着。“尤妮斯！”她那发硬的老成嗓音震得满屋嗡嗡响。“尤妮斯·杜邦（Eunice Dupont）！”

“嚷嚷什么呢，这么欢？”门口一个慢吞吞的声音说。“闹什么鬼名堂呀，亲爱的？”

“活脱一只顶漂亮的柴郡猫。”米丽亚姆暗自一遍遍念叨，用胳膊肘撑着装作用功、身子却在缩，只盼着自己不回头，尤妮斯那刻出来似的棕色鬈发、那双亮晶晶滑溜溜不透光的椭圆眼睛、她的短上唇、她那颗脑袋古怪邪恶的姿态、她身形里那些刁恶的线条，就会自己退出去。“柴郡猫，柴郡猫。”她在心里呵斥，只觉嗓子越来越疼。

“没什么。等等我。就这些。哦，我的肺、我的骨头，*还有*五脏六腑。我估摸着，是上了年纪喽，威廉大叔（Uncle William）。”

“那你赶紧上完你的年纪吧，就这样。我可是好了。”

“哎，别走呀，你这滑稽的小傻瓜，你等等不成吗？”

一群姑娘三三两两地进来，一个接一个，都漂到窗间波莉那儿去。

===Original===
“It’s the parties I look forward to.”

“Oh, look at her tie!”

“My tie? Six-three at Crisp’s.”

The sounds of Polly’s bootlacing came to an end. She sat holding a court. “Doesn’t look forward to parties? She must be a funny cuckoo!”

“Dancing’s divine,” said a smooth deep smiling voice. “Reversing. Khoo! with a fella. Khooo!”

“You surprise me, Edie. You do indeed. Hoh. Shocking.”

“Shocking? Why? What do you mean, Poll?”

“Nothing. Nothing. Riang doo too.”

“*I* don’t think dancing’s shocking. How can it be? You’re barmy, my son.”

===Chinese===
“我就盼着聚会呢。”

“哦，瞧她的领带！”

“我的领带？克里斯普店（Crisp’s）买的，六先令三便士。”

波莉系靴带的声音告一段落。她坐着，一副临朝听政的架势。“居然不盼着聚会？她准是个滑稽的小傻瓜！”

“跳舞呀，妙极了。”一个圆润低沉、含着笑意的嗓音说。“倒着转。嚯！挽着个小伙子。嚯——！”

“你真让我吃惊，伊迪（Edie）。真让我吃惊。嚯。不像话。”

“不像话？为什么？你什么意思，波尔？”

“没什么。没什么。Riang doo too（怪腔怪调）。”

“*我*可不觉得跳舞不像话。这怎么可能呢？你脑子有毛病吧，老兄。”

===Original===
“Ever heard of Lottie Collins?”

“Ssh. Don’t be silly.”

“I don’t see what Lottie Collins has got to do with it. My mother thinks dancing’s all right. That’s good enough for me.”

“Well﻿—I’m not your mother.”

“Nor anyone else’s.”

“Khoo, *Mabel*.”

“Who wants to be anyone’s mother?”

“Not me. Ug. Beastly little brats.”

“Oh shut *up*. Oh you *do* make me tired.”

“Kids are jolly. A1. I hope I have lots.”

Surprised into amazement, Miriam looked up to consult the face of Jessie Wheeler, the last speaker﻿—a tall flat-figured girl with a strong squarish pale face, hollow cheeks, and firm colourless lips. Was it being a Baptist that made her have such an extraordinary idea? Miriam’s eyes sought refuge from the defiant beam of her sea-blue eyes in the shimmering cloud of her hair. The strangest hair in the school; negroid in its intensity of fuzziness, but saved by its fine mesh.

===Chinese===
“听说过落蒂·柯林斯（Lottie Collins）没有？”

“嘘。别犯傻。”

“我不明白落蒂·柯林斯跟这有什么相干。我妈妈认为跳舞没什么不好。这对我够用了。”

“这个嘛——我可不是你妈妈。”

“也不是任何人的妈妈。”

“嚯，*梅布尔*（Mabel）。”

“谁想当谁的妈妈呀？”

“我可不想。呃。讨厌的小崽子们。”

“哦闭*嘴*。哦你可*真*让我烦。”

“小孩儿可带劲了。顶呱呱。我盼着生一大群呢。”

米丽亚姆惊得目瞪口呆，抬起头去请教最后说话那人的脸——杰西·惠勒（Jessie Wheeler），一个高挑平胸的姑娘，苍白的大方脸盘，凹下去的两颊，抿得紧紧的无色嘴唇。难道是因为信浸信会，她才有这么异乎寻常的想法？米丽亚姆的眼睛从她那双海蓝色眼睛挑战的光芒里逃出来，躲进她头发那一团微光闪闪的云里。全校最古怪的头发；那绒毛般的鬈曲浓密得像黑人的头发，幸而发丝细密，才算救了回来。

===Original===
“Don’t you adore kiddies, Miss Henderson?”

“I think they’re rather nice,” said Miriam quickly, and returned to her book.

“I should jolly well think they were,” said Jessie fervently.

“Hope your husband’ll think so too, my dear,” said Polly, getting up.

“Oh, of course, I should only have them if the fellow wanted me to.”

“You haven’t got a fella yet, madam.”

“Of course not, cuckoo. But I shall.”

“Plenty of time to think about that.”

“Hoo. Fancy never having a fellow. I should go off my nut.”

===Chinese===
“您不喜欢小孩儿吗，亨德森小姐？”

“我觉得他们挺好的。”米丽亚姆赶紧说，又回到她的书上。

“那可不，本来就好得不得了。”杰西热切地说。

“但愿你将来的丈夫也这么想，亲爱的。”波莉说着站起身来。

“哦，那当然，除非那家伙想要，我才会生。”

“你还没结识小伙子呢，小姐。”

“当然还没有，小傻瓜。不过会有的。”

“有的是工夫慢慢想。”

“嚯。想想看，居然从来没处过小伙子。我会疯掉的。”

===Original===
When they had all disappeared Miriam opened the windows. There was still someone moving about in the hall, and as she stood in the instreaming current of damp air looking wearily at the concrete﻿—a girl came into the room. “Can I come in a minute?” she said, advancing to the window. “I want to speak to you,” she pursued when she reached the bay. She stood at Miriam’s side and looked out of the window. Half-turning, Miriam had recognized Grace Broom, one of the elder first-class girls who attended only for a few subjects. She was a dark short-necked girl with thick shoulders; a receding mouth and boldly drawn nose and chin gave her a look of shrewd elderliness. The heavy mass of hair above the broad sweep of her forehead, her heavy frame and flat-footed walk added to this appearance. She wore a high-waisted black serge pinafore dress with black crape vest and sleeves.

“Do you mind me speaking to you?” she said in a hot voice. Her black-fringed brown eyes were fixed on the garden railings where people passed by and Miriam never looked.

“No,” said Miriam shyly.

“You know why we’re in mourning?”

Miriam stood silent with beating heart, trying to cope with the increasing invasion.

“Our father’s dead.”

Hurriedly Miriam noted the superstitious tone in the voice.﻿ ﻿… This is a family that revels in plumes and hearses. She glanced at the stiff rather full crape sleeve nearest to her and sought about in her mind for help as she said with a blush, “Oh, I see.”

“We’ve just moved.”

===Chinese===
等她们全都消失了，米丽亚姆打开窗子。门厅里还有人在走动，她正站在涌入的潮湿气流里，疲惫地望着水泥墙——一个姑娘走进了房间。“我能进来待一分钟吗？”她说着朝窗子走来。“我想同您说话。”她走到窗凹间又续上一句。她站到米丽亚姆身边，望着窗外。米丽亚姆半转过身，认出了格蕾丝·布鲁姆（Grace Broom），一等班年纪较大的姑娘之一，只来上几门课。她是个肤色黝黑、脖子短粗的姑娘，肩膀厚实；后缩的嘴巴、笔锋大胆的鼻子和下巴，给她添了一副精明早老的相貌。宽阔光洁的前额上沉沉一堆头发，厚重的身架和平板板的脚步行走，又加重了这副印象。她穿一条高腰的黑哔叽围裙裙，黑绉纱的背心和袖子。

“您介意我同您说话吗？”她用一种发烫的声音说。她那黑睫毛衬着的棕色眼睛死死盯着花园的栏杆——行人打那儿经过，米丽亚姆却从来不看一眼。

“不介意。”米丽亚姆羞怯地说。

“您知道我们为什么服丧吗？”

米丽亚姆站着没出声，心怦怦跳，努力招架这步步进逼。

“我们的爸爸死了。”

米丽亚姆匆匆注意到那声音里迷信的调子。……这是个痴迷于丧服翎毛和灵车的家族。她瞥了一眼离自己最近的那只硬挺挺、鼓鼓囊囊的黑绉纱袖子，一面在心里四处求援，一面红着脸说：“哦，我知道了。”

“我们刚搬了家。”

===Original===
“Oh yes, I see,” said Miriam, glancing fearfully at the heavy scroll of profile and finding it expressive and confused.

“We’ve got a house about a quarter as big as where we used to live.”

Miriam found it impossible to respond to this confession and still tried desperately to sweep away the sense of the figure so solidly planted at her side.

“I’ve asked our aunt if we can ask you to come to tea with us.”

“Thank you very much,” said Miriam in one word.

“When could you come?”

“Oh, I’m afraid I couldn’t come. It would be impossible.”

“Oh no. You must come. I shall ask Aunt Lucy to write to Miss Perne.”

“I really couldn’t come. I shouldn’t be able to ask you back.”

“That doesn’t matter,” panted the relentless voice. “I’ve wanted to speak to you ever since you came.”

===Chinese===
“哦，是，我知道了。”米丽亚姆说，怯怯地瞥了一眼那沉重卷轴般的侧影，只觉它富于表情，又混乱不清。

“现在的房子，只有我们从前住的那么大的四分之一。”

米丽亚姆发现无从应答这番告白，还在拼命想把身边这具扎得牢牢的人影的感觉扫出去。

“我已经问过我们的姨妈，能不能请您来我们家喝茶。”

“非常感谢。”米丽亚姆用一句话应道。

“您什么时候能来？”

“哦，恐怕我来不了。那是不可能的。”

“哦不。您一定得来。我会请露西姨妈（Aunt Lucy）写信给珀恩小姐。”

“我真的来不了。我没法回请您。”

“那不要紧。”那不留情面的声音喘着说。“打您一来，我就一直想同您说话。”

===Original===
When next Miriam saw the black-robed Brooms and their aunt file past the transept where were the Wordsworth House sittings, she felt that to visit them might perhaps not be the ordeal she had not dared to picture. It would be strange. Those three heavy black-dressed women. Their small new house. She imagined them sitting at tea in a little room. Why was Grace so determined that she should sit there too? Grace had a life and a home and was real. She did not know that things were awful. Nor did Florrie Broom, nor the aunt. But yet they did not look like “social” people. They were a little different. Not worldly. Not pious either. Nor intellectual. What could they want with her? She had soon forgotten them and the congregation assumed its normal look. As the service went on the thoughts came that came every Sunday. An old woman with a girl at her side were the only people whose faces were within Miriam’s line of vision from her place at the wall end of the Wordsworth House pew. The people in front of them were not even in profile, and those behind were hidden from her by the angle of the transept wall. To her right she could just see rising above the heads in the rows of pews in front of her the far end of the chancel screen. The faces grouped in the transept on the opposite side of the church were a blur. The two figures sat or knelt or stood in a heavy silence. They neither sang nor prayed. Their faces remained unaltered during the whole service. To Miriam they were its most intimate part. During the sermon she rarely raised her eyes from the circle they filled for her as they sat thrown into relief by the great white pillar. Their faces were turned towards the chancel. They could see its high dim roof and distant altar, the light on the altar, flowers, shining metal, embroideries, the maze of the east window, the white choir. They showed no sign of seeing these things. The old woman’s heavy face with its heavy jawbone seemed to have been dead for years under its coffin-shaped black bonnet. Her large body was covered by a mantle of thickly ribbed black material trimmed with braid and bugles. That bright yellow colour meant liver. Whatever she had she was dying of it. People were always dying when they looked like that. But it was a bad way to die. The real way was the way of that lady trailing about over the Heath near Roehampton, dying by inches of an internal complaint, with her face looking fragile﻿—like the little alabaster *chapelle* in the nursery with a candle alight inside. She was going to die, walking about alone on the Heath in the afternoons. Her family going on as usual at home; the greengrocer calling. She knew that everybody was alone and that all the fuss and noise people made all day was a pretence.﻿ ﻿… What to *do*? To be walking about with a quiet face meeting death. Nothing could be so alone as that. The pain, and struggle, and darkness.﻿ ﻿… That was what the old woman feared. She did not think about death. She was afraid and sullen all the time. Stunned, sitting there with her cold common daughter. She had been common herself as a girl, but more noisy, and she had married and never thought about dying, and now she was dying and hating her cold daughter. The daughter, sitting there with her stiff slatey-blue coat and skirt, her indistinct hat tied with a thin harsh veil to her small flat head﻿—what a home with her in it all the time. She would never laugh. Her poor-looking cheeks were yellowish, her fringe dry, without gloss. She would move her mouth when she spoke, sideways with a snarling curl of one-half of the upper lip and have that resentful way of speaking that all North Londoners have, and the maddening North London accent. The old woman’s voice would be deep and hollow.﻿ ﻿… The girl moving heavily about the house wearing boots and stiff dresses and stiff stays showing their outline through her clothes. They would be bitter to their servant and would not trust her. What was the good of their being alive﻿ ﻿… a house and a water system and drains and cooking, and they would take all these things for granted and grumble and snarl﻿ ﻿… the gas meter man would call there. Did men like that resent calling at houses like that? No. They’d just say, “The ole party she sez to me.” How good they were, these men. Good and kind and cheerful. Someone ought to prevent the extravagance of keeping whole houses and fires going for women like that. They ought to be in an institution. But they never thought about that. They were satisfied with themselves. They were self-satisfied because they did not know what they were like.﻿ ﻿… *Why* should you have a house, and tradesmen calling?

===Chinese===
再次看到一身黑衣的布鲁姆一家和她们的姨妈，从华兹华斯楼（Wordsworth House）一家坐席所在的耳堂旁鱼贯走过时，米丽亚姆觉得，去登门拜访，也许并不像她不敢细想的那样是一场酷刑。会是很奇怪的。那三个一身黑衣的沉甸甸的女人。她们那幢小小的新房子。她想象她们在一间小屋里围桌喝茶。格蕾丝为什么这样一门心思要她也坐在那儿？格蕾丝有自己的生活、有自己的家，是真实存在的。她不知道世事有多可怕。弗洛莉·布鲁姆（Florrie Broom）不知道，姨妈也不知道。可她们又不像“交际场”上的人。她们有点不一样。不世故。也不虔诚。更不是读书人。她们要她去做什么呢？她很快就忘了她们，会众恢复了平常的模样。礼拜往下进行，每逢礼拜天都要来的那些念头也来了。一个老妇人和她身边的一个姑娘，是米丽亚姆从华兹华斯楼长椅靠墙一端的位子上仅有的看得见脸的人。她们前面的人连侧脸都看不见，后面的人又被耳堂墙的拐角挡住了。她右边，圣坛屏的远端从前排一列列长椅上方的人头间刚露出一点，勉强可以看见。对面耳堂里聚着的那些脸只是一片模糊。那两个人影或坐或跪或站，裹在沉沉的静默里。她们既不唱也不祷告。整个礼拜过程中，她们的脸纹丝不变。对米丽亚姆来说，她们是这礼拜里最贴心的部分。讲道的时候，她难得把目光从她们为她圈出的那片天地上挪开——她们坐着，被那根巨大的白柱子衬得轮廓分明。她们的脸朝着圣坛。她们看得见圣坛高而幽暗的穹顶和远远的祭坛，祭坛上的光、鲜花、锃亮的金属器皿、绣品，东窗的迷宫，白衣的唱诗班。她们没有一点看见这些东西的样子。老妇人那张下颌沉重的脸，在那顶棺材形的黑帽兜底下，仿佛已经死了好些年了。她那庞大的身躯裹在一件棱纹粗密的黑色料子斗篷里，镶着绦子和玻璃珠管。那种蜡黄的颜色，意味着肝。不管她得的是什么，她都要死在这上头了。人一显出那副模样，总是在死的路上。可这是一种坏死法。真正的死法，是那位太太的死法——在罗汉普顿（Roehampton）附近的荒原上拖拖沓沓地荡来荡去，害着一身内科的痼疾，一寸一寸地死去，脸看着脆弱——像育儿室里那座雪花石膏的小圣龛（chapelle），里面点着一支蜡烛。她是要死的，午后独自在荒原上走来走去地死。家里呢，一家人照旧过活；菜贩照常上门。她知道人人都是孤独的，人们整天整天的忙乱喧腾全是装出来的。……该*怎么办*呢？脸上安安静静地走来走去，迎向死亡。再没有什么比那更孤独的了。疼痛，挣扎，黑暗。……那才是老妇人怕的东西。她并不思索死亡。她只是一直害怕着、愠着。呆木木的，同她那冷漠平庸的女儿坐在那儿。她做姑娘时自己也是平庸的，只是更吵闹些，后来嫁了人，从没想过死，如今她在死，心里恨着她的冷女儿。那女儿呢，穿着硬挺的青灰蓝上衣和裙子坐在那儿，那顶模模糊糊的帽子用一条薄薄的糙面纱系在她小小的扁头上——她在家的日子里，那该是个什么家啊。她永远不会笑。她那寒碜的脸颊黄黄的，刘海干枯，没有光泽。她一开口嘴巴要歪着动，上嘴唇的半边龇出一个咆哮的卷儿，带着全体北伦敦人都会有的那种愤愤的腔调，还有那让人发疯的北伦敦口音。老妇人的嗓音想必又低又空。……姑娘穿着靴子、硬邦邦的裙子和硬邦邦的紧身褡，把轮廓都透在衣裳外面，沉沉地在屋里走动。她们待女仆会刻薄，也信不过她。她们活着有什么意思……一座房子，一套供水系统，下水道，一日三餐，她们会把这一切都视作理所当然，一面还嘟囔抱怨、龇牙咧嘴……查煤气表的人会上她们家门去。那种男人上门到那种人家去，心里会恼吗？不会。他们只会说：“那老婆子她跟我说。”这些男人多好啊。善良，和气，快活。真该有人来拦一拦，别再为那样的女人白白供养整座整座的房子和炉火。她们本该住进收容机构去。可她们从来不往这上头想。她们对自己心满意足。她们自鸣得意，因为她们不知道自己是个什么样子。……*为什么*要有房子，要有上门送货的商贩？

===Original===
“*Jehoiakin!*” The rush of indistinct expostulating sound coming from the pulpit was accompanied for a moment by reverberations of the one clearly bawled word. The sense of the large cold church, the great stone pillars, the long narrow windows faintly stained with yellowish green, the harsh North London congregation stirred and seemed to settle down more securely. She saw the form of the vicar in the light grey stone pulpit standing up short and neat against the cold grey stone wall, enveloped in fine soft folds, his small puckered hands beautifully cuffed, his plump crumpled little face, his small bald head fringed with little saffron-white curls, his pink pouched busy mouth. What was it all about? Pompous pottering, going on and on and on﻿—in the Old Testament. The whole church was in the Old Testament.﻿ ﻿… *Honour* thy father and thy mother. How horribly the words would echo through the great cold church. *Why* honour thy father and thy mother? What had they done that was so honourable? Everybody was dying in cold secret fear. Christ, the son of God, was part of it all, the same family﻿ ﻿… vindictive. Christmas and Easter, hard white cold flowers, no real explanation. “I came not to destroy but to fulfil.” The stagnant blood flushed in her face and tingled in her ears as the words occurred to her. Why didn’t everybody die at once and stop it all?

===Chinese===
“*约雅斤*（Jehoiakin）！”讲道坛上一阵含混的劝诫声奔涌而下，其中一个清清楚楚吼出来的词回荡了好一会儿。这座又大又冷的教堂，巨大的石柱，透着淡黄绿色的狭长窗子，粗厉的北伦敦会众，这些感觉震了一震，仿佛落得更稳了。她看见教区牧师的身影立在浅灰石的讲道坛里，衬着冷灰的石墙，矮矮整整的一截，裹在细软的褶裥里，他那双皱缩的小手罩着漂亮的袖口，他那胖乎乎皱巴巴的小脸，他那小小的秃头镶了一圈藏红白色的小鬈发，他那粉红色的、鼓囊囊的忙个不停的嘴。这都是说的什么呢？浮夸的磨叨，讲啊讲啊讲啊——全在《旧约》里。整座教堂都泡在《旧约》里。……当*孝敬*父母。这几个词回荡在又大又冷的教堂里，会多么可怕。*为什么*要孝敬父母？他们做过什么了不得的可敬之事？人人都在冷冷的、秘密的恐惧里死去。基督，上帝之子，也是这一切里的一环，同一家族……挟怨报复的。圣诞节，复活节，又硬又白又冷的花，没有一句真正的解释。“我来不是要废掉，乃是要成全。”那句话浮上来时，她淤滞的血涌上脸，耳朵里嗡嗡刺痛。为什么大家不一下子都死掉，把这一切了结了呢？

---

===Original===
Miss Haddie paused at the door of her room and wheeled suddenly round to face Miriam who had just reached the landing.

“You’ve not seen my little corner,” she tweedled breathlessly, throwing open her door.

Miriam went in. “Oh how nice,” she said fearfully, breathing in the freshness of a little square sun-filled muslin-draped, blue-papered room. Taking refuge at the white-skirted window, she found a narrow view of the park, greener than the one she knew. The wide yellow pathway going up through the cricket ground had shifted away to the right.

“It’s really a﻿—a﻿—a dressing-room from your room.”

“Oh,” said Miriam vivaciously.

“There’s a door, a﻿—a﻿—a door. I daresay you’ve noticed.”

“Oh! *That’s* the door in our cupboard!” The dim door behind the hanging garments led to nothing but to Miss Haddie’s room. She began unbuttoning her gloves.

Miss Haddie was hesitating near a cupboard, making little sounds.

“I suppose we must all make ourselves tidy now,” said Miriam.

===Chinese===
哈迪小姐在自己房门口停住，突然一转身，正对着刚走上楼梯平台的米丽亚姆。

“你还没见过我的小角落呢。”她喘着气，啭着嗓子说，一把推开了房门。

米丽亚姆走进去。“哦，多好。”她惶惶地说，呼吸着一间小方形房间里的清新空气——满屋子太阳，细纱帘幔，蓝壁纸。她躲到那扇白裙似的小窗前，望见公园窄窄的一线，比她认得的那片更绿。那条穿板球场而上的宽阔黄色小径，已经挪到右边去了。

“这其实是——是——是您房间的——一间梳妆室。”

“哦。”米丽亚姆伶俐地说。

“有一扇门，一——一——一扇门。我想您注意到过的。”

“哦！*那*就是我们衣橱里的那扇门！”挂在里面的衣裳后头那扇幽暗的门，哪儿也不通，只通哈迪小姐的房间。她动手解手套的扣子。

哈迪小姐在一只衣橱旁犹豫着，发出些细碎的声音。

“我想我们这都得把自己收拾齐整了。”米丽亚姆说。

===Original===
“I thought you didn’t look very happy in church this morning,” cluttered Miss Haddie rapidly.

Miriam felt heavy with anger. “Oh,” she said clumsily, “I had the most frightful headache.”

“Poor child. I thought ye didn’t look yerself.”

The window was shut. But the room was mysteriously fresh, far away from the school. A fly was hovering about the muslin window blind with little reedy loops of song. The oboe﻿ ﻿… in the quintet, thought Miriam suddenly. “I don’t know,” she said, listening. The flies sang like this at home. She had heard them without knowing it. She moved in her place by the window. The fly swept up to the ceiling, wavering on a deep note like a tiny gong.﻿ ﻿… Hot sunny refined lawns, roses in bowls on summerhouse tea-tables, refined voices far away from the Caledonian Road.

“Flies don’t *buzz*,” she said passionately. “They don’t *buzz*. Why do people say they buzz?” The pain pressing behind her temples slackened. In a moment it would be only a glow.

Miss Haddie stood with bent head, her face turning from side to side, with its sour hesitating smile, her large eyes darting their strange glances about the room.

“Won’t you sit down a minute? They haven’t sounded the first bell yet.” Miriam sat down on the one little white-painted, cane-seated chair near the dressing-table. “Eh﻿—eh,” said Miss Haddie, beginning to unfasten her veil. “She doesn’t approve of general conversation,” thought Miriam. “She’s a female. Oh well, she’ll have to see I’m not.”

===Chinese===
“我瞧着你早上在教堂里不大快活。”哈迪小姐急急絮絮地说。

米丽亚姆恼得发沉。“哦，”她笨笨地说，“我头疼得吓死人。”

“可怜的孩子。我当是你不像平常的你了呢。”

窗子关着。可这房间神秘地清新，离学校远远的。一只苍蝇绕着细纱窗帘打转，唱出一圈圈细苇管似的小调。双簧管……五重奏里的，米丽亚姆忽然想。“我不知道。”她一边听一边说。家里的苍蝇就是这么唱的。她一直听着它们，自己却不知道。她在窗边的位子上动了动。那苍蝇掠上天花板，颤在一个低音上，像一面小小的锣。……炎热的、阳光灿烂的、精致的草坪，凉亭茶桌上钵里的玫瑰，精致的声音，离卡利多尼亚路（Caledonian Road）远远的。

“苍蝇不*嗡嗡*叫。”她热烈地说。“它们不*嗡嗡*叫。人们为什么说它们嗡嗡叫？”抵在她太阳穴后面的疼痛松了松。再过一会儿，它就只剩一团热了。

哈迪小姐垂着头站着，脸转来转去，带着那酸溜溜、迟疑疑的笑，大眼睛把古怪的一瞥一瞥投向房间四处。

“坐一会儿好不好？头一遍铃还没打呢。”米丽亚姆在梳妆台旁那把唯一的小白漆藤座椅上坐下。“呃——呃。”哈迪小姐说着，动手解面纱。“她不赞成泛泛的闲谈。”米丽亚姆想。“她是个女人。哦，好吧，她迟早得看出我不是。”

===Original===
“What gave you yer headache?”

“Oh well, I don’t know. I suppose I was wondering what it was all about.”

“I don’t think I quite understand ye.”

“Well, I mean﻿—what that old gentleman was in such a state of mind about.”

“D’ye mean Mr. La Trobe!”

“Yes. Why do you laugh?”

“I don’t understand what ye mean.”

Miriam watched Miss Haddie’s thin fingers feeling for the pins in her black toque. “Of course not,” she thought, looking at the unveiled shrivelled cheek.﻿ ﻿… “thirty-five years of being a lady.”

“Oh well,” she sighed fiercely.

“What is it ye mean, my dear?”

===Chinese===
“你的头是怎么疼起来的？”

“哦，这个，我不知道。我大概是在琢磨，这一切到底是为了什么。”

“我怕是不大明白你的意思。”

“这个，我是说——那位老先生心心念念那么激动的，到底是什么。”

“你是说拉特罗布先生（Mr. La Trobe）！”

“是啊。您笑什么？”

“我不明白你什么意思。”

米丽亚姆看着哈迪小姐瘦瘦的手指在黑色小圆帽里摸发针。“当然不明白。”她想着，望了望那揭去面纱的、起了皱的脸颊。……“做了三十五年的淑女。”

“哦，算了。”她狠狠叹了口气。

“你到底是指什么，亲爱的？”

===Original===
—“couldn’t make head or tail of a thing the old dodderer said”—no “old boy,” no﻿—these phrases would not do for Miss Haddie.

“I couldn’t agree with *anything* he said.”

Miss Haddie sat down on the edge of the little white bed burying her face in her hands and smoothing them up and down with a wiping movement.

“One can always criticise a sermon,” she said reproachfully.

“Well, why not?”

“I mean to say ye *can*,” said Miss Haddie from behind her fingers, “but, but ye shouldn’t.”

“You can’t help it.”

“Oh yes, ye can. If ye listen in the right spirit,” gargled Miss Haddie hurriedly.

===Chinese===
——“那老糊涂讲的东西叫人一头雾水”——不行，“老伙计”，不行——这些话对哈迪小姐用不得。

“他说的，我*一样*也不能同意。”

哈迪小姐在小白床的床沿坐下，把脸埋进手里，双手一上一下抹着，像擦拭的样子。

“讲道嘛，人总是可以批评的。”她责备似的说。

“哦，为什么不该？”

“我是说，你是*可以*。”哈迪小姐从手指后面说，“可是，可是你不该。”

“忍不住嘛。”

“哦不，忍得住的。只要你怀着正当的心情去听。”哈迪小姐急急地含混说。

===Original===
“Oh, it isn’t only the sermon, it’s the whole thing,” said Miriam crimsoning.

“Ye mustn’t think about the speaker,” went on Miss Haddie in faint hurried rebuke. “That’s wrong. That sets people running from church to church. You must attend your own parish church in the right spirit, let the preacher be who﻿—who﻿—what he may.”

“Oh, but I think that’s positively *dangerous*,” said Miriam gravely. “It simply means leaving your mind open for whatever they choose to say. Like Rome.”

“Eh, no‑o‑o,” flared Miss Haddie dropping her hands, “nonsense. Not like Rome at all.”

“But it *is*. It’s giving up your conscience.”

“You’re very determined,” laughed Miss Haddie bitterly.

“I’m certainly not going to give my mind up to a parson for him to do what he likes with. That’s what it is. That’s what they do. I’ve seen it again and again. I’ve heard people talking about sermons,” finished Miriam with vivacious intentness.

===Chinese===
“哦，不光是讲道，是整个这一切。”米丽亚姆涨红了脸说。

“你不该去想讲道的人。”哈迪小姐继续说，带着隐隐的、急急的责备。“那是不对的。那样人们就会从一座教堂跑到另一座教堂。你必须怀着正当的心情上自己教区的教堂去，不管讲道的是谁——谁——是什么人。”

“哦，可我认为这简直*危险*。”米丽亚姆郑重地说。“这无非是敞开你的头脑，随他们爱说什么就灌什么。跟罗马一样。”

“呃，不——不——不，”哈迪小姐撂下手，火冒冒地说，“胡说。跟罗马根本不一样。”

“可就是一样。这是交出你的良心。”

“你真是主意大得很。”哈迪小姐苦笑着说。

“我是断断不会把我的头脑交给一位牧师，随他爱怎么摆布就怎么摆布的。就是这么回事。他们干的就是这个。我见了一遍又一遍。我还听人们谈论过讲道呢。”米丽亚姆伶俐而专注地收了尾。

===Original===
Miss Haddie sat very still with her hands once more pressed tightly against her face.

“Oh, my dear. This is a dreadful state of affairs. I’m afraid you’re all wrong. That’s not it at all. If you listen only for the good, the good will come to you.”

“But these men don’t know. How should they? They don’t agree amongst themselves.”

“Oh, my dear, that is a very wrong attitude. How long have ye felt like this?”

“Oh, all my life,” responded Miriam proudly.

“I’m very sorry, my dear.”

“Ever since I can remember. Always.”

There were ivory-backed brushes on the dressing-table. Miriam stared at them and let her eyes wander on to a framed picture of an agonised thorn-crowned head.

“Were you﻿—have ye﻿—eh﻿—have ye been confirmed?”

“Oh yes.”

“Did ye discuss any of yer difficulties with yer vicar?”

===Chinese===
哈迪小姐一动不动地坐着，双手又一次紧紧按在脸上。

“哦，亲爱的。这可糟透了。恐怕你全错了。完全不是那么回事。只要你只为着善去听，善就会来到你身边。”

“可这些人并不知道。他们怎么会知道呢？他们自己人中间都统一不了。”

“哦，亲爱的，这可是很不应该的态度。你这样有多久了？”

“哦，一辈子了。”米丽亚姆骄傲地应道。

“我很为你难过，亲爱的。”

“打记事起就这样。一直这样。”

梳妆台上有几把象牙背的刷子。米丽亚姆盯着它们，又让眼睛游到一张装框的画上——一颗戴着荆棘冠冕、痛楚万状的头。

“你是不是——你可——呃——你可行过坚信礼？”

“哦，行过。”

“你把你的疑惑同你的牧师谈过吗？”

===Original===
“Not I. I knew his mind too well. Had heard him preach for years. He would have run round my questions. He wasn’t capable of answering them. For instance, supposing I had asked him what I’ve *always* wanted to know. How can people, ordinary people, be expected to be like Christ, as they say, when they think Christ was supernatural? Of course, if he was supernatural it was easy enough for him to be as he was; if he was not supernatural, then there’s nothing in the whole thing.”

“My *dear* child! I’m dreadfully sorry ye feel like that. I’d no idea ye felt like that, poor child. I knew ye weren’t quite happy always; I mean I’ve thought ye weren’t quite happy in yer mind sometimes, but I’d no idea﻿—eh, eh, have ye ever consulted anybody﻿—anybody able to give ye advice?”

“There you are. That’s exactly the whole thing! *Who* can one consult? There isn’t anybody. The people who are qualified are the people who have the thing called faith, which means that they beg the whole question from the beginning.”

“Eh﻿—dear﻿—me﻿—Miriam﻿—child!”

===Chinese===
“我可不谈。他的头脑我知道得太清楚了。听他讲道听了那么多年。他会绕着我的问题兜圈子。他没本事回答。比方说，假如我问了他那个我*一直*想知道的问题。既然人们认为基督是超自然的，又怎么能指望人们——普普通通的人们——像他们说的那样变得像基督呢？当然啦，他要是超自然的，他那样做人自然轻而易举；他要不是超自然的，那这整个事儿就什么都不是了。”

“我*亲*爱的孩子！你这样想，我难过极了。我一点都不知道你是这样想的，可怜的孩子。我知道你一直不很快活；我是说，我有时觉得你心里不很快活，可我万万没想到——呃，呃，你可请教过什么人——什么能给你出出主意的人？”

“您瞧。这恰恰就是全部问题！*谁*能请教？根本没有这样的人。有资格的人，是有那种叫作信仰的东西的人，而这等于说，他们从一开头就把整个问题的答案先装了进去。”

“呃——天——哪——米丽亚姆——孩子！”

===Original===
“Well, I’m made that way. How can I help it if faith seems to me just an abnormal condition of the mind with fanaticism at one end and agnosticism at the other?”

“My dear, ye believe in God?”

“Well, you see, I see things like this. On one side a prime cause with a certain object unknown to me, bringing humanity into being; on the other side humanity, all more or less miserable, never having been consulted as to whether they wanted to come to life. If that is belief, a South Sea Islander could have it. But good people, people with faith, want me to believe that one day God sent a saviour to rescue the world from sin and that the world can never be grateful enough and must become as Christ. Well. If God made people he is responsible and ought to save them.”

“What do yer parents think about yer ideas?”

“They don’t know.”

“Ye’ve never mentioned yer trouble to them?”

“I did ask Pater once when we were coming home from the Stabat Mater that question I’ve told you about.”

“What did he say?”

“He couldn’t answer. We were just by the gate. He said he thought it was a remarkably reasonable dilemma. He laughed.”

“And ye’ve never had any discussion of these things with him?”

“No."

“Ye’re an independent young woman,” said Miss Haddie.

===Chinese===
“这个，我就是这么生就的。要是信仰在我看来只是心灵的一种反常状态——一头是狂热，一头是不可知论——我又有什么办法呢？”

“亲爱的，你总信上帝吧？”

“这个，您听我说，我是这么看事情的。一边是第一因（prime cause），抱着某种我不知道的目的，把人类造出来；另一边是人类，个个或多或少悲惨着，从来没有人征求过他们的意见，问他们愿不愿意来到人世。如果这就算信仰，南太平洋的岛民也能有。可是好人，有信仰的人，却要我相信：有一天上帝派来一位救主，把世界从罪恶里救出去，世界感恩戴德也感恩不尽，必须变得像基督一样。好啊。既然是上帝造的人，他就该负责，就该来救他们。”

“你的父母对你的这些想法怎么说？”

“他们不知道。”

“你的苦恼从来没对他们提起过？”

“有一次听完《圣母悼歌》（Stabat Mater）回家，我倒是问过爸爸我同您说过的那个问题。”

“他怎么说？”

“他答不上来。我们刚好走到大门口。他说他觉得那是个合情合理得不同寻常的两难。他笑了。”

“这些事你就再没同他讨论过？”

“没有。”

“你真是个有主见的年轻姑娘。”哈迪小姐说。

===Original===
Miriam looked up. Miss Haddie was sitting on the edge of her bed. A faint pink flush on her cheeks made her eyes look almost blue. She was no longer frowning. “I’m something new﻿—a kind of different world. She is wondering. I must stick to my guns,” mused Miriam.

“I’ll not ask ye,” said Miss Haddie quietly and cheerfully, “to expect any help from yer fellow creatures since ye’ve such a poor opinion of them. But ye’re not happy. Why not go straight to the source?”

Miriam waited. For a moment the sheen on Miss Haddie’s silk sleeves had distracted her by becoming as gentle and unchallenging as the light on her mother’s dresses when there were other people in the room. She had feared the leaping out of some emotional appeal. But Miss Haddie had a plan. Strange secret knowledge.

“I should like to ask ye a question.”

“Yes?”

“Well, I’ll put it in this way. While ye’ve watched the doings of yer fellow creatures ye’ve forgotten that the truth ye’re seeking is a﻿—a Person.”

Miriam pondered.

===Chinese===
米丽亚姆抬起头。哈迪小姐坐在床沿上。两颊一层淡淡的粉红，衬得她的眼睛几乎是蓝的。她不再皱眉了。“我是某种新东西——一个不一样的世界呢。她在纳罕。我可得守住阵地。”米丽亚姆寻思。

“我自然不会请你，”哈迪小姐安静而快活地说，“指望从你的同类那里得到什么帮助，既然你把他们看得那样不堪。可你并不快活。为什么不到源头那里去呢？”

米丽亚姆等着。有一忽儿，哈迪小姐绸袖上的光泽让她分了神——那光泽变得同屋里有旁人时她妈妈裙衫上的光一样温软，一样不带挑衅。她本来怕的是一通热辣辣的情感诉求跳出来。可哈迪小姐有一个打算。神秘的秘密知识。

“我想问你一个问题。”

“嗯？”

“这样吧，我就这么说。你只顾观察你同类的所作所为，却忘了你所寻找的真理，是——是一个‘人’（Person）。”

米丽亚姆沉吟。

===Original===
“That’s where ye ought to begin. And how about﻿—what﻿—what about﻿—I fancy ye’ve been neglecting the﻿—the means of grace.﻿ ﻿… I think ye have.” Miss Haddie rose and crossed the room to a little bookshelf at the head of her bed, talking happily on. “Upright as a dart,” commented Miriam mentally, waiting for the fulfilment of the promise of Miss Haddie’s cheerfulness. Against the straight lines of the wallpaper Miss Haddie showed as swaying slightly backwards from the waist as she moved.

The first bell rang and Miriam got up to go. Miss Haddie came forward with a small volume in her hands and held it out, standing close by her and keeping her own hold on the volume. “Ye’ll find no argument in it. Not but I think a few sound arguments would do ye good. Give it a try. Don’t be stiff-necked. Just read it and see.” The smooth soft leather slipped altogether into Miriam’s hands and she felt the passing contact of a cool small hand and noted a faint fine scent coming to her from Miss Haddie’s person.

In her own room she found that the soft binding of the book had rounded corners and nothing on the cover but a small plain gold cross in the right-hand corner. She feasted her eyes on it as she took off her things. When the second bell rang she glanced inside the cover. “Preparation for Holy Communion.” Hurriedly hiding it in her long drawer under a pile of linen, she ran to the door. Running back again she took it out and put it, together with her prayer book and hymn book, in the small top drawer.

===Chinese===
“那才是你该起步的地方。还有——怎么——怎么说呢——我猜你把那——那恩典的途径（means of grace）疏忽了。……我想你是疏忽了。”哈迪小姐站起身，穿过房间，朝床头一只小书架走去，一面快快活活说个不停。“笔直得像支镖。”米丽亚姆在心里评点，等着哈迪小姐这份快活兑现它的承诺。衬着壁纸的直线，哈迪小姐走动时显出从腰部微微后仰的摇曳。

第一遍铃响了，米丽亚姆起身要走。哈迪小姐双手捧着一册小书走上前来递给她，紧挨着她站着，自己仍握着那书不放。“你在里面找不到一句争辩。倒不是说，我想几句扎实的论证对你会有好处。试一试。别硬着颈项。读一读看。”那平滑柔软的皮面整个滑进米丽亚姆手里，她触到一只凉凉的小手一掠而过的相碰，还觉察到从哈迪小姐身上传来的一缕淡淡的幽香。

回到自己房里，她发现那本书的软面装订四角浑圆，封面干干净净，只有右下角一个小小的素金十字。她一面脱衣裳，一面饱看它。第二遍铃响时，她往封面里瞟了一眼。“Preparation for Holy Communion（《预备圣餐》）。”她慌忙把它藏进长抽屉里一堆亚麻布下面，跑到门口。又跑回来，把它取出来，连同她的祷告书和赞美诗集，一起放进最上面的小抽屉。

---

===Original===
The opportunity to use Miss Haddie’s book came with Nancie’s departure for a weekend visit. Beadie was in the deeps of her first sleep and the room seemed empty. The book lay open on her bed. She noted as she placed it there when she began preparing for bed that it was written by a bishop, a man she knew by name as being still alive. It struck her as extraordinary that a book should be printed and read while the author was alive, and she turned away with a feeling of shame from the idea of the bishop, still going about in his lawn sleeves and talking, while people read a book that he had written in his study. But it was very interesting to have the book to look at, because he probably knew about modern people with doubts and would not think about them as “infidels”—“an honest agnostic has my sympathy,” he might say, and it was possible he did not believe in eternal punishment. If he did he would not have had his book printed with rounded edges and that beautiful little cross.﻿ ﻿… *Line Upon Line* and the *Pilgrim’s Progress* were not meant for modern minds. Archbishop Whateley had a “chaste and eloquent wit” and was a “great gardener.” A witty archbishop fond of gardening was simply aggravating and silly.

===Chinese===
用上哈迪小姐那本书的机会，随着南茜动身去过周末而来到了。比迪头一觉正睡到酣处，房间显得空荡荡的。书摊开在她的床上。她先前预备上床时把它放在那儿，注意到它出自一位主教之手——一个她知道名字、知道还活着的人。一本书，作者还活着就印出来给人读，这让她觉得不可思议；她怀着一种羞愧，回避不去想那位主教的情形——还套着细麻布的袖子四处走动、谈话，人们却读着他在书房里写下的书。不过手里有这样一本书看看还是很有意思的，因为他多半了解现代人的种种怀疑，不会把他们当作“异教徒”来想——“一个诚实的不可知论者，有我的同情。”他或许会这么说；而且他可能不相信永罚。他要是相信，就不会把书印成圆角，配那个美丽的小十字了。……《一行接一行》（Line Upon Line）和《天路历程》（Pilgrim's Progress）都不是给现代头脑预备的。惠特利大主教（Archbishop Whateley）有一副“贞洁而雄辩的机智”，还是一位“了不起的园艺家”。一位机智幽默、爱好园艺的大主教，简直叫人窝火，又傻气。

===Original===
Restraining her desire to hurry, Miriam completed her toilet and at last knelt down in her dressing-gown. Its pinked neck-frill fell heavily against her face as she leant over the bed. Tucking it into her neck she clasped her outstretched hands, leaving the book within the circle of her arms. The attitude seemed a little lacking in respect for the beautifully printed gilt-edged pages. Flattening her entwined hands between herself and the edge of the bed, she read very slowly that just as for worldly communion men cleanse and deck their bodies so for attendance at the Holy Feast must there be a cleansing and decking of the spirit. She knelt upright, feeling herself grow very grave. The cold air of the bedroom flowed round her carrying conviction. Then that dreadful feeling at early service, kneeling like a lump in the pew, too late to begin to be good, the exhausted moments by the altar rail﻿—the challenging light on the shining brass rod, on the priest’s ring and the golden lining of the cup, the curious bite of the wine in the throat﻿—the sullen disappointed homecoming; all the strange failure was due to lack of preparation. She knelt for some moments, without thoughts, breathing in the cleansing air, sighing heavily at intervals. What she ought to do was clear. A certain time for preparation could be taken every night, kneeling up in bed with the gas out if Nancie were awake, and a specially long time on Saturday night. The decision took her back to her book. She read that no man can cleanse himself, but it is his part to examine his conscience and confess his sins with a prayer for cleansing grace.

===Chinese===
米丽亚姆按捺住想快些的急切，把睡前的一切收拾停当，最后穿着晨衣跪了下来。她朝床俯身时，晨衣领口锯齿边的褶边沉沉地垂在脸上。她把它掖进领口，交握起伸出的双手，让那本书留在臂弯圈起来的地方。这姿势对那些印得漂漂亮亮的天金边书页，似乎有欠恭敬。她把交缠的手压平在自己和床沿之间，非常缓慢地读到：正如赴世俗的宴席，人要洗净、装扮自己的身体；要出席那神圣的筵席，也必须有心灵的洗净与装扮。她直起身跪着，觉得自己变得非常郑重。卧室的冷空气绕着她流动，携着信念而来。接着是早礼拜上那可怕的感觉——像一块木头似的跪在长椅里，要开始学好已经太迟——祭坛栏杆边那些精疲力竭的时刻——锃亮的铜杆上、神父的戒指上、圣杯的金衬里上那咄咄逼人的光，酒滑过喉咙时那奇异的灼刺——回家路上那怏怏的失望；这一切古怪的失败，都是由于缺乏预备。她跪了一些时候，无所思念，呼吸着这洗净的空气，不时重重地叹一口气。她该怎么做，已经清楚了。每天夜里可以抽出一段预备的时间，要是南茜醒着，就灭掉煤气灯在床上跪着，礼拜六夜里再格外多跪一会儿。这个决定把她带回书上。她读到：没有人能洗净自己，但人所能做的，是省察自己的良心，认自己的罪，祈求洗净的恩典。

===Original===
The list of questions for self-examination as to sins past and present in thought, word, and deed brought back the sense of her body with its load of well-known memories. Could they be got rid of? She could cast them off, feel them sliding away like Christian’s Burden. But was that all? Was it being reconciled with your brother to throw off ill-feeling without letting him know and telling him you were sorry for unkind deeds and words? Those you met would find out the change; but all the others﻿—those you had offended from your youth up﻿—all your family? Write to them. A sense of a checking of the tide that had seemed to flow through her fingertips came with this suggestion, and Miriam knelt heavily on the hard floor, feeling the weight of her well-known body. The wallpaper attracted her attention and the honeycomb pattern of the thick fringed white counterpane. She shut the little book and rose from her knees. Moving quickly about the room, she turned at random to her washhand basin and vigorously rewashed her hands in its soapy water. The Englishman, she reflected as she wasted the soap, puts a dirty shirt on a clean body, and the Frenchman a clean shirt on a dirty body.

===Chinese===
那张自省的清单，一条条问着往昔与如今在念头上、言语上、行为上的罪，把她身体的感觉连同它驮着的满满一堆熟识的记忆带了回来。能甩掉它们吗？她能把它们抖落，觉出它们像基督徒的“重负”（Christian's Burden）一样滑落下去。可这就完了？不同你兄弟照面，不同他说你为那些狠心的事、狠心的话抱歉，只把恶意一扔就算同他和好了吗？遇见你的人自会看出这变化；可所有其他人——那些你从幼年就得罪下的人——你全家呢？写信去。仿佛有一道潮水，先前直流过她的指尖，这个提议一来，潮水便受了阻，米丽亚姆沉沉地跪在硬地板上，觉出她那熟识的身体熟识的分量。壁纸引起了她的注意，还有厚厚的白流苏床罩上的蜂窝纹。她合上小书，从跪着的地方站起身。在房间里快步走来走去，她随脚转到洗脸架前，就着盆里的肥皂水重新狠狠洗了手。英国人呢，她一边糟蹋着肥皂一边想，把脏衬衫穿在干净的身体上；法国人，把干净衬衫穿在脏的身体上。

## V / V

===Original===
Miriam felt very proud of tall Miss Perne when she met her in the hall at the beginning of her second term. Miss Perne had kissed her and held one of her hands in two small welcoming ones, talking in a gleeful voice. “Well, my dear,” she said at the end of a little pause, “you’ll have a clear evening. The gels do not return until tomorrow, so you’ll be able to unpack and settle yerself in comfortably. Come and sit with us when ye’ve done. We’ll have supper in the sitting-room. M’yes.” Smiling and laughing she turned eagerly away. “Of course, Miss Perne,” said Miriam in a loud wavering voice, arresting her, “I enjoyed my holidays; but I want to tell you how glad I am to be back here.”

“Yes, yes,” said Miss Perne hilariously, “we’re all glad.”

There was a little break in her voice, and Miriam saw that she would have once more taken her in her arms.

“I like being here,” she said hoarsely, looking down, and supported herself by putting two trembling fingers on the hall table. She was holding back from the gnawing of the despair that had made her sick with pain when she heard once more the jingle-jingle, plock-plock of the North London trams. This strong feeling of pride in Miss Perne was beating it down. “I’m very glad, my dear,” responded Miss Perne in a quivering gleeful falsetto. “If you can’t have what you like you must like what you have,” said Miriam over and over to herself as she went with heavy feet up the four flights of stairs.

===Chinese===
第二个学期开学那天，米丽亚姆在门厅里迎面碰见高个子的珀恩小姐，心里涌起一股强烈的自豪。珀恩小姐吻了她，用两只小小的、表示欢迎的手握住她的一只手，喜滋滋地说着话。“这个，亲爱的，”她小小地顿了一顿，说，“你今晚清净。姑娘们明天才回来，你可以好好开箱，把自己安顿舒坦。弄完了就来同我们坐坐。我们在起居室吃晚饭。唔——是的。”她笑着、乐着，急煎煎地转身要走。“那当然，珀恩小姐，”米丽亚姆用响亮而发颤的声音留住她，“我假期过得很好；可我想告诉您，回到这儿我有多高兴。”

“是啊，是啊，”珀恩小姐欢天喜地地说，“我们都高兴。”

她的嗓音里有一小点哽咽，米丽亚姆看出来，她恨不得再一次把她搂进怀里。

“我喜欢在这儿。”她哑着嗓子说，眼睛朝下，用两根发颤的手指撑在门厅桌上稳住自己。她正在抵挡一股绝望的啃噬——重新听见北伦敦电车叮叮当当、橐橐的声响时，那绝望曾让她疼得发呕。对珀恩小姐这股强烈的自豪感，正把它压下去。“我太高兴了，亲爱的。”珀恩小姐用发颤的、喜洋洋的假嗓应道。“喜欢的得不到，就得喜欢得到的。”米丽亚姆一遍又一遍对自己说，拖着沉重的脚步爬上四段楼梯。

---

===Original===
A candle was already burning in the empty bedroom. “I’m back. I’m back. It’s all over,” she gasped as she shut the door. “And a jolly good thing too. This is my place. I can keep myself here and cost nothing and not interfere with anybody. It’s just as if I’d never been away. It’ll always be like that now. Short holidays, gone in a minute, and then the long term. Getting out of touch with everything, things happening, knowing nothing about them, going home like a visitor, and people talking to you about things that are only theirs, now and not wanting to hear about yours﻿ ﻿… not about the little real everyday things that give you an idea of anything but only the startling things that are not important. You have to think of them though to make people interested﻿—awful, awful, awful, really only putting people further away afterwards when you’ve told the thing and their interest dies down and you can’t think of anything else to say. ‘Miss Perne’s hair is *perfectly* black﻿—as black as coal, and she’s the eldest, just *fancy*.’ Then everybody looks up. ‘My room’s downstairs, the room where I teach, is in the basement. Directly breakfast is over﻿—’

===Chinese===
空卧室里已经点起了一支蜡烛。“我回来了。我回来了。都结束了。”她关上门，喘着气说。“而且是大好事。这儿是我的地方。我在这儿养活自己，不花谁的钱，也不打扰谁。就好像我从来没离开过。往后永远都会这样了。短短的假期，一眨眼就完，然后是漫长的学期。同一切脱了节，事情在发生，你一概不知，回家像个客人，人们同你谈的又只是他们自己的事，如今还不想听你讲你的……不讲那些让你对任何东西有点概念的、真实的日常小事，只讲那些耸人听闻、其实无关紧要的事。可你还是得想出几件来，好让人家感兴趣——可怕，可怕，可怕，其实讲完之后只会把人推得更远，他们的兴头一冷，你又想不出别的可说了。“珀恩小姐的头发*乌黑*乌黑的——黑得像煤，她是大姐，你说*怪不怪*。”于是人人都抬起头来看。“我的房间在楼下，我教课的那间屋在地下室。早早饭一吃完——”

===Original===
“ ‘Basement? What a pity! Basement rooms are awfully bad,’ and by the time you have stopped them exclaiming and are just going to begin, you see that they are fidgetting and thinking about something else.”﻿ ﻿… Eve had listened a little; because she wanted to tell everything about her own place and had agreed that nobody really wanted to hear the details.﻿ ﻿… The landscapes from the windows of the big country house, all like pictures by Leader, the stables and laundry, a “laundry-maid” who was sixty-five, the eldest pupil with seven muslin dresses in the summer and being scolded because she swelled out after two helpings of meat and two of pie and cream, and the youngest almost square in her little covert coat and with a square face and large blue eyes and the puppies who went out in a boat in Weston-super-Mare and were seasick.﻿ ﻿… Eve did not seem to mind the family being common. Eve was changing. “They are so jolly and strong. They enjoy life. They’re like other people.”﻿ ﻿… “D’you think that’s jolly? Would you like to be like that﻿—like other people?” “Rather. I mean to be.” “Do?” “Of course it can’t be done all at once. But it’s good for me to be there. It’s awfully jolly to be in a house with no worry about money and plenty of jolly food. Mrs. Green is so strong and clever. She can do anything. She’s good for me, she keeps me going.” “Would you like to be like her?” “Of course. They’re all so jolly﻿—even when they’re old. Her sister’s forty and she’s still pretty; not given up hope a bit.” “*Eve!*”

===Chinese===
“‘地下室？多可惜！地下室的房子糟糕透了。’等你止住她们的惊叹，正要开始讲，你却发现她们在坐立不安，心里想着别的。”……伊芙倒是听了一小会儿；因为她急着要讲她自己那边的一切，也同意说谁也不会真想听那些细节。……乡间大宅窗外一幅一幅的风景，全都像利德（Leader）的画，马厩和洗衣房，一位六十五岁的“洗衣女仆”，大弟子夏天有七条细纱裙，因为两客肉、两客馅饼加奶油下肚肚子鼓起来挨了骂，最小的那个穿着小小的骑装外套几乎成了个方块，方脸盘、蓝大眼，还有几只小狗，在滨海韦斯顿（Weston-super-Mare）坐船出海，晕了船。……伊芙似乎并不介意那家人平庸。伊芙在变。“他们多快活多壮实啊。他们会享受生活。他们跟别人一样。”……“你觉得那叫快活？你想变成那样吗——跟大家一样？”“想极了。我打定主意要这样。”“是吗？”“当然不可能一下子办到。可我待在那儿有好处。住在一座不用为钱发愁、到处是好吃食的宅子里，太痛快了。格林太太（Mrs. Green）那么壮实那么能干。她什么都会。她对我好，她让我撑得住。”“你想变成她那样？”“当然。他们一家人都那么快活——连老了都那么快活。她姐姐四十了，还挺漂亮；一点儿都没死心。”“*伊芙*！”

===Original===
Eve had listened; but not agreed about the teaching, about making the girls see how easy it was to get hold of the things and then letting them talk about other things. “I see how you do it, and I see why the girls obey you, of course.” Funny. Eve thought it was hard and inhuman. That’s what she really thought.

Two newly purchased lengths of spotted net veiling were lying at the top of her lightly packed trunk partly folded in uncrumpled tissue paper. She took the crisp dye-scented net very gently into her hands, getting, sitting alone on the floor by her trunk, the full satisfaction that had failed her in the shop with Harriett’s surprise at her sudden desire flowing over the counter and infecting the charm of baskets full of cheap stockings and common bright-bordered handkerchiefs some of which had borders so narrow and faint as really hardly to show when they were scrumpled up. “Veiling, moddom? Yes, moddom,” the assistant had retorted when she had asked for a veil. “Wot on *earth* fower?”﻿ ﻿… Without answering Harriett she had bought two. There was no need to have bought two. One could go back in the trunk as a store. They would be the beginning of gradually getting a “suitable outfit,” “things convenient for you.” She got up to put a veil in the little top drawer very carefully; trying it across her face first. It almost obliterated her features in the dim candlelight. It would be the greatest comfort on winter walks, warm and like a rampart. “You’ve no idea how warm it keeps you,” she could say if anybody said anything. She arranged her clothes very slowly and exactly in her half of the chest of drawers. “My appointments ought to be an influence in the room﻿—until all my things are perfectly refined I shan’t be able to influence the girls as I ought. I must begin it from now. At the end of the term I shall be stronger. From strength to strength.” She wished she could go to bed at once and prepare for tomorrow lying alone in the dark with the trams going up and down outside as they would do night by night for the rest of her life.

===Chinese===
伊芙听了；可在教书这件事上不以为然——让姑娘们看到掌握这些东西多么容易，然后随她们去谈别的。“我看得出你是怎么弄的，当然，我也看得出姑娘们为什么服你。”有意思。伊芙觉得那套又生硬又不近人情。她心里真是这么想的。

两条新买的有小点子的网眼面纱，半裹在没起皱的薄棉纸里，放在她那只装得松松的箱子的最上面。她把那脆生生、带着染料气味的网纱极轻地捧进手里，独自坐在箱子旁的地板上，这才得到充分的满足——在铺子里时这满足曾经落了空：哈丽特对她突发的欲望一脸吃惊，那份吃惊漫过柜台，沾染了一篮篮廉价长袜和花哨滚边手帕的魅力，有几条手帕的边窄得、淡得，揉成一团时几乎都看不出来。“面纱料子，太太？有的，太太。”她要买面纱时，店员这么应着。“究*竟*要那个干啥呀？”……她没理哈丽特，买了两条。其实用不着买两条。一条可以放回箱子里存着。它们将是一个开头，慢慢置起一套“合适的行头”，“对你方便的东西”。她起身把一条面纱小心翼翼放进最上面的小抽屉；先在脸上比了比。昏暗的烛光里，它几乎把她的五官整个抹掉了。冬天散步时它将是天大的安慰，又暖和，又像一道壁垒。“您想象不到它多保暖。”要是有人说三道四，她就可以这么说。她把衣裳慢慢地、一丝不苟地归置进五斗橱属于她的那一半。“我的什物应该成为这房间里的感化力——我所有的东西不做到完全雅致，我就没法按该有的样子去感化姑娘们。必须从现在就开始。到学期末我就会更强壮。从力量到力量。”她真想立刻上床，独自躺在黑暗里为明天养神，任电车在外面上上下下地驶过——往后余生，它们夜夜都将这样驶过。

---

===Original===
The nine o’clock post brought a letter from Harriett. Miriam carried it upstairs after supper. Placing it unopened on a chair by the head of her bed under the gas bracket she tried to put away the warm dizzy feeling it brought her in an elaborate toilet that included the placing in readiness of everything she would need for the morning. When all was complete she was filled with a peace that promised to remain indefinitely as long as everything she had to do should be carried out with unhurried exactitude. It could be made to become the atmosphere of her life. It would come nearer and nearer and she would live more and more richly into it until she had grown like those women who were called blessed.﻿ ﻿… She looked about her. The plain room gave her encouragement. It became the scene of adventure. She tiptoed about it in her nightgown. All the world would come to her there. Flora knew. Flora was the same, sweeping the floors and going to bed in an ugly room with two other servants; but she was in it alone sometimes and knew.﻿ ﻿…

“One verse tonight will be enough.” Opening her Bible at random she read, “And not only so, but we glory in tribulations also: knowing that tribulation worketh patience.” Eagerly closing the volume she knelt down smiling. “Oh do let tribulation work patience in me,” she murmured, blushing, and got up staring gladly at the wall behind her bed. Shaking her pillow lengthwise against the ironwork head of the bed, she established herself with the bedclothes neatly arranged, sitting up to read Harriett’s letter before turning out the gas:

===Chinese===
九点钟的邮班送来哈丽特的一封信。晚饭后米丽亚姆把它带上楼来。她把它原封不动放在床头的椅子上、煤气灯架底下，用一套一丝不苟的睡前梳洗来安放它带来的那阵温暖眩晕，包括把明早要用的一切一一预备停当。一切齐备之后，她心里盛满了一种安宁，这安宁许诺无限期地留驻，只要她要做的一切都从容不迫、分毫不差地完成。可以把它经营成她一生的空气。它会越靠越近，她会在其中越活越丰厚，直到她长成那些被人称作有福的女人。……她环顾四周。素净的房间给她鼓劲。它成了历险的舞台。她穿着睡衣踮着脚在屋里走。整个世界都会到这儿来找她。弗洛拉（Flora）就懂。弗洛拉也是这样，扫着地板，同另外两个仆人睡在一间难看的屋里；可她有时独自置身其中，她懂。……

“今晚一节就够了。”她随手翻开《圣经》，读到：“不但如此，就是在患难中也是欢欢喜喜的，因为知道患难生忍耐。”她急切地合上书，微笑着跪下。“哦，就让患难在我身上生出忍耐来吧。”她红着脸喃喃说，站起来，欢欢喜喜地望着床后的墙。她把枕头顺着床的铁架床头拍了拍，被褥铺得整整齐齐地安顿下来，坐起身，在熄掉煤气灯之前读哈丽特的信：

===Original===
“Toosday morning﻿—You’ve not gone yet, old tooral-ooral, but I’m writing this because I know you’ll feel blue this evening, to tell you not to. Becos, it’s *no* time to Easter and becos here’s a great piece of news. The last of the Neville Subscription Dances comes in the Easter holidays and *you’re to come*. D’ye *’ear*, Liza? Gerald says if you can’t stump up he’s going to get you a ticket, and anyhow you’ve got to come. You’ll enjoy it just as much as you did the first and probably more, because most of the same people will be there. So Goodni’. Mind the lamppost. Harry. P.S.﻿—Heaps of love, old silly. You’re just the same. It’s no bally good pretending you’re not.”

Miriam felt her heart writhe in her breast. “Get thee behind me, Harry,” she said, pushing the letter under the pillow and kneeling up to turn out the gas. When she lay down again her mind was rushing on by itself.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
“礼拜二（Toosday）早上——你还没走吧，老突啦乌啦（tooral-ooral），我提前写这封，因为我知道你今晚准发蔫，特此通知不许。因为，离复活节还*早*着呢，还因为，这儿有一条天大的新闻。内维尔订户舞会（Neville Subscription Dances）的最后一场就落在复活节假期，*你可得来*。听*见*没，莉扎（Liza）？杰拉尔德说了，你要是凑不出份子钱，他就给你出票钱，反正你横竖得来。你保管跟头一回一样痛快，多半还要更痛快，因为到场的多半还是同一拨人。那就晚安喽（Goodni'）。留神路灯杆。哈里。又及——满满的爱，老傻帽。你还是老样子。硬装不是也白搭。”

米丽亚姆觉得心在胸口里扭绞。“退到我后边去吧，哈里。”她说着，把信塞到枕头底下，跪起来关掉煤气灯。重新躺下时，她的思绪已经自己在往前奔了。……

---

===Original===
Harry doesn’t realise a bit how short holidays are. Easter﻿—nothing. Just one dance and never seeing the people again. I was right just now. I was on the right track then. I must get back to that. It’s no good giving way right or left; I must make a beginning of my own life.﻿ ﻿… I wish I had been called “Patience” and had thin features.﻿ ﻿… Adam Street, Adelphi.﻿ ﻿… “Now do you want to be dancing out there with one of those young fellows, my dear girl﻿—No? That’s a very good thing for me. I’m an old buffer who can’t manage more than every other dance or so. But if you do me the honour of sitting here while those young barbarians romp their Lancers?﻿ ﻿… Ah, that is excellent﻿—I want you to talk to me. You needn’t mind me. Hey? What? I’ve known that young would-be brother-in-law of yours for many years and this evening I’ve been watching your face. Do you mind that, dear girl, that I’ve watched your face? In all homage. I’m a staunch worshipper of womanhood. I’ve seen rough life as well as suave. I’m an old gold-digger﻿—Ustralia took many years of my life; but it never robbed me of my homage for women.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
哈里一点儿也没意识到假期有多短。复活节——算什么。不过一场舞会，跳完那些人再也见不着。我刚才是对的。我当时走对了路子。我必须回到那上面去。左也让步右也让步没有用处；我必须开始一种我自己的生活。……真希望我就叫“忍耐”，长一张清瘦的脸。……亚当街（Adam Street），阿德尔菲（Adelphi）。……“我说，你不想出去同那些小伙子里的哪一位跳一支吗，亲爱的姑娘——不想？那对我可是件大好事。我是个上了年纪的老家伙，隔一支舞才跳得动一支。不过你可肯赏光坐在这儿，让那些年轻的野蛮人撒欢去跳他们的方阵舞？……啊，这就再好不过了——我要你同我说话。别拘束。嗯？什么？你那位一心想当姐夫的年轻人，我认识好些年了，今晚我一直看着你的脸。你在意吗，亲爱的姑娘，我看了你的脸？怀着全然的敬意。我是个坚定的女性崇拜者。粗野的日子我见过，文雅的日子我也见过。我是个老淘金客——“乌斯特拉利亚”（Ustralia，他把 Australia 头一个音吞了）占去了我多年的光阴；可它从没夺走过我对女性的敬意。……

===Original===
“That’s a mystery to me. How you’ve allowed your young sister to overhaul you. Perhaps you have a Corydon hidden away somewhere﻿—or don’t think favourably of the bonds of matrimony? Is that it?

“You are not one to be easily happy. But that is no reason why you should say you pity anyone undertaking to pass through life at your side. Don’t let your thoughts and ideas allow you to miss happiness. Women are made to find and dispense happiness. Even intense women like yourself. But you won’t find it an easy matter to discover your mate.

“Have you ever thought of committing your ideas to paper? There’s a book called *The Confessions of a Woman*. It had a great sale and its composition occupied the authoress for only six weeks. You could write in your holidays.

“Think over what I’ve told you, my dear, dear girl. And don’t forget old Bob Greville’s address. You’re eighteen. He’s only eight; eight Adam Street. The old Adam. Waiting to hear from the new Eve﻿—whenever she’s unhappy.”

===Chinese===
“这我可摸不透。你怎么会让你妹妹后来居上。要么，你在什么地方藏着一位科里东（Corydon，牧歌里的情郎）——要么，就是对婚姻的枷锁不甚看好？是这样吗？

“你不是个轻易快活起来的人。可这不能成为理由，让你说什么谁若答应陪你走完这一世，你只有可怜他。别让你的那些想法、那些念头耽误了你的幸福。女人生来就是要寻得幸福、散播幸福的。像你这样热烈的女人也不例外。不过，要找到你的良人，可没那么容易。

“你想过把你的想法写成文字吗？有本书叫《一个女人的忏悔》（The Confessions of a Woman）。销路好得很，女作者统共只花了六个星期就写成了。你假期里就可以写嘛。

“好好想想我说的话，我亲爱的、亲爱的姑娘。别忘了老鲍勃·格雷维尔（old Bob Greville）的地址。你十八岁。他才八岁；亚当街八号。老亚当。等着新夏娃来信——不管她什么时候不快活。”

===Original===
He would be there again, old flatterer, with his steely blue eyes and that strong little Dr. Conelly﻿—Conelly who held you like a vice and swung you round and kept putting you back from him to say things. “If only you knew the refreshment it is to dance with a girl who can talk sense and doesn’t *giggle*.﻿ ﻿… Yes yes yes, women are *physically* incapable of keeping a secret.﻿ ﻿… Meredith, he’s the man. He understands woman as no other writer﻿—” And the little dark man﻿—De Vigne﻿—who danced like a snake.﻿ ﻿… Tired? Divinely drowsy? That’s what I like. Don’t talk. Let yourself go. Little snail, Harriett called him. And that giant, Conelly’s friend, whirling you round the room like a gust, with his eyes fixed far away in the distance and dropping you with the chaperones at the end of the dance. If *he* had suddenly said “Let yourself go”﻿ ﻿… He too would have become a snail. God has made life ugly.

Dear Mr. Greville, dear *Bob*. Do you know anything about a writer called Meredith? If you have one of his books I should like to read it. No. Dear Bob, I’m simply wretched. I want to talk to you.

===Chinese===
他还会在的，那个老马屁精，带着他那双钢蓝色的眼睛，还有那个结结实实的小个子康纳利医生（Dr. Conelly）——康纳利攥住你像一把老虎钳，带你飞旋，还一次一次把你从怀里推远，好对着你说话。“你哪里知道，同一个说话有见地、又不*咯咯*傻笑的姑娘跳舞，是多么提神的事。……是的是的是的，女人在*体质上*就没法保守秘密。……梅瑞狄斯（Meredith），那才是人物。他懂女人，别的作家谁也比不上——”还有那个黑黑的小个子——德·维涅（De Vigne）——跳起舞来像条蛇。……累了？困得飘飘欲仙？我就喜欢这样。别说话。由着它去。小蜗牛——哈丽特这么叫他。还有那个大个子，康纳利的朋友，像一阵疾风似的带你满屋转，眼睛直直地定在极远处，一曲终了把你撂在陪护太太们中间。要是*他*突然说一声“由着它去”……他也会变成一只蜗牛的。上帝把生活造得真丑。

亲爱的格雷维尔先生，亲爱的*鲍勃*。您可知道一位叫梅瑞狄斯的作家？要是您有他的书，我想读一读。不。亲爱的鲍勃，我简直糟透了。我想同您说话。

---

===Original===
Footsteps sounded on the stairs﻿—the servants, coming upstairs to bed. No dancing for them. Work, caps and aprons. And those strange rooms upstairs to sleep in that nobody ever saw. Probably Miss Perne went up occasionally to look at them and see that they were all right; clean and tidy.﻿ ﻿… They had to go up every night, carrying little jugs of water and making no noise on the stairs, and come down every morning. They were the servants﻿—and there would never be any dancing. Nobody thought about them.﻿ ﻿… They could not get away from each other, and cook.﻿ ﻿…

To be a general servant would be very hard work. Perhaps impossible. But there would be two rooms, the kitchen at the bottom of the house, and a bedroom at the top, your own. It would not matter what the family was like. You would look after them, like children, and be alone to read and sleep.﻿ ﻿… Toothache. Cheap dentists; a red lamp “painless extractions”﻿ ﻿… having to go there before nine in the morning, and be alone in a cold room, the dentist doing what he thought best and coming back to your work crying with pain, your head wrapped up in a black shawl. Hospitals; being quite helpless and grateful for wrong treatment; coming back to work, ill. Sinks and slops﻿ ﻿… quinsey, all alone﻿ ﻿… growths﻿ ﻿… consumption.

===Chinese===
楼梯上响起脚步声——仆人们，上楼睡觉来了。她们没有舞会。只有干活，帽子，围裙。还有楼上那些睡觉的怪屋子，谁也没见过。大概珀恩小姐偶尔上去看看，瞧着一切都好好的；干净，齐整。……她们夜夜都得端着小水壶上去，楼梯上不敢出一点声，天天早上再下来。她们是仆人——永远不会有舞会。没有人想着她们。……她们谁也躲不开谁，还有厨娘。……

做个打杂的女仆会是苦极了的重活。也许根本扛不下来。可会有两个房间，屋底下的厨房，屋顶尖上的卧室，归你自己。那家人是什么样子倒不要紧。你照管他们，像照管孩子，然后独自一人读书、睡觉。……牙疼。便宜的牙医；一盏红灯，“无痛拔牙”……清早九点前就得赶去，独自坐在冰冷的房间里，牙医爱怎么治怎么治，回去干活时疼得直哭，头上裹着黑披巾。医院；全然无助地受着错误的治疗还要道谢；病着回去干活。水槽，污水……扁桃体脓肿（quinsey），孤零零一个人……肿瘤……痨病。

===Original===
Go to sleep. It would be better to think in the morning. But then this clear first impression would be gone and school would begin and go on from hour to hour through the term, mornings and afternoons and evenings, dragging you along further and further and changing you, months and months and years until it was too late to get back and there was nothing ahead.

The thing to remember, to keep in mind all the time was to save money﻿—not to spend a single penny that could be saved, to be determined about that so that when the temptation came you could just hang on until it was past.

No fun in the holidays, no money spent on flowers and gloves and blouses. Keeping stiff and sensible all the time. The family of the two little Quaker girls had a home library, with lists, an inventory, lending each other their books and talking about them, and albums of pressed leaves and flowers with the Latin names, and went on wearing the same plain clothes.﻿ ﻿… You had to be a certain sort of person to do that.

===Chinese===
睡吧。到早上再想会更好。可到那时这清清楚楚的第一印象就没了，学校就要开学，一个钟头接一个钟头地过完学期，早晨、下午、晚上，把你越拖越远，改变着你，一月又一月，一年又一年，直到想回头也迟了，前头什么也没有。

要记住的、要时时刻刻放在心上的，是攒钱——能省下一个便士也不花，在这上头拿出决断来，这样诱惑来的时候，你只消咬紧牙关撑到它过去。

假期里不作乐，鲜花、手套、新衬衫上一分钱不花。时时刻刻绷着，理智着。那两个贵格会小姑娘的家里有一座家庭藏书室，有书单，有登记册，一家人互相借书、谈书，还有压花压叶的册子，注着拉丁学名，身上年年穿的还是同一身素净衣裳。……得是那么一种人才办得到。

===Original===
It would spoil the holidays to be like that at home. Every penny must be spent, if only on things for other people. Not spending would bring a nice strong secret feeling and a horrid expression into one’s eyes.

The only way was to give up your family and stay at your work, like Flora, and have a box of half-crowns in your drawer.﻿ ﻿… Spend and always be afraid of “rainy days”﻿—or save and never enjoy life at all.

But going out now and again in the holidays, feeling stiff and governessy and just beginning to learn to be oneself again when it was time to go back was not enjoying life﻿ ﻿… your money was spent and people forgot you and you forgot them and went back to your convent to begin again.

Save, save. Sooner or later saving must begin. Why not at once. Harry, it’s no good. I’m old already. I’ve got to be one of those who have to give everything up.

===Chinese===
在家里也这样，假期就毁了。每一个便士都得花掉，哪怕只花在给别人买的东西上。不花钱倒会带来一种怪好的、硬扎扎的隐秘感，只是眼睛里会带上一种讨厌的神情。

唯一的办法是舍下你的家人，守着你的差事，像弗洛拉那样，抽屉里锁一匣半克朗（half-crown）银币。……花掉，就永远怕着“雨天”——攒下，就一辈子也别想痛快。

可假期里偶尔出门，浑身僵直、一副家庭女教师的做派，刚刚开始学着重新做回自己，就到了该回去的时候——这可不叫痛快……钱花掉了，人们忘了你，你也忘了他们，你回到你的修道院从头再来。

攒，攒。早晚总得开始攒。为什么不现在就开始。哈里，没用的。我已经老了。我注定是那种什么都要放弃的人了。

===Original===
I wonder if Flora is asleep?

That’s settled. Go to sleep. Get thee behind me. Sleep﻿ ﻿… the dark cool room. Air; we breathe it in and it keeps us alive. Everybody has air. Manna. As much as you want, full measure, pressed down and running over.﻿ ﻿… Wonderful. There is somebody giving things, whatever goes﻿ ﻿… something left.﻿ ﻿… Somebody seeing that things are not quite unbearable,﻿ ﻿… but the pain, the pain all the time, mysterious black pain.﻿ ﻿…

Into thy hands I commit my spirit. *In manus* something.﻿ ﻿… You understand if nobody else does. But *why* must I be one of the ones to give everything up? *Why* do you make me suffer so?

===Chinese===
不知道弗洛拉睡了没有？

就这么定了。睡吧。退到我后边去吧。睡……又黑又凉的房间。空气；我们把它吸进来，它让我们活着。人人都有空气。吗哪（Manna）。要多少有多少，十足的升斗，压得实实的，满得往外流。……奇妙。有谁在给着东西，不管怎么流走……总剩下一点。……有谁在看着，让事情不至于全然熬不下去，……可是疼，一直在疼，神秘的黑疼。……

我将我的灵魂交在你手里。*In manus*什么的（拉丁语：交在你手里）。……你懂的，就算谁都不懂。可*为什么*我必须是那种什么都要放弃的人？你*为什么*让我受这么大的苦？

## VI / VI

===Original===
Piecemeal statements in her letter home brought Miriam now and again a momentary sense of developing activities, but she did not recognise the completeness of the change in her position at the school until halfway through her second term she found herself talking to the new pupil teacher. She had heard apathetically of her existence during supper-table conversations with the Misses Perne at the beginning of the term. She was an Irish girl of sixteen, one of a large family living on the outskirts of Dublin, and would be a boarder, attending the first class for English and earning pocket money by helping with the lower school. As the weeks went on and Miriam grew accustomed to hearing her name﻿—Julia Doyle﻿—she began to associate it with an idea of charm that brought her a sinking of heart. She knew her position in the esteem of the Pernes was secure. But this new young teacher would work strange miracles with the girls. She would do it quite easily and unconsciously. The girls would be easy with her and would laugh and one would have to hear them.

However, when at last her arrival was near and the three ladies discussed the difficulty of having her met, Miriam plied them until they reluctantly gave her permission to go, taking a workman’s train that would bring her to Euston station at seven o’clock in the morning.

At the end of an hour spent pacing the half-dark platform exhausted with cold and excitement and the monotonously reiterated effort to imagine the arrival of one of Mrs. Hungerford’s heroines from a train journey, Miriam, whose costume had been described in a letter to the girl’s mother, was startled wandering amidst the vociferous passengers at the luggage end of the newly arrived train by a liquid colourless intimate voice at her elbow. “I think I’ll be right to say how d’you do.”

She turned and saw a slender girl in a middle-aged toque and an ill-cut old-fashioned coat and skirt. What were they to say to each other, two dowdy struggling women both in the same box? She must get her to Banbury Park as quickly as possible. It was dreadful that they should be seen together there on the platform in their ragbag clothes. At any rate they must not talk. “Oh, I’m very pleased to see you. I’m glad you’ve come. I suppose the train must have been late,” she said eagerly.

===Chinese===
写回家去的信里零零碎碎的汇报，不时给米丽亚姆带来一阵转瞬即逝的、诸事正在进展的感觉；可直到第二个学期过半，她发觉自己已经在同新来的见习教师说话，才认识到自己在学校里的处境已变得多么彻底。学期初同珀恩姐妹们吃晚饭闲谈时，她漠然地听说过有这么个人。她是个十六岁的爱尔兰姑娘，都柏林郊外一户大家里的一个，将寄宿在校，进一等班学英语，帮着带低年级挣点零花钱。一星期一星期过去，米丽亚姆听惯了她的名字——朱莉娅·多伊尔（Julia Doyle）——便开始把它同一个“魅力”的概念连在一起，那概念让她心口一沉。她知道自己在珀恩姐妹们心中的地位稳当。可这位新来的年轻教师会对姑娘们施出奇怪的魔法。她会做得毫不费力、全然不觉。姑娘们会同她随便亲近，会笑，而人是不得不听见那笑声的。

不过，她到来的日子终于临近，三姐妹正为难于该派谁去接，米丽亚姆缠了她们许久，她们才勉强准了她去，搭一班工人列车，早晨七点把她带到尤斯顿车站（Euston）。

她在半明半暗的站台上踱了一个钟头，冻透了，也兴奋透了，还要一遍又一遍、单调反复地努力想象一位亨格福德太太（Mrs. Hungerford）笔下的女主人公坐火车到来的情形——末了，米丽亚姆——她的装束已在给那姑娘母亲的信里描述过了——正在刚进站的列车行李车厢那头喧腾的旅客中间乱转，冷不防被胳膊肘边一个流水般、无色而亲切的声音吓了一跳：“我想我这会儿说一声‘您好’，总归是对的。”

她转过身，看见一个瘦条条的姑娘，戴一顶中年妇人式的小圆帽，穿一身剪裁不良、样式过时的上衣和裙子。她们俩能对彼此说什么呢，两个寒碜的、挣扎着的女人，拴在同一只匣子里？她必须尽快把她带回班伯里公园。让她们这样一起被人看见，站在站台上，一身破布袋似的衣裳，真是太可怕了。无论如何她们不能说话。“哦，见到你真高兴。你来了我很欢喜。我想火车一定是晚点了。”她热切地说。

===Original===
“Ah, we’ll be late I dare venture. Haven’t an idea of the hour.”

“Oh, yes,” said Miriam emphatically, “I’m sure the train’s *late*.”

“Where’ll we find a core?”

“What?”

“We’ll need a core for the luggage.”

“Oh yes, a cab. We must get a cab. We’d better find a porter.”

“Ah, I’ve a man here seeking out my things.”

===Chinese===
“啊，我看我们八成是迟了。几点钟我一点儿概念也没有。”

“哦，是啊，”米丽亚姆加重语气说，“我敢肯定火车*晚点*了。”

“我们上哪儿找一辆 core（她把 car 念走了样）呢？”

“什么？”

“行李总得有辆 core 才行呀。”

“哦，对，马车。我们得叫辆马车。最好先找个搬运工。”

“啊，我那儿正有个伙计在替我找东西呢。”

===Original===
Inside the cab Julia’s face shone chalky white, and Miriam found that her eyes looked like Weymouth Bay﻿—the sea in general, on days when clouds keep sweeping across the sun. When she laughed she had dimples and the thick white rims of her eyelids looked like piping cord round her eyes. But she was not pretty. There were lines in her cheeks as well as dimples, and there was something apologetic in her little gusty laugh. She laughed a good deal as they started off, saying things, little quiet remarks that Miriam could not understand and that did not seem to be answers to her efforts to make conversation. Perhaps she was not going the right way to make her talk. Perhaps she had not said any of the things she thought she had said.

She cleared her throat and looked out of the window thinking over a possible opening.

“I’ve never been so glad over anything in my life as hearing you’re one of the teachers,” said Julia presently.

“The Pernes call me by my name, so I suppose you will too as you’re a teacher,” said Miriam headlong.

“That’s awfully sweet of you,” replied Julia laughing and blushing a clear deep rose. “It makes anyone feel at home. I’ll be looking out till I hear it.”
